Differential Galois groups, specializations and Matzat’s conjecture
Ruyong Feng, Michael Wibmer

Abstract
We study families of linear differential equations parametrized by an algebraic variety 𝒳 \mathcal{X} and show that the set of all points x ∈ 𝒳 x\in\mathcal{X} , such that the differential Galois group at the generic fibre specializes to the differential Galois group at the fibre over x x , is Zariski dense in 𝒳 \mathcal{X} . As an application, we prove Matzat’s conjecture in full generality: The absolute differential Galois group of a one-variable function field over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero is a free proalgebraic group. † † footnotetext: Mathematics Subject Classification Codes: 14L15, 34M50, 12H05. Key words and phrases : Differential Galois theory, Matzat’s conjecture, specializations of linear differential equations, Néron’s specialization theorem



1
Introduction

The absolute differential Galois group of a differential field
F
governs the algebraic properties of solutions of linear differential equations over
F
. Determining the structure of this group for interesting differential fields is a central problem in differential Galois theory (see e.g.,
[
vdPS03
, Section 10]
). Matzat’s conjecture addresses the case of one-variable function fields.

Matzat’s Conjecture
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and let
F
be a one-variable function field over
k
, equipped with a non-trivial
k
-derivation. Then the absolute differential Galois group of
F
is the free proalgebraic group on a set of cardinality
|F|
.

The main goal of this article is to prove Matzat’s conjecture (Theorem
5.15
) in the form stated above. Several special cases of Matzat’s conjecture are already known: The first case, when the differential field
F
equals
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
, with
k
countable and of infinite transcendence degree (over
\mathbb{Q}
) was established in
[
BHHW21b
]
. The case when
F
is
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
, with
k
of infinite transcendence degree was treated in
[
BHHW21a
]
. The case when
k
has infinite transcendence degree was established in
[
Wib23
]
.
Moreover, in
[
Wib23
]
it was shown that for an arbitrary algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero, Matzat’s conjecture for
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
implies Matzat’s conjecture for one-variable function fields over
k
. On the other hand, thanks to the work in
[
Wib20
]
and
[
BHHW21b
]
, it is known that Matzat’s conjecture holds for
F
countable if and only if every differential embedding problem of finite type over
F
is solvable. Thus, to prove Matzat’s conjecture in full generality, it suffices to prove the following:

Theorem A

(Theorem
5.14
)
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Then every differential embedding problem of finite type over
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
is solvable.

Theorem A was proved in
[
BHHP21
]
under the assumption that
k
has infinite transcendence degree. To remove this unnecessary assumption, we establish a specialization result for Picard-Vessiot rings and differential Galois groups that we deem of independent interest.
The most conceptual and useful formulation of our specialization result is in terms of differential torsors (Theorem
4.26
). For clarity, we state here a simpler, more concrete, version of our specialization result.

Let
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
be an inclusion of algebraically closed fields of characteristic zero. A Picard-Vessiot ring
R/k^{\prime}(x)
(the differential analog of the splitting field of a polynomial) for a linear differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
with
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
, is as a quotient
R=k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathfrak{m}
, where
X
is an
n\times n
matrix of indeterminates,
\delta(X)=AX
and
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal differential ideal of
k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

Assume the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
is generated by
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
. Choose a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
and a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
A\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{n\times n}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
. For
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
, a specialization
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{B},k)
extends to a map
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
by
c(x)=x
. In particular, applying
c
to the coefficients of
A
yields a matrix
A^{c}\in k(x)^{n\times n}
and applying
c
to the coefficients of a
p_{i}
yields a
p_{i}^{c}\in k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

Theorem B

(Corollary
5.1
)
.

The set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{c}=(p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c})
is a maximal differential ideal of
k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
, i.e.,
R^{c}=k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathfrak{m}^{c}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
, is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

So, roughly speaking, Theorem B asserts that, for many specializations, the algebraic relations among the solutions of the specialized equation
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
are exactly the specializations of the algebraic relations among the solutions of the generic equation
\delta(y)=Ay
. We note that already the existence of a single “good” specialization in Theorem B is a nontrivial problem.

The behavior of differential Galois groups and Picard-Vessiot rings under specialization has been studied by various authors in various different settings (
[
Gol57
]
,
[
Kat90
, Section 2.4]
,
[
Sin93
]
,
[
Hru02
, Section V]
,
[
And04
]

[
BvdP06
]
,
[
dS09
]
,

[
Dav16
, Section 1.6]
,
[
Sei22
]
,
[
RS23
]
). Unfortunately, these results are not general or precise enough to be applicable to a proof of Matzat’s conjecture.

It is well-known that the set of good specializations in Theorem B may not contain a Zariski open subset, in particular it is in general not Zariski constructible. The easiest example illustrating this point is the differential equation
\delta(y)=\frac{\alpha}{x}y
with solution
x^{\alpha}
, which is transcendental for
\alpha\in k\smallsetminus\mathbb{Q}
but algebraic for
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
.

As in this example, despite the lack of generic behavior with respect to the Zariski topology, the set of good specializations is expected to be “large” and not merely Zariski-dense. To confirm this expectation, we use, following
[
Hru02
]
, commutative group schemes of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
to define “open” subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.
An ad-open subset (the ad refers to the additive group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
) of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is of the form
\{c\in\mathcal{X}(k)|\ c\text{ is injective on }\Gamma\}
, where
\Gamma
is a finitely generated subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B})=(\mathcal{B},+)
and
\mathcal{X}(k)
is identified with
\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{B},k)
. For example,
k
minus a finitely generated
\mathbb{Q}
-
subspace of
k
, is an ad-open subset of
k=\mathbb{A}^{1}(k)
. Note that in case
\Gamma
is generated by a single element
h
, the corresponding ad-open subset is the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
where
h
does not vanish. We can thus think of ad-open subsets as a generalization of Zariski open subsets. Ad
\times
Jac-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
are defined roughly similar but with the additive group replaced by a product of the additive group with a Jacobian variety. (For a precise formulation see Definition
2.6
below.)
For example,

\{\alpha\in k|\ 256\alpha^{3}-27\neq 0\text{ and }(0,1)\text{ is not torsion on the elliptic curve }y^{2}=x^{3}-4\alpha x+1\}

(1)

is an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
k=\mathbb{A}^{1}(k)
.

A more detailed version of Theorem B, confirming the expectation that the set of good specializations is large, states that the set of good specializations contains an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
(Theorem
4.26
). Moreover, if the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) is connected, then the Jacobian variety is not needed, i.e., the set of good specializations contains an ad-open subset (Corollary
4.27
).

We note that a result similar to Theorem B but for finite difference equations, i.e., for the operator
k(x)\to k(x),\ h(x)\mapsto h(x+1)
instead of the derivation
\frac{d}{dx}
, was established in
[
Fen21
]
. There Jacobian varieties are not needed, essentially because
k(x)
does not have nontrivial finite difference field extensions.
(In our context, the Jacobian varieties arise via Jacobians of curves associated to finite field extensions of
k(x)
, which are of course differential fields.) However, in
[
Fen21
]
one also requires subsets that are “open” with respect to the multiplicative group. These occur in the step when one has to determine the multiplicative independence of rational functions. In our context, this step is replaced by determining the logarithmic independence of algebraic functions which leads to ad
\times
Jac
-
open subsets.

Our proof of Theorem B owes a lot to
[
Hru02
]
. In particular, it relies on the ideas underlying Hrushovski’s algorithm for computing the differential Galois group of a linear differential equation.
Compared to the specialization results in
[
Hru02
, Section V]
, our result is more general and more precise: In
[
Hru02
]
open subsets with respect to arbitrary commutative algebraic groups are required, whereas for us, the algebraic group can be chosen to be a direct product of the additive group with a Jacobian variety. If the generic differential Galois group is connected, even the additive group alone is sufficient for us. Moreover, many of the statements in
[
Hru02
, Section V.A]
are restricted to the case that
\mathcal{B}
has Krull dimension one and
k=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Historically, a prime force for the development of differential algebra and differential Galois theory, was the desire to understand when an indefinite integral or differential equation can be solved “by quadratures”. Of course, making the notion of “solving” rigorous was part of the problem.
As explained in the beautiful survey
[
Zan14a
]
, when studied in families, this somewhat old-fashioned topic of solving, is closely connected to some modern problems in the realm of unlikely intersections.

Given an indefinite integral of an algebraic function depending on a parameter, or a linear differential equation depending on a parameter, an important question (asked in
[
Zan14a
, p. 550]
) is the following:

For which values of the parameter can the solutions be expressed within some
prescribed class, assuming this can’t be done for the generic solution?

The expectation is that this set of “exceptional” parameter values is small (albeit not necessarily finite). For example, in
[
MZ20
]
, it was shown that for a parametric family of differentials on an algebraic curve, which cannot be integrated in elementary terms at the generic fibre, the set of specializations such that the specialized differential can be integrated in elementary terms is small, in fact, finite in many circumstances.

Based on our specialization result (Theorem
4.26
) we can answer the above question for classes of functions that are amenable to differential Galois theory in the sense that solvability of a linear differential equation in the class of functions can be characterized through a property of the differential Galois group. For example, for the widely used class of Liouvillian functions we obtain:

Corollary B1

(Corollary
5.6
)
.

Assume that the differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) does not have a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions, then the set of specializations
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the differential equation
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
(over
k(x)
) has a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions, is contained in an ad
\times
Jac-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Moreover, if the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) is connected, then already an ad-closed subset suffices. Here a subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is called ad
\times
Jac-closed (or ad-closed) if its complement is ad
\times
Jac-open (or ad-open). For example, a finitely generated
\mathbb{Q}
-subspace of
k
is an ad-closed subset of
k=\mathbb{A}^{1}(k)
.

As an illustration of Corollary B1, consider Bessel’s differential equation

\delta^{2}(y)+\tfrac{1}{x}\delta(y)+(1-(\tfrac{\alpha}{x})^{2})y=0.

It has a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions if and only if
\alpha\notin\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
. The exceptional set
\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z}
is contained in
\mathbb{Q}
, an ad-closed subset of
k
. As predicted by the general theory, in this case, the Jacobian variety is not needed because the differential Galois group at the generic fibre (i.e., the differential Galois group of Bessel’s equation over
k^{\prime}(x)
, where
k^{\prime}
is the algebraic closure of the rational function field
k(\alpha)
) is
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
, which is connected.

If we choose as class of functions, the class of all algebraic functions, we obtain an equicharacteristic zero version of the Grothendieck-Katz conjecture (cf.
[
Hru02
, Cor. to Prop. 5.1]
and
[
And04
, Prop. 7.1.1]
):

Corollary B2

(Corollary
5.8
)
.

Assume that the differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) does not have a basis of solutions consisting of algebraic functions, then the set of all specializations
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the differential equation
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
(over
k(x)
) has a basis of solutions consisting of algebraic functions, is contained in an ad
\times
Jac-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Again, if the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) is connected, then already an ad-closed subset suffices.
As the intersection of two ad
\times
Jac-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is nonempty (Remark
2.25
), Corollary B2 can also be reformulated in the maybe more familiar spirit of a local to global principle: If the set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
has a basis of solutions consisting of algebraic functions, contains an ad
\times
Jac-open subset (e.g., a nonempty Zariski open subset), then
\delta(y)=Ay
has a basis of solutions consisting of algebraic functions.

Theorem B (and its more detailed variant Theorem
4.26
) can be used to transfer results from a single algebraically closed field of constants (e.g., the field
\mathbb{C}
of complex numbers) to an arbitrary algebraically closed field of constants. Our prime illustration of this fact is of course our proof of Theorem A. However, we also show how one can deduce a new and very short proof of the
solution of the inverse problem
in differential Galois theory from Theorem
4.26
. Recall that the solution of the inverse problem states that every linear algebraic group over
k
is a differential Galois group over
k(x)
. Using analytic methods, namely Plemelj’s (weak) solution of the Riemann-Hilbert problem, this was proved for
k=\mathbb{C}
already in 1979 (
[
TT79
]
). However, it took more than 25 years and contributions of many authors to finally solve the inverse problem for an arbitrary algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero (
[
Har05
]
).
Using Theorem
4.26
, we can deduce the solution of the inverse problem from the solution over
\mathbb{C}
rather directly. To the best of our knowledge, so far only two types of proofs of the solution of the inverse problem were known. Firstly, the proof from
[
Har05
]
, secondly proofs that rely on patching. Of course, also Matzat’s conjecture and Theorem A imply the solution of the inverse problem. However, these results ultimately rely on patching.

We conclude the introduction with a more detailed outline of the paper.
The larger part of the article (Sections
3
and
4
) is concerned with the proof of the specialization theorem, which states that the set of “good” specializations contains an ad
\times
Jac-open subset. This result would be vacuous if we do not know that ad
\times
Jac-open subsets are nonempty. While the Zariski denseness of ad-open subsets is readily available in the literature, the Zariski denseness of ad
\times
Jac-open subsets does not seem to be available in the generality required for the proof of Matzat’s conjecture. In Section
2
we therefore provide a proof of the Zariski denseness of ad
\times
Jac-open subsets based on a variant of Néron’s specialization theorem and a geometric version of abstract Hilbert sets.

In Section
3
we collect various constructions and results from differential Galois theory that are required for the proof of the specialization theorem, including the concept of differential torsors. Differential torsors provide a convenient framework to simultaneously study Picard-Vessiot rings and differential Galois groups under specialization in a compatible fashion. This compatibility is vital for the proof of Theorem A, where, in effect, we consider differential embedding problems under specialization. Roughly, a differential torsor is obtained by spreading out a Picard-Vessiot ring
R
for
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) with the action of the differential Galois group
G
, into a nice family
\mathcal{R}
with an action of an affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
. Our main specialization result, proved at the end of Section
4
, then states that there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of the parameter space
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{R}_{c}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring with differential Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{c}
for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

In the earlier parts of Section
4
the various constructions and criteria from Section
3
are shown to be well-behaved under specialization. Roughly, the proof of the main specialization theorem relies on two intermediate specialization results corresponding to the two main steps of Hrushovski’s algorithm. In the first main step of Hrushovski’s algorithm one computes the
k^{\prime}(x)
-algebraic relations among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix for
\delta(y)=Ay
,
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
up to a fixed predetermined degree
d=d(n)
. The corresponding specialization result (Theorem
4.24
) is that, if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
is a basis of the
k^{\prime}(x)
-vector space of all algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a generic solution matrix, then there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c}
is a
k(x)
-basis of the vector space of all
k(x)
-algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix for the specialized equation
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Roughly, the first step in Hrushovski’s algorithm allows one to compute the differential Galois group up to a torus (and a finite group, which is however irrelevant for our purpose). In the second main step of Hrushovski’s algorithm (“the toric part”) the central question is to decide the logarithmic independence of algebraic functions. Here, elements
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
belonging to a finite field extension
F
of
k^{\prime}(x)
are called
logarithmically independent
over
F
if a relation of the form

d_{1}f_{1}+\ldots+d_{m}f_{m}=\tfrac{\delta(f)}{f}\quad\text{ with }d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }f\in F^{\times}

implies
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}=0
.

For
F=k^{\prime}(x)
this question can be settled as follows. An element of
k^{\prime}(x)
is a logarithmic derivative of an element of
k^{\prime}(x)
if and only if all its poles are simple with integer residues. Thus an inspection of the poles and residues of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
will yield all possible values of
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}
. For
F/k^{\prime}(x)
finite, there exists a similar criterion, however, in this case, it involves the degree zero divisor class group of
F/k^{\prime}
. This is exactly how the Jacobian varieties enter into the picture. The degree zero divisor class group of
F/k^{\prime}
can be identified with the group
J(k^{\prime})
, where
J
is the Jacobian of a smooth projective curve with function field
F/k^{\prime}
.
The criterion for the logarithmic independence, thus relates the computation of the differential Galois group to the question if certain elements
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell}\in J(K)
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent. For example, if
a=\frac{x^{2}}{x^{4}+x+\alpha}
, the differential Galois group
G_{\alpha}
of

\delta^{2}(y)-\big(2a+\tfrac{\delta(a)}{2a}\big)\delta(y)+\big(a^{2}-a-\tfrac{\delta(a)}{2}\big)y=0

is a subgroup of the group
G
of
2\times 2
monomial matrices. A sufficient criterion for
G_{\alpha}=G
, derived from the criterion for logarithmic independence, is that
the point
(0,1)
is not a torsion point on the elliptic curve
y^{2}=x^{3}-4\alpha x+1
. This leads to the ad
\times
Jac-open subset in (
1
).
The specialization result corresponding to the second step of Hrushovski’s algorithm (Theorem
4.10
) states that logarithmic independence of algebraic functions is preserved on an ad
\times
Jac-open subset.

Finally, in Section
5
, with the specialization theorem in hand, we prove all the results outlined in the introduction above, including Matzat’s conjecture.

We are grateful to Sebastian Petersen for helpful comments on Néron’s specialization theorem. We are also thankful to the anonymous referee for helpful suggestions.

Notation and conventions:
All rings are assumed to be commutative and unital. All fields are assumed to be of characteristic zero.
The group of multiplicative units of a ring
\mathcal{B}
is denoted by
\mathcal{B}^{\times}
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is a scheme over
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a morphism of rings, then
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
denotes the scheme over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
obtained by base change via
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. A similar notation applies to morphisms of schemes.
For an affine scheme
\mathcal{X}
over
\mathcal{B}
we write
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{X}]
for the ring of global sections of
\mathcal{X}
so that
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{X}])
. We sometimes abbreviate
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}]
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[\mathcal{X}]
.

For a ring
\mathcal{B}
, the polynomial ring
\mathcal{B}[x]
over
\mathcal{B}
in the variable
x
is always considered as a differential ring with respect to the derivation
\frac{d}{dx}
. Similarly, the field of rational functions in
x
(over some field of constants) is always considered as a differential field with respect to
\frac{d}{dx}
.
We use
R^{\delta}=\{r\in R|\ \delta(r)=0\}
to denote the constants of a differential ring
(R,\delta)
.
With
X=(X_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
we always denote an
n\times n
matrix of indeterminates (over practically any ring that is around). It is the matrix of coordinate functions on
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
.

For an algebra
\mathcal{B}
over a field
k
and a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathcal{B}
we denote the residue field at
\mathfrak{p}
with
k(\mathfrak{p})
. If
\mathcal{B}
is an integral domain, we write
k(\mathcal{B})
for the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
. If
\mathcal{X}
is an integral scheme over
k
, we write
k(\mathcal{X})
for the function field of
\mathcal{X}
. With
\overline{K}
we denote the algebraic closure of a field
K
.
As a general rule, we use calligraphic letters (like
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{E},\mathcal{X}
,
\ldots)
when thinking about objects that vary in a family.

Throughout this article
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.
By “algebraic group over
k
” we mean a (not necessarily linear) group scheme of finite type over
k
.
Algebraic groups, or more generally group schemes, are often identified with their functor of points.
By a “closed subgroup” of a group scheme we mean a closed subgroup scheme. A “variety” is a geometrically integral separated scheme of finite type over a field.

2
Ad
\times
Jac-open sets

As explained in the introduction, our main goal is to show that, for a linear differential equation depending on parameters, the set of parameter values under which the algebraic properties of the solutions are preserved is “large”. In this section, we make precise the meaning of “large” by introducing ad
\times
Jac-open sets following
[
Hru02
]
and
[
Fen21
]
.
Our main specialization theorem (Theorem
4.26
) states that the set of good specializations contains an ad
\times
Jac-open subset. This result would of course be useless if it is not known that an ad
\times
Jac-open subset is nonempty.

The main result of this section is that an ad
\times
Jac-open subset is not only nonempty but in fact Zariski dense (Theorem
2.24
). It was already shown in
[
Fen21
]
that the intersection of an ad-open set with an open set with respect to the multiplicative group is Zariski dense.
So most of our work here is focused on the case of abelian varieties. For related but weaker results see
[
Hru02
, Section V.A]
.

Throughout Section
2
we assume that

•

k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero;

•

\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-
algebra that is an integral domain and

•

\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
.

2.1
Open sets defined by commutative group schemes

Let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a commutative group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
. For every
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
we have a specialization morphism

\sigma_{c}\colon{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B})\to{\mathcal{E}}(k),

given by applying
{\mathcal{E}}
, considered as a functor on the category of
\mathcal{B}
-algebras, to the morphism
c\colon\mathcal{B}\to k
. For a finitely generated subgroup
\Gamma
of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B})
we set

W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma)=\{c\in\mathcal{X}(k)|\ \sigma_{c}\text{ is injective on }\Gamma\}.

The subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
of the form
W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma)
satisfy the axioms for a basis of a topology:
\mathcal{X}(k)=W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}},1)
and

W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}}_{2},\Gamma_{2})=W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{2},\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}).

(2)

This statement remains true if we restrict the
{\mathcal{E}}
’s to belong to a class of group schemes that is closed under taking products, such as the class of all abelian schemes.

We can thus consider the topology of
\mathcal{X}(k)
generated by such a basis. The point of this construction is that, while families of linear differential equations parametrized by
\mathcal{X}
do not exhibit generic behaviour with respect to the Zariski topology, they may exhibit generic behaviour with respect to such a finer topology. If we allow more
{\mathcal{E}}
’s in our class, the corresponding topology gets finer and so the genericity statement we hope to prove gets weaker. It is therefore vital to restrict the shape of the allowed
{\mathcal{E}}
’s as much as possible. In this context, it is then irrelevant whether or not the sets of the form
W_{\mathcal{X}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma)
with
{\mathcal{E}}
an allowed group scheme still form a basis of a topology. We note however, that already a genericity result with respect to the discrete topology is not easily obtained, i.e., in the context of Theorem B from the introduction, it is nontrivial to prove the existence of a single good specialization.
In fact, we are not aware of a proof of the existence of a single good specialization that does not go through Theorem
2.24
.

We next discuss the shape that we will allow for the possible
{\mathcal{E}}
’s.

Definition 2.1
.

A subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is
ad-open
if it is of the form
\mathcal{U}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
, for some finitely generated subgroup
\Gamma
of
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B})=(\mathcal{B},+)
.

In other words, a subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is ad-open if it is open with respect to the additive group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{a}
over
\mathcal{B}
.
As we are in characteristic zero, the group
\Gamma\leq(\mathcal{B},+)
is torsion free and thus a finite free
\mathbb{Z}
-module. If follows that the ad-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
consist of exactly those specializations that preserve the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear independence of a finite family of elements of
\mathcal{B}
.

Note that if
\Gamma
is generated by a single nonzero element
b\in\mathcal{B}
, then

W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)=\{c\in\mathcal{X}(k)|\ c(b)\neq 0\}

is the basic Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
where
b
does not vanish. In this sense, the “topology” generated by the ad-open sets is finer than the Zariski topology.

Example 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}=k[\alpha]
be a univariate polynomial ring over
k
so that
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and
\mathcal{X}(k)=k
. For
\mathbb{Q}
-linearly independent elements
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in k
, let
\Gamma
be the subgroup of
(\mathcal{B},+)
generated by
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
and
\alpha
. Then
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)=k\smallsetminus V
consists of all elements of
k
that are not contained in the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
V
generated by
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
.

Remark 2.3
.

If
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
are finitely generated subgroups of
(\mathcal{B},+)
and
\Gamma
is the subgroup generated by
\Gamma_{1},\ldots,\Gamma_{n}
, then
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\subseteq W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap\ldots\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{n})
. Thus a finite intersection of ad-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
contains an ad-open subset.

For the specialization arguments to be carried out in Section
4
, it is often important to be able to enlarge the
k
-
algebra
\mathcal{B}
by adjoining finitely many elements from
\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}
, because certain properties of the differential equation at the generic fibre may only manifest themselves over the algebraic closure
\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}
of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
. We therefore also need to consider subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
that are the form
\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma))
, where
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is finitely generated over
\mathcal{B}
,
{\mathcal{E}}
is a commutative group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma
is a finitely generated subgroup of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
and
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

We call an inclusion of integral domains
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}

algebraic
if the field extension
k(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})/k(\mathcal{B})
is algebraic.

The following lemma is a slight variation of

[
Hru02
, Lemma 5A.1]
. It shows that for ad-open subsets it is not necessary to consider these more general subsets.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be an inclusion of integral domains such that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
. Set
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
and let
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
be the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is an ad-open subset
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
, then there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
and
\phi^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma^{\prime})
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},+)
is generated by
\gamma^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\gamma^{\prime}_{n}
. Let
M
be the Galois closure of
k(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})/k(\mathcal{B})
and let
T,y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
be indeterminates. Set

p(T,y_{1},\dots,y_{n})=\prod_{g\in\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))}\left(T-\sum_{i=1}^{n}g(\gamma^{\prime}_{i})y_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{|\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))|}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}b_{i,j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i,j}\right)T^{i},

where
{\mathbf{m}}_{i,1},\dots,{\mathbf{m}}_{i,\ell_{i}}
are all monomials in
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
of degree
|\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))|-i
.
Since
g(p)=p
for every
g\in\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))
, all
b_{i,j}
are in
k(\mathcal{B})
. Write
b_{i,j}=\gamma_{i,j}/a
where
\gamma_{i,j},a\in\mathcal{B}
and
a\neq 0
. Let
b
be a nonzero element of
\mathcal{B}
such that any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
with
c(b)\neq 0
can be lifted to a
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
. (Such a
b
exists by Chevalley’s theorem.) Set
\mathcal{U}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma
is the subgroup of
\mathcal{B}
generated by
b
and all
\gamma_{i,j}
’s.

Assume that
c\in\mathcal{U}
,
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
and
\phi(c^{\prime})=c
. We claim that
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. For a contradiction, suppose
c^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Then there exists a
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}
such that
c^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime})=0
. Write
\gamma^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
with
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}\neq 0
,

ap(0,d_{1},\dots,d_{n})=a(-1)^{|\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))|}\prod_{g\in\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))}g(\gamma^{\prime})\neq 0.

Furthermore,
ap(\gamma^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{n})=0
and

ap(0,d_{1},\dots,d_{n})=a\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}}b_{0,j}{\mathbf{m}}_{0,j}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}}\gamma_{0,j}{\mathbf{m}}_{0,j}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in\Gamma.

On the other hand, since
c^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime})=0
, one has that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\left(ap(\gamma^{\prime},d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{|\operatorname{Gal}(M/k(\mathcal{B}))|}c^{\prime}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\gamma_{i,j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i,j}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\right)c^{\prime}(\gamma^{\prime})^{i}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{0}}\gamma_{0,j}{\mathbf{m}}_{0,j}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\right)=c^{\prime}(ap(0,d_{1},\dots,d_{n}))=c(ap(0,d_{1},\dots,d_{n})).

This implies that
c
is not injective on
\Gamma
, because
0\neq ap(0,d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in\Gamma
, a contradiction. Therefore
\phi^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

To see that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
, it suffices to note that every
c\in\mathcal{U}
lifts to a
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
because
c(b)\neq 0
as
b\in\Gamma
.
∎

For group schemes, other than the additive group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, we do not have a statement analogous to Lemma
2.4
at hand. Therefore, in this case, we need to consider algebraic extensions
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathcal{B}
.

For simplicity, we call a group scheme
{\mathcal{E}}
over some integral domain
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of
abelian type
(or of
Jacobian type
) if it is commutative, separated and of finite type over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that the generic fibre
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})}\operatorname{Spec}(K_{0})
is an abelian variety (or a Jacobian variety) over
K_{0}
, where
K_{0}
is the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Consider the following three kinds of subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
:

(i)

subsets of the form
\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}\times{\mathcal{J}},\Gamma))
with
{\mathcal{J}}
of Jacobian type;

(ii)

subsets of the form
\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))
with
{\mathcal{J}}
of Jacobian type;

(iii)

subsets of the form
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))
with
{\mathcal{J}}
of Jacobian type.

Then a subset of one kind contains a subset of any other kind. Here, in all of the above cases,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, with
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
and
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

As
W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2})=W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}\times{\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2})
, a set of the second kind is in fact a set of the first kind. Conversely, given
\Gamma\leq\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\times{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
let
\Gamma_{1}\leq\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
denote the image of
\Gamma
under
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\times{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
and
\Gamma_{2}\leq{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
the image of
\Gamma
under
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\times{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{J}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. Then
\Gamma\leq\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2}
and so

W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2})=W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}\times{\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{1}\times\Gamma_{2})\subseteq W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a}\times{\mathcal{J}},\Gamma).

Thus a subset of the first kind contains a subset of the second kind.

If
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))
is a subset of the third kind, we may consider
\Gamma_{1}
as a subgroup of
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime},+)
and then
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))=\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))
. Thus a subset of the third kind is in fact a subset of the second kind.

Finally, given a subset
\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))
of the second kind, there exists, by Lemma
2.4
, a finitely generated subgroup
\Gamma_{0}
of
(\mathcal{B},+)
such that
W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{0})\subseteq\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1}))
and
\phi^{-1}(W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{0}))\subseteq(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})
. We then have

W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{0})\cap\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2}))\subseteq\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma_{1})\cap W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{J}},\Gamma_{2})).

So a subset of the second kind contains a subset of the third kind.
∎

We now define the “open” subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
that are the most relevant for us.

Definition 2.6
.

A subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is
ab-open
(or
Jac-open
) if
it is of the form
\mathcal{U}=\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma))
, where

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
containing
\mathcal{B}
an integral domain finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
,
{\mathcal{E}}
is a group scheme over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
of abelian (or of Jacobian) type,
\Gamma
is a finitely generated subgroup of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
and
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

A subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is
ad
\times
Jac-open
if it is the intersection of an ad-open subset with a Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

A subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is
ad
\times
Jac-closed
(or
ad-closed
) if its complement is ad
\times
Jac-open (or
ad-open
).

In particular, a Jac-open subset is ab-open. We have used the third kind of subsets from Lemma
2.5
to define ad
\times
Jac-open subset. The lemma shows that we could have, more or less equivalently, also used the first or second kind of subsets in the definition.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
. Let
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
denote the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and let
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
be a subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
that is either ad-open, ab-open, Jac-open or ad
\times
Jac
-
open, then
\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
contains a subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
that is of the same type as
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The case of ad-open subsets follows from Lemma
2.4
. For the case of ab-open and Jac-open subsets it suffices to note that if
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
are such that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
. The case of ad
\times
Jac-open subsets is similar but also uses Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.2
Hilbert sets

To show that ad
\times
Jac-open subsets are Zarisiki dense we will use Hilbert sets. We therefore, in this section, recall their definition and discuss the properties that are relevant for our proof.

Throughout Section
2.2
let
k_{0}
be a field of characteristic zero and let
\mathcal{B}_{0}
be a finitely generated
k_{0}
-algebra that is an integral domain.

We begin by recalling the abstract definition of
Hilbert sets
from
[
Lan83
, Chapter 9, Section 5]
. This is closely related to the notion of
thin sets
(see e.g.,
[
FJ08
, Section 13.5]
) but restricted to the affine setting.

For an inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
of integral domains, an element
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is called
algebraic
over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
if
b^{\prime}
(considered as an element of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
) is algebraic over the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, i.e.,
b^{\prime}
satisfies a nonzero (not necessarily monic) univariate polynomial over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is
algebraic
over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
if every element of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
, i.e., the field extension
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})/k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
of the corresponding fields of fractions is algebraic.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
be an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra. The corresponding fields of fractions then form a finite field extension
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})/k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
.

Let
H(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
denote the subset of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
consisting of all
\mathfrak{p}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
such that there exists a unique
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
and for this
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
one has
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
.
A subset of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
of the form
H(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
is called a
basic Hilbert set
.

Recall that a basic Zariski open subset of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
has the form
D(b)=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})|\ b\notin\mathfrak{p}\}
for some nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
.
A
Hilbert subset
of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
is a finite intersection of basic Hilbert sets with a basic Zariski open set.

Example 2.8
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
be a univariate polynomial of positive degree such that
(p)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
is a prime ideal. Set
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]/(p)
.
Then
H(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
consists of all
\mathfrak{p}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
such that the leading coefficient of
p
does not lie in
\mathfrak{p}
and the image
\overline{p}
of
p
in
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})[y]
is an irreducible polynomial. To see this, note that the set of primes of
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
can be identified with the spectrum of
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})=k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})[y]/(\overline{p})
.

For
k_{0}
algebraically closed, the above notion of Hilbert subset is not really useful: If
\mathfrak{p}
is a closed point of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
(i.e., a maximal ideal) then
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})=k_{0}
has no finite extensions. Thus, if
\mathfrak{p}
belongs to some basic Hilbert set
H(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
, we must have
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]=1
. So
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})=k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is contained in a localization of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

The following definition introduces a notion of Hilbert set that is useful when working over an algebraically closed field. Recall that (throughout Section
2
)
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero,
\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra that is an integral domain and
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
.

Definition 2.9
.

A subset
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is a
geometric Hilbert set
if there exists a subfield
k_{0}
of
k
finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
, a finitely generated
k_{0}
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of
\mathcal{B}
such that the canonical map
\mathcal{B}_{0}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}
is an isomorphism and a Hilbert subset
H
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
such that
\mathcal{H}
is the inverse image of
H
under
\mathcal{X}(k)\to\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
In case we need to be more specific, we call such an
\mathcal{H}
a geometric
\mathcal{B}_{0}/k_{0}
-Hilbert set
.

We will see below that geometric Hilbert sets are always Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
. The following two lemmas will be needed to establish further good behavior of (geometric) Hilbert sets.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
be an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra. Then there exists a nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that for every
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)

•

there exists a
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
;

•

the fibre
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
is a
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
-vector space of dimension
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
;

•

for every prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
one has
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\leq[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is finitely generated algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, there exists a nonzero
b_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})_{b_{1}}
is integral over
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b_{1}}
. Since
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})_{b_{1}}
is finitely generated over
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b_{1}}
, in fact
(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})_{b_{1}}
is finite over
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b_{1}}
. By generic freeness (see e.g.,
[
Sta24
,
Tag 051S
]
) there exists a nonzero
b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b_{1}b_{2}}
is not only finite over
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b_{1}b_{2}}
but even free. The rank
d
of
(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b_{1}b_{2}}
as a
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b_{1}b_{2}}
-module is then necessarily equal to
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
.

We claim that
b=b_{1}b_{2}
has the required property.
First note that because
(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b}
is integral over
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}
, for every
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)
there exists a
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
.

Regarding the second point, note that for
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)
the fibre

\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})=(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b}\otimes_{(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})

is a
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
-vector space of dimension
d
. Thirdly, a prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
corresponds to a prime
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}))
. The residue field of
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
can be identified with the residue field of
\tilde{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
, which is an extension of degree at most
d
of
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
be an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is algebraic and finitely generated over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Let
\pi\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
denote the morphism corresponding to the inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
and let
H^{\prime}\subseteq\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
be a Hilbert set. Then there exists a Hilbert set
H
in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
satisfying the following two conditions:

•

H\subseteq\pi(H^{\prime})
and

•

for every
\mathfrak{p}\in H
there exists a unique
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
. (Of course then
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
.)

Proof.

Write
H^{\prime}=H(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})\cap\ldots\cap H(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}_{n}/\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})\cap D(b^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
2.10
there exists a nonzero
b^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
such that, for
i=1,\ldots,n
, every prime in
D(b^{\prime}_{1})
lifts to a prime in
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
and for every prime
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in D(b_{1}^{\prime})
one has
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})]\leq[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})]
. By Chevalley’s theorem there exists a nonzero
b_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
D(b_{1})\subseteq\pi(D(b^{\prime}_{1}b^{\prime}))
.

By Lemma
2.10
again, there exists a nonzero
b_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such
that for every
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b_{2})
the
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
-vector space
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
has dimension
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
and for every prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
one has
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\leq[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
.

We claim that
H=H(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}_{1}/\mathcal{B}_{0})\cap\ldots\cap H(\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}_{n}/\mathcal{B}_{0})\cap D(b_{1}b_{2})
has the required property. Let
\mathfrak{p}\in H
. As
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b_{1})\subseteq\pi(D(b_{1}^{\prime}b^{\prime}))
, there exists a prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in D(b_{1}^{\prime}b^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b_{2})
we have
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]\leq[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
.

Because
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in D(b_{1}^{\prime})
there exists, for every
i=1,\ldots,n
, a prime
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
and satisfying
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})]\leq[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})]
. As
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\operatorname{Spec}(B_{i}^{\prime\prime})
is a prime above
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{p}\in H(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
, we see that
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is the unique prime of
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
above
\mathfrak{p}
and that
[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
. Therefore

\displaystyle[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]

\displaystyle=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})]\cdot[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]\geq[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})]\cdot[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]=

\displaystyle=[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]

and it follows that

[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})]=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]\quad\text{ and }\quad[k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})]=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})].

(3)

We next show that
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
. As
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in D(b^{\prime})
we only have to show that
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in H(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. If there was a prime of
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
other than
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
lying above
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
, this would also be a prime other than
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
which is impossible. Thus
\mathfrak{p}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is the unique prime of
\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
lying above
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
. By (
3
) we see that
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in H(\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. So
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\in H^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{p}\in\pi(H^{\prime})
, i.e.,
H\subseteq\pi(H^{\prime})
.

Regarding the second item, note that by the choice of
b_{2}
, the fibre
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
is a
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
-vector space of dimension
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
. On the other hand, there exists a prime in
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
whose residue field is an extension of
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
of degree
[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
. Thus
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
is a field and there is only one prime in
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
lying above
\mathfrak{p}
.
∎

We next relate the abstract notion of Hilbert set with the more classical notion concerned with polynomials that remain irreducible under specialization (see
[
FJ08
, Chapter 12]
).

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
be monic univariate polynomials that are irreducible as elements of
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[y]
. For a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
let
\overline{p_{i}}
denote the image of
p_{i}
in
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})[y]
, i.e., the coefficients of
p_{i}
are reduced modulo
\mathfrak{p}
.
We set

H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})|\ \overline{p_{1}},\ldots,\overline{p_{n}}\in k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})[y]\text{ are irreducible }\}.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
be an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Then there exists a monic polynomial
p\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
such that
p
is irreducible in
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[y]
and a nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that

H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p)\cap D(b)\subseteq H(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0}).

Proof.

As
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})/k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
is a finite field extension, there exists, by the primitive element theorem, a
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
such that
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})=k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[b^{\prime}]
. Replacing
b^{\prime}
with a
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-multiple of
b^{\prime}
if necessary, we can assume that the minimal polynomial
p
of
b^{\prime}
over
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
has coefficients in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Write
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}_{0}[b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{n}]
and
b^{\prime}_{i}=p_{i}(b^{\prime})
with
p_{i}\in k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[y]

(1\leq i\leq n)
. Let
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}\smallsetminus\{0\}
be such that
bp_{i}
has coefficients in
\mathcal{B}_{0}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Then
b^{\prime}_{i}\in(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}[b^{\prime}]
and therefore
(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b}=(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}[b^{\prime}]=(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}[y]/(p)
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}\in H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p)\cap D(b)
. Then
\mathfrak{p}_{b}
is a prime ideal of
(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}
and for the fibre of
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0}
over
\mathfrak{p}
we have

\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})=(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})_{b}\otimes_{(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{b})=(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}[y]/(p)\otimes_{(\mathcal{B}_{0})_{b}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p}_{b})=k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})[y]/(\overline{p}).

As
\mathfrak{p}\in H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p)
, we see that
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
is a field extension of
k_{0}(\mathfrak{p})
of degree
\deg(p)=[k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}):k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})]
. Thus
\mathfrak{p}\in H(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}_{0})
as desired.
∎

As an immediate corollary to Lemma
2.12
we obtain:

Corollary 2.13
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert subset of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. Then there exist
p_{1}\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
monic and irreducible in
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[y]
and
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that

H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\cap D(b)\subseteq H.

∎

A different, less intrinsic, notion of geometric Hilbert set was used in
[
Fen21
]
. In order to be able to use the results from
[
Fen21
]
, we have to relate the two notions. Let us first recall the definition from
[
Fen21
, Notation 2.6]
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra that is an integral domain. By Noether’s normalization lemma, we can find
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell},\ldots,\eta_{m}\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}=k[\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}]
,
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}
are algebraically independent over
k
and
\eta_{\ell+1},\ldots,\ldots,\eta_{m}
are integral over
k[\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}]
. We abbreviate
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\ell}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{l})
.

Note that for
i=1,\ldots,m-\ell
, the minimal polynomial of
\eta_{\ell+i}
over
k(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{l})
has coefficients in
k[\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\ell}]
since
k[\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\ell}]
is integrally closed (
[
AM69
, Prop. 5.15]
). We can thus find a subfield
k_{0}
of
k
finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
such that, for
i=1,\ldots,m-\ell
, the minimal polynomial of
\eta_{\ell+i}
over
k(\boldsymbol{\eta}_{l})
has coefficients in
k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}_{\ell}]
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}
be a finite tuple of monic elements of
k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}][y]
that are irreducible in
k_{0}(\boldsymbol{\eta})[y]
. Furthermore, let
b\in k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}]\smallsetminus\{0\}
and let
\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\ell}
be an
\ell
-tuple of positive integers. Identifying
\mathcal{X}(k)
with
\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{B},k)
we define

\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{p},b)

as the set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that

•

[k_{0}(c(\eta_{1}),\ldots,c(\eta_{i})):k_{0}(c(\eta_{1}),\ldots,c(\eta_{i-1}))]\geq d_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
;

•

for every entry
p
of
\boldsymbol{p}
the polynomial
p^{c}
obtained from
p
by applying
c
to the coefficients is irreducible in
k_{0}(c(\boldsymbol{\eta}))[y]
;

•

c(b)\neq 0
.

The following lemma shows that every geometric Hilbert set contains a set of the form
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{p},b)
.

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
k_{0}\subseteq k
be a finitely generated field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
a finitely generated
k_{0}
-algebra such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}
is an isomorphism. Furthermore, let
H
be a Hilbert subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
and let
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(k)
be the corresponding geometric Hilbert set.

Then there exists
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{m}
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}=k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}]
and appropriate
\boldsymbol{p}
,
b
such that

\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}((1,\ldots,1),\boldsymbol{p},b)\subseteq\mathcal{H}.

Proof.

Applying Noether’s normalization lemma to
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, we find
\boldsymbol{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell},\ldots,\eta_{m})\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{m}
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}=k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}]
such that
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}
are algebraically independent over
k_{0}
and
\eta_{\ell+1},\ldots,\eta_{m}
are integral over
k_{0}[\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}]
. Note that
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}
are also algebraically independent over
k
and
\eta_{\ell+1},\ldots,\eta_{m}
are integral over
k[\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}]
. Moreover, for
i=1,\ldots,m-\ell
, the minimal polynomial of
\eta_{\ell+i}
over
k(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell})
has coefficients in
k_{0}[\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}]
.

By Corollary
2.13
, there exist monic polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[y]
irreducible in
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})[y]
and a
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}\smallsetminus\{0\}
such that

H_{\mathcal{B}_{0}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\cap D(b)\subseteq H
. With
\boldsymbol{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
we thus have
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}((1,\ldots,1),\boldsymbol{p},b)\subseteq\mathcal{H}.

∎

The following proposition, going back to
[
Hru02
]
, explains why the sets of the form
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{p},b)
are useful for us. We assume that
k_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
are as described after Corollary
2.13
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated subgroup of
(k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}],+)
. Then there exists a
\bf{d}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\emptyset,1)\subseteq W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
.

Proof.

This is really just a restatement of
[
Fen21
, Prop. 2.10]
.
∎

2.3
Néron’s specialization theorem

Néron’s specialization theorem is a crucial ingredient in our proof that ad
\times
Jac-open sets are Zariski dense (Theorem
2.24
). Roughly speaking, Néron’s specialization theorem states that in an algebraic family of abelian varieties, the specialization map defined by a point of the parameter space is injective for all points in a Hilbert subset of the parameter space. This theorem can be used to construct abelian varieties with groups of rational points of large rank. Several versions of Néron’s specialization theorem are available in the literature. The original reference is
[
Nér52
]
. Other presentations are in
[
Ser97b
, Section 11.1]
,
[
Lan83
, Chapter 9, Section 6]
and
[
Pet06
, Section 3]
. Unfortunately, none of these versions seems to directly yield the result we need. We therefore include a self-contained proof of our variant of Néron’s specialization theorem, mostly following
[
Ser97b
]
in the argument (cf.
[
Fen21
, Section 2]
).

Throughout Section
2.3
we make the following assumptions:

•

k_{0}
is a field of characteristic zero;

•

\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
is an affine variety over
k_{0}
(i.e., a geometrically integral affine scheme of finite type over
k_{0}
);

•

K_{0}=k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
is the function field of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, i.e., the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\subseteq\overline{K_{0}}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra;

•

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
;

•

{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
is a commutative separated group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\times_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}\operatorname{Spec}(K_{0})
is connected.

We also make the following assumption on
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
.

(A)

For every finitely generated subgroup
\Gamma
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
, the group

\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\mid\varepsilon^{n}\in\Gamma\mbox{ for some $n\geq 1$}\}

is also finitely generated.

We will need a few lemmas of a preparatory nature.

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
G
be a connected commutative algebraic group over
k_{0}
and let
n\geq 1
be an integer. Then the morphism
[n]\colon G\to G,\ g\mapsto g^{n}
has finite kernel and
[n]\colon G(\overline{k_{0}})\to G(\overline{k_{0}})
is surjective.

Proof.

Note that
[n]
is a morphism of algebraic groups because
G
is commutative. The induced morphism

\operatorname{Lie}([n])\colon\operatorname{Lie}(G)\to\operatorname{Lie}(G)
on the Lie algebra of
G
is multiplication by
n
. As we are in characteristic zero, this implies that
\operatorname{Lie}([n])
is an isomorphism. In particular,
\operatorname{Lie}(\ker([n]))=\ker(\operatorname{Lie}([n]))=0
. Thus
\ker([n])
is finite.
Then, for dimension reasons, the image of
[n]\colon G(\overline{k_{0}})\to G(\overline{k_{0}})
must have the same dimension as
G
. As the image is closed and
G
is connected, it follows that
[n]\colon G(\overline{k_{0}})\to G(\overline{k_{0}})
is surjective.
∎

For every
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
we have a specialization morphism

\sigma_{x^{\prime}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x^{\prime})),

obtained by applying
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
, considered as a functor on the category of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebras, to the morphism
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}\to k_{0}(x^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
and
n\geq 1
. Then the set
\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{K_{0}})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}
is finite and nonempty and there exist a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that

|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{K_{0}})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}|

for every
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The set
\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{K_{0}})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}
is finite and nonempty by Lemma
2.16
. Set
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0}={\mathcal{E}}_{0,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}}
and consider the morphism
[n]\colon{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0},\ g\mapsto g^{n}
and its kernel
\ker([n])\leq{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0}
. Then
\ker([n])_{x^{\prime}}
is the kernel of the
n
-th power map on
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,x^{\prime}}
for every
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
.

It follows from Lemma
2.16
that
\ker([n])_{\xi^{\prime}}
is finite, where
\xi^{\prime}
is the generic point of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
. Being finite spreads out from the generic fibre and also the number of geometric points is constant over a nonempty Zariski open subset (
[
Gro66
, Cor. 9.7.9]
). Thus, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that

|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,x^{\prime}}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})|\ \varepsilon^{n}=1\}|=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,\xi^{\prime}}(\overline{K_{0}})|\ \varepsilon^{n}=1\}|

for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Shrinking
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
if necessary, we can also assume that
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,x^{\prime}}
is connected for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. (Recall that an algebraic group is connected if and only if it is geometrically connected). Using Lemma
2.16
we then find

\displaystyle|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,x^{\prime}}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|=

\displaystyle=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,x^{\prime}}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})|\ \varepsilon^{n}=1\}|=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}_{0,\xi^{\prime}}(\overline{K_{0}})|\ \varepsilon^{n}=1\}|=

\displaystyle=|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{K_{0}})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}|

for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. ∎

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and let
s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
be such that
k_{0}(s(\xi^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(\xi^{\prime})=k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
is an isomorphism, where
\xi^{\prime}
is the generic point of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
and we think of
s
as a morphism
s\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\to\mathcal{S}
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that the morphism
k_{0}(s(x^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(x^{\prime})
induced by
s
is an isomorphism for every
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an affine open subset of
\mathcal{S}
containing
s(\xi^{\prime})
and let
D(b^{\prime})
be a basic Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
contained in
s^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
. Then the restriction
\tilde{s}\colon D(b^{\prime})\to\mathcal{U}
of
s\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\to\mathcal{S}
satisfies the assumption of the lemma, i.e., the induced map
k_{0}(\tilde{s}(\xi^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(\xi^{\prime})
is an isomorphism. We can thus assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{S}
is affine.

So we can identify
\mathcal{S}
with the spectrum of a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra
\mathcal{R}
. The point
s\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
corresponds to a morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebras. The assumption that
k_{0}(s(\xi^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(\xi^{\prime})
is an isomorphism, means that the field of fractions of
\psi(\mathcal{R})
(formed inside
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
) equals all of
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
.

Fix
b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{0}[b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{n}]
. For
i=1,\ldots,n
we can then write
b^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{\psi(r_{i})}{\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})}
with
r_{1},\ldots,r_{n},\widetilde{r_{1}},\ldots,\widetilde{r_{n}}\in\mathcal{R}
and
\psi(\widetilde{r_{1}}),\ldots,\psi(\widetilde{r_{m}})\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
nonzero. We claim that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=D(\psi(\widetilde{r_{1}})\ldots\psi(\widetilde{r_{n}}))\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
has the desired property.

Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
correspond to the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
. For
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
let
\overline{b^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\subseteq k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})=k_{0}(x^{\prime})
denote the image of
b^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
. As
\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})b^{\prime}_{i}=\psi(r_{i})
in
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
, we have
\overline{\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})}\overline{b^{\prime}_{i}}=\overline{\psi(r_{i})}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. As
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, we can divide by
\overline{\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})}
in
k_{0}(x^{\prime})
to obtain
\overline{b^{\prime}_{i}}=\frac{\overline{\psi(r_{i})}}{\overline{\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})}}
in
k_{0}(x^{\prime})
.
Note that the morphism
k_{0}(s(x^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(x^{\prime})
is obtained from the injection
\mathcal{R}/\psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
by passing to the field of fractions. In particular,
k_{0}(s(x^{\prime}))\to k_{0}(x^{\prime})
is an isomorphism if and only if the field of fractions of
\overline{\psi(\mathcal{R})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}\subseteq k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})
equals all of
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})
. As
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})=k_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})(\overline{b^{\prime}_{1}},\ldots,\overline{b^{\prime}_{n}})
, the latter follows from
\overline{b_{i}^{\prime}}=\frac{\overline{\psi(r_{i})}}{\overline{\psi(\widetilde{r_{i}})}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
S
be a finite subset of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}
is injective on
S
for every
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

It suffices to treat the case when
S
consists of two distinct elements
s_{1},s_{2}
.
As
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
is separated, the equalizer
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
of
s_{1},s_{2}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
(in the category of schemes over
\mathcal{X}_{0}
) is a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
(
[
Sta24
,
Tag 01KM
]
). In particular, for a morphism
\phi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
-schemes one has
s_{1}\phi=s_{2}\phi
if and only if
\phi
factors through the closed immersion
\mathcal{Z}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
.

We will show that
\mathcal{Z}
defines a proper closed subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
. Suppose, for a contradiction, that the underlying set of
\mathcal{Z}
is all of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
is reduced, the only closed subscheme supported on all of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
is
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
itself. So
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
(as schemes). But then
s_{1}=s_{2}
; a contradiction.

Thus
\mathcal{Z}
defines a proper closed subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
and its complement
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
is a nonempty Zariski open subset. For
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, the morphism
\phi_{x^{\prime}}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(k_{0}(x^{\prime}))\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
does not factor through
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
and so
s\phi_{x^{\prime}}\neq s^{\prime}\phi_{x^{\prime}}
, i.e.,
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(s_{1})\neq\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(s_{2})
. ∎

The following lemma explains how Hilbert subsets enter into Néron’s specialization theorem.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq K_{0}
a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra and
\gamma\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
. Then there exists a Hilbert subset
\tilde{H}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
such that

\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma)\}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}).

(4)

for all
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}
.

Proof.

Note that the inclusion “
\supseteq
” in (
4
) is trivial.
Set
S=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{K_{0}})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}
. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}\subseteq\overline{K_{0}}
be a finitely generated
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
-algebra such that
S\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
. By Lemma
2.19
there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
such that
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}
is injective on
S
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.17
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that

|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|=|S|

(5)

for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
.
Let
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\to\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
be the morphism corresponding to the inclusion
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
is algebraic over
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}

such that
k_{0}(x^{\prime})
is algebraic over
k_{0}(\phi(x^{\prime}))
for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
. (This follows, for example, from Lemma
2.10
.) By Chevalley’s theorem, there exists a nonempty basic Zariski open subset
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
contained in
\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime})
.

Let
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
. Then there exists an
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
with
\phi(x^{\prime})=\tilde{x}
. We claim that

\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(\tilde{x})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma)\}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(S).

(6)

As
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}
is injective on
S
, we find

|S|=|\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(S)|\leq|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x^{\prime}))\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|\leq|\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}|.

From (
5
), we thus deduce that
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(S)=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\gamma)\}
. This implies (
6
) because
\overline{k_{0}(x^{\prime})}=\overline{k_{0}(\tilde{x})}
since
k_{0}(x^{\prime})
is algebraic over
k_{0}(\tilde{x})=k_{0}(\phi(x^{\prime}))
.

We first assume that
S\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
. Then
S=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})|\ \varepsilon^{n}=\gamma\}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
by assumption.
The commutativity of

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\tilde{x}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{x^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x^{\prime}))}

paired with (
6
) then yields
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(S)=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\overline{k_{0}(\tilde{x})})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma)\}
. In particular,
\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma)\}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(S)
as desired.
So, in case
S\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
, we can choose as
\tilde{H}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}
.

Now assume that
S\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\neq\emptyset
and let
\varepsilon\in S\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
.
Then
k_{0}(\varepsilon(\overline{\xi}))\hookrightarrow\overline{K_{0}}
is a finite extension of
K_{0}
with
[k_{0}(\varepsilon(\overline{\xi})):K_{0}]>1
, where we think of
\varepsilon
as a morphism
\varepsilon\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{K_{0}})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
and
\overline{\xi}
is the unique point of
\operatorname{Spec}(\overline{K_{0}})
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq k_{0}(\varepsilon(\overline{\xi}))
be a finitely generated
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
-
algebra with
k_{0}(\varepsilon(\overline{\xi}))
as field of fractions and such that
\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
. Furthermore, we may assume
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
.

Then Lemma
2.18
, applied with
\mathcal{S}={\mathcal{E}}_{0}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}
and
s=\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
, yields a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}
of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
such that

k_{0}(\varepsilon(x))\to k_{0}(x)
is an isomorphism for every
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}
.
By Chevalley’s theorem, there exists a nonempty basic Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
lies in the image of
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}
under
