\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
.

Set
\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}\cap H(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}/\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
and let
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon}
. Then
[k_{0}(x_{\varepsilon}):k_{0}(\tilde{x})]=[k_{0}(\varepsilon(\overline{\xi})):K_{0}]>1
, where
x_{\varepsilon}
is the unique element of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
lying above
\tilde{x}
. As
x_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}
, the field extensions
k_{0}(\varepsilon(x_{\varepsilon}))
and
k_{0}(x_{\varepsilon})
of
k_{0}(\tilde{x})
are isomorphic. Therefore,
k_{0}(\varepsilon(x_{\varepsilon}))
is a non-trivial field extension of
k_{0}(\tilde{x})
.

We will show that
\tilde{H}=\tilde{\mathcal{U}}\cap(\cap_{\varepsilon\in S\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})}\tilde{H}_{\varepsilon})
has the desired property. Let
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}
.
Then there exist an
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
with
\phi(x^{\prime})=\tilde{x}
.
Given (
6
), to verify (
4
), it suffices to show that
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\varepsilon)\notin{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))
for every
\varepsilon\in S\smallsetminus{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
.
Note that
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}(\varepsilon)\notin{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))
if and only if
k_{0}(\varepsilon(x^{\prime}))
is a non-trivial extension of
k_{0}(\tilde{x})
.

For
\varepsilon\in S\setminus{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
, since
x_{\varepsilon}
is the unique element of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
lying above
\tilde{x}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
, we see that
x_{\varepsilon}
is the image of
x^{\prime}
under
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon})
. So
\varepsilon(x^{\prime})=\varepsilon(x_{\varepsilon})
and
k_{0}(\varepsilon(x^{\prime}))=k_{0}(\varepsilon(x_{\varepsilon}))
is a non-trivial extension of
k_{0}(\tilde{x})
as desired. ∎

We are now prepared to prove our variant of Néron’s specialization theorem.

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finitely generated subgroup of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
. Then there exists a Hilbert subset
H_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that
\sigma_{x_{0}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x_{0}))
is injective on
\Gamma
for every
x_{0}\in H_{0}
.

Proof.

Set
\tilde{\Gamma}=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\mid\varepsilon^{n}\in\Gamma\mbox{ for some $n\geq 1$}\}
. Then
\tilde{\Gamma}
is a subgroup of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
and by assumption (A) it is finitely generated. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq K_{0}
be a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{0}
-algebra such that
\tilde{\Gamma}\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
. We shall show that there exists a Hilbert subset
\tilde{H}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
such that the specialization map
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathcal{E}}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))
is injective on
\tilde{\Gamma}
for every
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}
. Note that this implies the theorem since, by Lemma
2.11
, there exists a Hilbert subset
H_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
contained in the image of
\tilde{H}
under
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
and if
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}
maps to
x_{0}\in H_{0}
, then the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{x_{0}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\tilde{x}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x_{0}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))}

commutes.

Let
n>1
be an integer such that the order of the torsion subgroup of
\tilde{\Gamma}
divides
n
.
Let
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m}\}
be a set of representatives of
\tilde{\Gamma}/\tilde{\Gamma}^{n}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\gamma_{1}=1
. For
i=1,\dots,m
set
\Delta_{i}=\{\varepsilon\in\tilde{\Gamma}\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma_{i}\}
. Note that
\Delta_{i}=\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma_{i}\}
by construction of
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

Lemma
2.20
applied with
\gamma=\gamma_{i}
, yields a Hilbert subset
\tilde{H}_{i}
of
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
such that

\{\varepsilon\in{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(\tilde{x}))\mid\varepsilon^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma_{i})\}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\Delta_{i})

for every
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}_{i}
.
Set
\tilde{H}=\cap_{i=1}^{m}\tilde{H}_{i}
and let
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}
.
We will show that
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}
is injective on
\tilde{\Gamma}
.
It suffices to show that
\Gamma_{0}=\ker(\sigma_{\tilde{x}})\cap\tilde{\Gamma}
is trivial. Note that
\Gamma_{0}
is a finitely generated group because it is a subgroup of the finitely generated group
\tilde{\Gamma}
.

We claim that
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{0}^{n}
. Let
\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}
and write
\gamma=\gamma_{i}\tilde{\gamma}^{n}
for some
i
and
\tilde{\gamma}\in\tilde{\Gamma}
. Then
1=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma)=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma_{i})\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\tilde{\gamma})^{n}
and so
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\tilde{\gamma}^{-1})^{n}=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\gamma_{i})
. Because
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{H}_{i}
, we have
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\tilde{\gamma}^{-1})=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon\in\Delta_{i}
, i.e.,
\varepsilon^{n}=\gamma_{i}
. This implies that
\gamma_{i}=1
, i.e.,
i=1
.

Hence
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\tilde{\gamma}^{-1})=\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon\in\tilde{\Gamma}
and
\varepsilon^{n}=1
. So
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}(\varepsilon\tilde{\gamma})=1
and
\varepsilon\tilde{\gamma}\in\Gamma_{0}
. Moreover,

\gamma=\gamma_{i}\tilde{\gamma}^{n}=\tilde{\gamma}^{n}=\varepsilon^{n}\tilde{\gamma}^{n}=(\varepsilon\tilde{\gamma})^{n}\in\Gamma_{0}^{n}.

Thus
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{0}^{n}
as claimed.
As the order of the torsion subgroup of
\tilde{\Gamma}
divides
n
, the group
\tilde{\Gamma}^{n}
is torsion free. Therefore, also
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{0}^{n}\subseteq\tilde{\Gamma}^{n}
is torsion free.
But a finitely generated torsion-free group is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module and so can satisfy
\Gamma_{0}^{n}=\Gamma_{0}
only if
\Gamma_{0}=1
is trivial. Thus
\Gamma_{0}=1
and
\sigma_{\tilde{x}}
is injective on
\tilde{\Gamma}
for every
\tilde{x}\in\tilde{\Gamma}
as desired.
∎

The following corollary contains the special case of Theorem
2.21
relevant for our purpose.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
k_{0}
be a finitely generated field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
,
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
an affine variety over
k_{0}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
a group scheme over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of abelian type. Then there exists a Hilbert subset
H_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that the specialization morphism
\sigma_{x_{0}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x_{0}))
is injective for every
x_{0}\in H_{0}
.

Proof.

Since
K_{0}
is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
, by the generalized Mordell-Weil theorem (
[
Lan83
, Chapter 6, Thm. 1]
) the group
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
is finitely generated. Therefore, assumption (A) is satisfied and also
\Gamma={\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0})\leq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(K_{0})
is finitely generated. Thus Theorem
2.21
applies.
∎

In addition to the case of group schemes of abelian type explained in the above corollary, Theorem
2.21
also applies in the case where
k_{0}
is a finitely generated field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}=\mathbb{G}_{m,\mathcal{B}_{0}}
is the multiplicative group scheme over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. In this case assumption
assumption (A) is verified by
[
Fen21
, Prop. 2.12]
.

Theorem
2.21
does not apply to the additive group scheme
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{B}_{0}}
over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
because assumption (A) is violated. For example, for
\Gamma=(\mathbb{Z},+)
, the group
in (A) equals
(\mathbb{Q},+)
.

2.4
Denseness of ad
\times
Jac-open sets

In this section we combine the results of the previous sections to show that ad
\times
Jac-open subsets are Zariski dense.
The following proposition is an important step in this direction. It can be seen as a “geometric” version of Corollary
2.22
.

Proposition 2.23
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an ab-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}
a finite subset. Then there exists a geometric
\mathcal{B}_{0}/k_{0}
-Hilbert set
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. In particular,
every ab-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
contains a geometric Hilbert set.

Proof.

The ab-open set
\mathcal{U}
is of the form
\mathcal{U}=\phi(W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma))
,
where
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
with
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
an integral domain containing
\mathcal{B}
, finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
,
{\mathcal{E}}
is a group scheme of abelian type over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
\Gamma
is a finitely generated subgroup of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
and
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
The idea of the proof is to descend the whole situation from
k
to a finitely generated field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
so that we can apply Néron’s specialization theorem. We claim that we can find

•

a nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}
such that every
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)
lifts to a prime ideal of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
;

•

a subfield
k_{0}
of
k
that is finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
;

•

a finitely generated
k_{0}
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of
\mathcal{B}
containing
b
and
S
such that the canonical map
\mathcal{B}_{0}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}
is an isomorphism;

•

a finitely generated
k_{0}
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
such that the canonical map
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism and such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
is algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
;

•

a group scheme
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
of abelian type such that the base change from
k_{0}
to
k
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
equals
{\mathcal{E}}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
(where we identify
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})
with a subset of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
).

To see that we can find the above items we argue as follows. First, by Chevalley’s theorem, we can find an appropriate
b\in\mathcal{B}
.
Let
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}
be a finite generating set of
\Gamma
.
Writing
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as the directed union of all of its finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-algebras and considering the morphisms
\gamma_{1}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{E}},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{E}}
and the separated commutative group scheme
{\mathcal{E}}
over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, we can combine Theorem 8.8.2 and Theorem 8.10.5 (v) of
[
Gro66
]
, to obtain a finitely generated
\mathbb{Z}
-subalgebra
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
, a commutative separated group scheme
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
of finite type over
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
and morphisms
\tilde{\gamma_{i}}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})\to\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}

(i=1,\ldots,n)
such that the base change of
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
from
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is
{\mathcal{E}}
and the base change of
\tilde{\gamma_{i}}
from
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is
\gamma_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. So, if we identify
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
with a subset of
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, then
\Gamma\subseteq\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
.

Next, choose a finite generating set
S^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as a
k
-algebra such that
S^{\prime}
contains a finite generating set of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra. Then choose a finite generating set
T
of
\mathcal{B}
as a
k
-algebra such that
b\in T
,
S\subseteq T
and all elements of
S^{\prime}
satisfy a nonzero univariate (not necessarily monic) polynomial over
\mathbb{Z}[T]
. Set
T^{\prime}=S^{\prime}\cup T
. Then
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
,
T
generates
\mathcal{B}
as a
k
-algebra,
T^{\prime}
generates
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as a
k
-algebra and every element of
T^{\prime}
is algebraic over
\mathbb{Z}[T]
. Next, let
k_{0}\subseteq k
be a finitely generated field extension of
\mathbb{Q}
such that the ideal of all
k
-algebraic relations among the elements of
T
as well as the ideal of all
k
-algebraic relations among the elements of
T^{\prime}
is generated by polynomials with coefficients in
k_{0}
. Set
\mathcal{B}_{0}=k_{0}[T]
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}=k_{0}[T^{\prime}]
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Moreover,
b\in\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
and the morphisms
\mathcal{B}_{0}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}\otimes_{k_{0}}k\to\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
are isomorphism.

Let
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
be the base change of
\tilde{{\mathcal{E}}}
from
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
.
Then
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
is a commutative separated group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
with
\Gamma\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
and the base change of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
from
\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is
{\mathcal{E}}
. It follows that the base change of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})}\operatorname{Spec}(k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}))
from
k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}))
to
k(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
is
{\mathcal{E}}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})}k(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. Since the latter is an abelian variety, also
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})}\operatorname{Spec}(k_{0}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}))
must be an abelian variety. So
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
is of abelian type. In summary, we have successfully descended everything to
k_{0}
: The base change of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}/\mathcal{B}_{0}
from
k_{0}
to
k
is
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}
and the base change of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0}
from
k_{0}
to
k
is
{\mathcal{E}}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Set
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0})
so that
{\mathcal{E}}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is the base change from
k_{0}
to
k
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

By Neron’s specialization theorem (Corollary
2.22
), there exists a Hilbert subset
H_{0}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{0}
such that the specialization map
\sigma_{x_{0}^{\prime}}\colon{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})\to{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime}))
is injective for every
x^{\prime}_{0}\in H^{\prime}_{0}
.
By Lemma
2.11
there exists a Hilbert subset
H_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that the image of
H_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
contains
H_{0}
and every element of
H_{0}
has a unique lift to an element of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the inverse image of
H_{0}\cap D(b)
under
\mathcal{X}(k)\to\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Then
\mathcal{H}
is a geometric
\mathcal{B}_{0}/k_{0}
-Hilbert set.

We claim that the geometric Hilbert set
\mathcal{H}
is contained in
\mathcal{U}
. Let
c\in\mathcal{H}
and let
x,x_{0}
denote the image of
c
in
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{0}
respectively. By construction of
H_{0}
, there exists an
x_{0}^{\prime}\in H_{0}^{\prime}
mapping to
x_{0}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{p}
is the prime ideal of
\mathcal{B}
corresponding to
x
, then
b\notin\mathfrak{p}
and so
x
lifts to a point
x^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
by the choice of
b
. The commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{0}}

then shows that the image of
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
equals
x_{0}^{\prime}
because
x_{0}^{\prime}
is the unique lift of
x_{0}
to an element of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
.
We then have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(\mathcal{B}_{0}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{x_{0}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{x^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}_{0}(k_{0}(x_{0}^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{E}}(k(x^{\prime}))}

where the horizontal maps are injections. As
\sigma_{x_{0}^{\prime}}
is injective, it is in particular injective on
\Gamma
. Thus also
\sigma_{x^{\prime}}=\sigma_{c}
is injective on
\Gamma
. So
c\in W_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{E}},\Gamma)=\mathcal{U}
as desired.
∎

We are now prepared to prove the main theorem of Section
2
.

Theorem 2.24
.

The intersection of an ad-open and an ab-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
. In particular, an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
be an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
and let
\mathcal{V}
be an ab-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. We first show that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}
is nonempty.

Choose a finite generating set
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}
of the finitely generated subgroup
\Gamma
of
(\mathcal{B},+)
. By Proposition
2.23
there exists a geometric
\mathcal{B}_{0}/k_{0}
-Hilbert set
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
with
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
. Now Lemma
2.14
yields an
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}^{m}
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}=k_{0}[\boldsymbol{\eta}]
and appropriate
\boldsymbol{p}
,
b
such that

\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}((1,\ldots,1),\boldsymbol{p},b)\subseteq\mathcal{H}.
In particular,
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}((1,\ldots,1),\boldsymbol{p},b)\subseteq\mathcal{V}.

On the other hand,
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
as
S\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}
and so by Proposition
2.15
, there exists a
\boldsymbol{d}
such that
\mathcal{H}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}(\boldsymbol{d},\emptyset,1)\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
Thus

\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{p},b)=\mathcal{H}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}(\boldsymbol{d},\emptyset,1)\cap\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}((1,\ldots,1),\boldsymbol{p},b)\subseteq\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{k_{0},\mathcal{X}(k)}^{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{p},b)\neq\emptyset
by
[
Fen21
, Prop. 2.8]
, we can conclude that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}\neq\emptyset
.

We next show that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}
is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
. Suppose, for a contradiction, that
\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}
is not Zariski dense. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}=\emptyset
. So, if
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
is any ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
contained in
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}
, then
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{V}=\emptyset
. This contradicts the first part of the proof.
∎

We can do slightly better:

Remark 2.25
.

The intersection of finitely many ad-open and finitely many ab-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proof.

The intersection of finitely many ad-open subsets contains an ad-open subset (Remark
2.3
) and the intersection of finitely many ab-open subsets is an ab-open subset (argue as in (
2
)). Thus the remark follows from Theorem
2.24
.
∎

3
Some topics in differential Galois theory

This section is a collection of definitions, results and constructions from differential Galois theory, mostly related to the computation of the differential Galois group via Hrushovski’s algorithm. In Section
4
, in the course of the proof of our main specialization theorem (Theorem
4.26
), we will then show that many of the properties and constructions discussed here are preserved on an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of the parameter space.

Throughout Section
3
we assume that

•

(F,\delta)
is a differential field with field of constants

•

k=F^{\delta}
algebraically closed and of characteristic zero.

In the later subsections we will specialize to the case
(F,\delta)=(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
or
F
a finite field extension of
k(x)
. The reader is invited to recall our notational conventions from the end of the introduction.

3.1
Basics of differential Galois theory

We begin by recalling the basic definitions and results from the Galois theory of linear differential equations. Proofs and more background can be found in any of the introductory textbooks
[
Mag94
]
,
[
vdPS03
]
,
[
CH11
]
,
[
Sau16
]
.

Recall that a differential ring
R
is
\delta
-simple
if it is not the zero ring and the only
\delta
-ideals of
R
are
R
and the zero ideal.

Definition 3.1
.

A
Picard-Vessiot ring
for
\delta(y)=Ay
, (
A\in F^{n\times n}
) is a
\delta
-simple
F
-
\delta
-
algebra
R
such that there exists a
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
with
\delta(Y)=AY
and
R=F[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
.

For any given
A\in F^{n\times n}
, there exist a Picard-Vessiot ring
R/F
for
\delta(y)=Ay
and it is unique up to an
F
-
\delta
-isomorphism. Moreover,
R^{\delta}=k
. The
differential Galois group

G=G(R/F)
of
R/F
(or of
\delta(y)=Ay
) is the functor from the category of
k
-algebras to the category of groups given by

G(T)=\operatorname{Aut}(R\otimes_{k}T/F\otimes_{k}T)
, where
T
is considered as a constant differential ring. It is a linear algebraic group (over
k
).
Its coordinate ring
k[G]
can explicitly be described as
k[G]=(R\otimes_{F}R)^{\delta}=k[Z,\frac{1}{\det(Z)}]
, with
Z=Y^{-1}\otimes Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}((R\otimes_{K}R)^{\delta})
and
Y
as in Definition
3.1
. The canonical map

R\otimes_{k}k[G]\to R\otimes_{F}R

is an isomorphism and so we may make the identification
R\otimes_{F}R=R\otimes_{k}k[G]
. For a
k
-
algebra
T
, any
g\in G(T)
, i.e., any
K\otimes_{k}T
-
\delta
-isomorphism
g\colon R\otimes_{k}T\to R\otimes_{k}T
, is determined by
g(Y\otimes 1)
. Moreover,
g(Y\otimes 1)=Y\otimes[g]
for a unique
[g]\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(T)
. The assignment
g\mapsto[g]
determines a morphism
G\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k}
of algebraic groups over
k
which is a closed embedding. The dual of this morphism is the map
k[\operatorname{GL}_{n}]=k[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\to k[G],\ X\mapsto Z
.

The (functorial) action of
G
on
R/F
is determined by the map
\rho\colon R\to R\otimes_{k}k[G]
, which, under the identification
R\otimes_{F}R=R\otimes_{k}k[G]
corresponds to the inclusion into the second factor. Explicitly, we have
\rho(Y)=Y\otimes Z
. For
g\in G(T)=\operatorname{Hom}(k[G],T)
, the corresponding automorphism
g\colon R\otimes_{k}T\to R\otimes_{k}T
is the
T
-linear extension of
R\xrightarrow{\rho}R\otimes_{k}k[G]\to R\otimes_{k}T
.

The following lemma shows that Picard-Vessiot rings and differential Galois group are well-behaved under extension of the constants. For a proof see, e.g.
[
BHHW21a
, Lemma 5.2]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
be an inclusion of algebraically closed fields and let
R/k(x)
be a Picard-Vessiot ring with differential Galois group
G
. Then
R\otimes_{k(x)}k^{\prime}(x)/k^{\prime}(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring with differential Galois group
G_{k^{\prime}}
. ∎

3.2
Differential torsors

Differential torsors were introduced in
[
BHHW18
]
for the purpose of solving differential embedding problems. To study the behaviour of Picard-Vessiot rings and differential embedding problems under specialization, we will need a version of differential torsors that works over an arbitrary base and not just a differential field, as in
[
BHHW18
]
.

As in the introduction, consider an inclusion
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
of algebraically closed fields of characteristic zero and a differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
over
k^{\prime}(x)
with differential Galois group
G
and Picard-Vessiot ring
R/k^{\prime}(x)
. To discuss specializations of
G
and
R
down to
k
one first needs to spread out
G
and
R
into families, i.e., we would like to have a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
, an affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
and a differential ring
\mathcal{R}
over
\mathcal{B}
such that the base change from
\mathcal{B}
to
k^{\prime}
yields
G
and
R
. It is then natural to also ask for an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}
compatible with the action of
G
on
R
. Moreover, since
R
defines a
G
-torsor, also
\mathcal{R}
should define a
\mathcal{G}
-torsor. This idea is formalized through the notion of differential torsor.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a
k
-algebra (considered as a constant
\delta
-ring) and let
\mathcal{Q}
be a
\mathcal{B}
-
\delta
-algebra.
The two most relevant cases for us are, firstly
\mathcal{B}=k
and
\mathcal{Q}=k(x)
and, secondly
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
, where
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
is a monic polynomial.

For a
\mathcal{Q}
-
\delta
-algebra
\mathcal{R}
, we define
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q})
to be the functor from the category of
\mathcal{B}
-
algebras to the category of groups given by
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q})(\mathcal{T})=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T})
for any
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
. Here
\mathcal{T}
is considered as a constant
\delta
-ring and the automorphisms are understood to be differential automorphisms. An element
g
of
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q})(\mathcal{T})
is thus a
\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
-
\delta
-
isomorphism
g\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
. On morphisms
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q})
is given by base change.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be an affine group scheme over
\mathcal{B}
. An
action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is a morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q})
of group functors. In particular, for a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
, every
g\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})
defines a
\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
-
\delta
-
automorphism
g\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

To specify an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is equivalent to specifying a morphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
of
\mathcal{Q}
-
\delta
-algebras such that the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho\otimes\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes\Delta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]}

and

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\cdot\varepsilon}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}}

commute, where
\Delta\colon\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\to\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is the comultiplication and
\varepsilon\colon\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\to\mathcal{B}
the counit of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
over
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

As this is a fairly standard argument, we only sketch the proof. First assume that an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is given.
For
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
and
g=\operatorname{id}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}],\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}])
we thus have a
\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
-
\delta
-automorphism
g\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
. Then we can define
\rho
as the composition
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\xrightarrow{g}\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
.

Conversely, given
\rho
, we define an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
as follows. For a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
and
g\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}],\mathcal{T})
we define
g\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
as the
\mathcal{T}
-linear extension of
\mathcal{R}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\xrightarrow{\mathcal{R}\otimes g}\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
. The first diagram corresponds to the associativity of this action while the second diagram shows that the identity acts trivial.
∎

As above, let
\mathcal{G}
act on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
and let
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
denote the
coaction
(as in Lemma
3.3
). We call
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
a
differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
if
\mathcal{R}
is not the zero ring and the map
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}],\ a\otimes b\mapsto(a\otimes 1)\cdot\rho(b)
is an isomorphism.
The geometric interpretation of this condition is the usual torsor condition, i.e., for
\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{R})
, the morphism

\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{Q}}\to\mathcal{Z}\times_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{Z},\ (z,g)\mapsto(z,zg)

of affine schemes over
\mathcal{Q}
is an isomorphism.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{Q}^{n\times n}
. A differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is a
differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
if there exists a
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
such that

(i)

\delta(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{Y}
,

(ii)

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Q}[\mathcal{Y},\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}]
and

(iii)

for every
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
and every
g\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})
there exists a
[g]\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{T})
such that
g(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)=(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)(1\otimes[g])
.

A matrix
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
such that the above three conditions are satisfied is called a
fundamental matrix
.

Assuming Definition
3.4
, the assignment
g\mapsto[g]
defines a morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
of group schemes over
\mathcal{B}
.

Example 3.5
.

The most basic example of the above definitions is when
\mathcal{B}=k
,
\mathcal{Q}=F
is a differential field with
F^{\delta}=k
and
\mathcal{R}=R
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
F
for
\delta(y)=Ay
(
A\in F^{n\times n}
) with differential Galois group
\mathcal{G}=G
. As explained in Section
3.1
,
G
acts on
R/F
and
R/F
is a differential
G
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
. Note that if
\mathcal{Y}=Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
is such that
\delta(Y)=AY
and
R=K[Y,\frac{1}{\det{Y}}]
, then condition (iii) of Definition
3.4
is automatically satisfied (because
(R\otimes_{k}T)^{\delta}=T
for any
k
-algebra
T
).

In the following example we construct a differential torsor for the group scheme of
2\times 2
monomial matrices.

Example 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}[\eta]
be an inclusion of differential
k
-algebras such that
\mathcal{B}
is constant and
\mathcal{Q}[\eta]
is an integral domain. We assume that
\eta
has minimal polynomial
y^{2}-a
over the field of fractions of
\mathcal{Q}
, where
a
is some element of
\mathcal{Q}^{\times}
. We also fix
b\in\mathcal{Q}
. Let
y_{1},y_{2}
be indeterminates over
\mathcal{Q}[\eta]
and define a derivation
\delta
on
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Q}[\eta][y_{1},y_{2},y^{-1}_{1},y^{-1}_{2}]
by
\delta(y_{1})=(b+\eta)y_{1},\ \delta(y_{2})=(b-\eta)y_{2}
.
Set

\mathcal{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&y_{2}\\
\delta(y_{1})&\delta(y_{2})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&y_{2}\\
(b+\eta)y_{1}&(b-\eta)y_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Then
\det(\mathcal{Y})=-2\eta y_{1}y_{2}
. Therefore
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Q}[\mathcal{Y},\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}]
.
A direct computation shows that
\delta(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{Y}
, where

\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
a+\delta(b)-b^{2}-\frac{\delta(a)b}{2a}&2b+\frac{\delta(a)}{2a}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{Q}^{2\times 2}.

We would like to describe
\mathcal{Z}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{R})
explicitly as a closed subscheme of
\operatorname{GL}_{2,\mathcal{Q}}
. To this end, let
\mathcal{I}
denote the kernel of the morphism
\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\to\mathcal{R},\ X\mapsto\mathcal{Y}
. As
\mathcal{R}
is an integral domain,
\mathcal{I}
is a prime differential ideal of
\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
, where
\delta(X)=\mathcal{A}X
. We have

(b+\eta)y_{1}\cdot(b-\eta)y_{2}=(b^{2}-a)y_{1}y_{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad(b+\eta)y_{1}\cdot y_{2}+(b-\eta)y_{2}\cdot y_{1}=2by_{1}y_{2}.

Therefore, the polynomials

p_{1}=X_{21}X_{22}-(b^{2}-a)X_{11}X_{12}\quad\text{ and }\quad p_{2}=X_{21}X_{12}+X_{22}X_{11}-2bX_{11}X_{12}

lie in
\mathcal{I}
. We will show that
\mathcal{I}=(p_{1},p_{2})
. For this, it suffices to show that the induced map
\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})\to\mathcal{R}
is injective.
Let
z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}
denote the image in
\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})
of
X_{11},X_{12},X_{21},X_{22}
respectively. Then

z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}^{\prime}=(b^{2}-a)z_{1}z_{2},

(7)

and

z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}+z_{2}^{\prime}z_{1}=2bz_{1}z_{2}.

(8)

Subtracting
2z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}
from (
8
) yields
z_{1}z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}=2z_{2}(bz_{1}-z_{1}^{\prime})
. Since
z_{1}z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}
is invertible, we see that also
z_{2}
is invertible. Similarly, subtracting
2z_{2}^{\prime}z_{1}
from (
8
), we see that
z_{1}
is invertible. So (
8
) can be rewritten as

\tfrac{z^{\prime}_{1}}{z_{1}}+\tfrac{z_{2}^{\prime}}{z_{2}}=2b

(9)

whereas (
7
) becomes
\frac{z^{\prime}_{1}}{z_{1}}\cdot\frac{z_{2}^{\prime}}{z_{2}}=b^{2}-a
. Plugging
\frac{z^{\prime}_{2}}{z_{2}}=2b-\frac{z_{1}^{\prime}}{z_{1}}
into the latter equation yields
(\frac{z^{\prime}_{1}}{z_{1}}-b)^{2}=a
. Set
\eta^{\prime}=\frac{z^{\prime}_{1}}{z_{1}}-b
. Then
\eta^{\prime 2}=a
,
z_{1}^{\prime}=(b+\eta^{\prime})z_{1}
and from (
9
) we obtain
z_{2}^{\prime}=(b-\eta^{\prime})z_{2}
.

Set
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{Q}[\eta^{\prime},z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}^{-1},z_{2}^{-1}]
. We claim that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})
. It suffices to show that
z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime}
and
\frac{1}{z_{1}z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}}
lie in
\mathcal{S}
. But
z_{1}^{\prime}=(b+\eta^{\prime})z_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
,
z_{2}^{\prime}=(b-\eta^{\prime})z_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
and

z_{1}z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}=2\eta^{\prime}z_{1}z_{2}
. So
\frac{1}{z_{1}z_{2}^{\prime}-z_{1}^{\prime}z_{2}}\in\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{Q}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})
as claimed.

The map

\mathcal{Q}[\eta^{\prime}][z_{1},z_{2},z_{1}^{-1},z_{2}^{-1}]=\mathcal{Q}[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{Q}[\eta][y_{1},y_{2},y^{-1}_{1},y^{-1}_{2}]

(10)

sends
\eta^{\prime}
to
\eta
,
z_{1}
to
y_{1}
and
z_{2}
to
y_{2}
.
As
\eta^{\prime 2}=a
, the restriction
\mathcal{Q}[\eta^{\prime}]\to\mathcal{Q}[\eta]
is injective. But then, since
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are algebraically independent over
\mathcal{Q}[\eta]
, the map (
10
) is injective. Thus
\mathcal{I}=(p_{1},p_{2})
as claimed. In other words,
\mathcal{Z}
is the closed subscheme of
\operatorname{GL}_{2,\mathcal{Q}}
defined by
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the group scheme of monomial
2\times 2
matrices over
\mathcal{B}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{T})\mid g_{11}g_{12}=g_{21}g_{22}=g_{11}g_{21}=g_{12}g_{22}=0\right\}\leq\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{T})

(11)

for any
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
. In particular, if
\mathcal{T}
is an integral domain then

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}s&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}\mid s,t\in\mathcal{T}^{\times}\right\}\cup\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&s\\
t&0\end{pmatrix}\mid s,t\in\mathcal{T}^{\times}\right\}\leq\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{T}).

Note that the equations in (
11
) are redundant, in fact,

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{T})\mid g_{11}g_{12}=g_{21}g_{22}=0\right\}

for any
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
. For example, if
g_{11}g_{12}=g_{21}g_{22}=0
, then
(g_{11}g_{22}-g_{12}g_{21})g_{11}g_{21}=0
and so also
g_{11}g_{21}=0
since
g_{11}g_{22}-g_{12}g_{21}
is invertible.

We will define an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
. Roughly, the idea is that a diagonal matrix
\begin{pmatrix}s&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}
acts by
y_{1}\mapsto sy_{1}
,
y_{2}\mapsto ty_{2}
and fixing
\eta
, whereas the permutation matrix
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}

acts by interchanging
y_{1}
with
y_{2}
and
\eta
with
-\eta
. In general, for
\mathcal{T}
a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra,
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})
acts on
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
by
g(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{Y}\otimes g
. To make sure that this is well-defined, we check that
for any
\mathcal{Q}
-algebra
\mathcal{S}
,
Z=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{11}&Z_{12}\\
Z_{21}&Z_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
and
g=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&g_{12}\\
g_{21}&g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{S})
, we have
Zg=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{11}g_{11}+Z_{12}g_{21}&Z_{11}g_{12}+Z_{12}g_{22}\\
Z_{21}g_{11}+Z_{22}g_{21}&Z_{21}g_{12}+Z_{22}g_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
.
Using (
11
), we find

\displaystyle p_{1}(Zg)

\displaystyle=(Z_{21}g_{11}+Z_{22}g_{21})(Z_{21}g_{12}+Z_{22}g_{22})-(b^{2}-a)(Z_{11}g_{11}+Z_{12}g_{21})(Z_{11}g_{12}+Z_{12}g_{22})

\displaystyle=Z_{21}Z_{22}g_{11}g_{22}+Z_{22}Z_{21}g_{21}g_{12}-(b^{2}-a)(Z_{11}Z_{12}g_{11}g_{22}+Z_{12}Z_{11}g_{21}g_{12})

\displaystyle=p_{1}(Z)g_{11}g_{22}+p_{1}(Z)g_{21}g_{12}=0

and

\displaystyle p_{2}(Zg)

\displaystyle=(Z_{21}g_{11}+Z_{22}g_{21})(Z_{11}g_{12}+Z_{12}g_{22})+(Z_{21}g_{12}+Z_{22}g_{22})(Z_{11}g_{11}+Z_{12}g_{21})-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad 2b(Z_{11}g_{11}+Z_{12}g_{21})(Z_{11}g_{12}+Z_{12}g_{22})

\displaystyle=Z_{21}Z_{12}g_{11}g_{22}+Z_{22}Z_{11}g_{21}g_{12}+Z_{21}Z_{12}g_{12}g_{21}+Z_{22}Z_{11}g_{11}g_{22}-

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad 2b(Z_{11}Z_{12}g_{11}g_{22}+Z_{12}Z_{11}g_{21}g_{12})

\displaystyle=p_{2}(Z)g_{11}g_{22}+p_{2}(Z)g_{21}g_{12}=0.

To see that
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is indeed a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor, it suffices to check that for
Z,Z^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{S})
, the matrix
Z^{-1}Z^{\prime}
lies in
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{S})
.
As

Z^{-1}Z^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\det(Z)}\begin{pmatrix}Z_{22}&-Z_{12}\\
-Z_{21}&Z_{11}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Z^{\prime}_{11}&Z^{\prime}_{12}\\
Z^{\prime}_{21}&Z^{\prime}_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{\det(Z)}\begin{pmatrix}Z_{22}Z^{\prime}_{11}-Z_{12}Z^{\prime}_{21}&Z_{22}Z^{\prime}_{12}-Z_{12}Z^{\prime}_{22}\\
Z_{11}Z^{\prime}_{21}-Z_{21}Z^{\prime}_{11}&Z_{11}Z^{\prime}_{22}-Z_{21}Z^{\prime}_{12}\end{pmatrix},

it suffices to show that

(Z_{22}Z^{\prime}_{11}-Z_{12}Z^{\prime}_{21})(Z_{22}Z^{\prime}_{12}-Z_{12}Z^{\prime}_{22})=0

(12)

and

(Z_{11}Z^{\prime}_{21}-Z_{21}Z^{\prime}_{11})(Z_{11}Z^{\prime}_{22}-Z_{21}Z^{\prime}_{12})=0.

(13)

To verify these identities, it is helpful to rewrite
p_{1}(Z)=0
and
p_{2}(Z)=0
as

\tfrac{Z_{21}}{Z_{11}}\cdot\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}=b^{2}-a\quad\text{ and }\tfrac{Z_{21}}{Z_{11}}+\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}=2b.

(14)

(Note that subtracting
2Z_{21}Z_{12}
from
p_{2}(Z)=0
and using the invertibility of
\det(Z)
shows that
Z_{12}
is invertible. By a similar argument,
Z_{11}
is invertible.) From (
14
) we deduce
(\frac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}-b)^{2}=a
, which leads to

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}})^{2}-2b\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}+(b^{2}-a)=(\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}})^{2}-\left(\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{21}}{Z^{\prime}_{11}}+\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{22}}{Z^{\prime}_{12}}\right)\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}-\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{21}}{Z^{\prime}_{11}}\cdot\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{22}}{Z^{\prime}_{12}}=

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}-\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{21}}{Z^{\prime}_{11}}\right)\left(\tfrac{Z_{22}}{Z_{12}}-\tfrac{Z^{\prime}_{22}}{Z^{\prime}_{12}}\right).

Clearing denominators, we find (
12
). A similar computation verifies (
13
). In summary, we see that
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}
.

In the next lemma we collect three basic observations that will be used repeatedly in what follows.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
act on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
.

(i)

If
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B^{\prime}}
is a morphism of
k
-algebras, then
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
acts on
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B^{\prime}}/\mathcal{Q}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B^{\prime}}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{S}
is a multiplicatively closed subset of
\mathcal{Q}
, then the action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
extends canonically to an action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{Q}
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a morphism of
\delta
-rings, then
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}/\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{Q}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor, then also the actions in
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
define differential torsors, provided that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{S}^{-1}\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
respectively is not the zero ring. ∎

For later use we record a lemma on differential torsors in the case
\mathcal{B}=k
and
\mathcal{Q}=k(x)
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
and let
R/k(x)
be a differential
G
-torsor. Then the intersection of all maximal
\delta
-ideals of
R
is zero.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
. For every
g\in G(k)
, also
g(\mathfrak{m})
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
. It thus suffices to show that the ideal
I=\bigcap_{g\in G(k)}g(\mathfrak{m})
of
R
is zero.
Clearly
g(I)\subseteq I
for every
g\in G(k)
. In terms of the coaction
\rho\colon R\to R\otimes_{k}k[G]
(as in Lemma
3.3
) this property translates to
\rho(I)\subseteq I\otimes_{k}k[G]
. But then the closed subscheme
W
of
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
defined by
I
is stable under the action of
G_{k(x)}
. Since
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
is a
G_{k(x)}
-torsor, this is only possible if
W=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
, i.e,
I=0
.
∎

To explain how differential torsors are obtained by “spreading out” a Picard-Vessiot ring into a family of differential rings we will need a basic lemma on linear algebraic groups.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
and let
k^{\prime}/k
be a field extension. If
\phi^{\prime}\colon G_{k^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
is a closed embedding of algebraic groups over
k^{\prime}
, then there exists a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
and a closed embedding
\phi\colon G_{\mathcal{B}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
of group schemes over
\mathcal{B}
such that
\phi_{k^{\prime}}=\phi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Writing
k^{\prime}
as the directed union of its finitely generated
k
-subalgebras, this follows from Theorem 8.8.2 and Theorem 8.10.5 (iv) in
[
Gro66
]
.
∎

The following simple example illustrates Lemma
3.9
.

Example 3.10
.

Let
k^{\prime}/k
be a field extension and
b\in k^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Consider the closed embedding
\phi^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{G}_{a,k^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2,k^{\prime}}
of algebraic groups over
k^{\prime}
(where
\mathbb{G}_{a,k^{\prime}}
denotes the additive group over
k^{\prime}
) given by

g\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&bg\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

(15)

For
\mathcal{B}=k[b]\subseteq k^{\prime}
, formula (
15
) defines a morphism
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{B}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2,\mathcal{B}}
of group schemes over
\mathcal{B}
that need not be a closed embedding, e.g., if
b
is transcendental over
k
, the fibre over
b=0
is not a closed embedding. However, for
\mathcal{B}=k[b,b^{-1}]\subseteq k^{\prime}
, formula (
15
) defines a closed embedding
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{B}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2,\mathcal{B}}
of group schemes over
\mathcal{B}
.

One can think of differential torsors as the kind of objects one obtains when “spreading out” a Picard-Vessiot ring into a nice family. This is formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
be an inclusion of algebraically closed fields and let
R/k^{\prime}(x)
be a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
with
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
and
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
such that
\delta(Y)=AY
. Then there exist

•

a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
,

•

a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
,

•

an affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
and

•

a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}

such that

•

A\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{n\times n}
,

•

\mathcal{R}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
and
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
with fundamental solution matrix
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
and
R^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple, where
K\subseteq k^{\prime}
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
and

•

R^{\operatorname{gen}}\otimes_{K(x)}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq R
via an isomorphism that identifies
\mathcal{Y}
with
Y
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{B}_{0}
is a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
is monic,
then
\mathcal{B}
can be chosen such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and
f
can be chosen such that
f_{0}
divides
f
in
\mathcal{B}[x]
. If, in addition,
f_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
is such that
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}^{n\times n}
, then
\mathcal{B}
can, in addition, be chosen such that
\mathcal{B}
is contained in the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
.

Furthermore, if the differential Galois group
G
of
R/k^{\prime}(x)
is of the form
G=G_{0,k^{\prime}}
for some algebraic group
G_{0}
over
k
, then
\mathcal{G}
can be chosen to be equal to
G_{0,\mathcal{B}}
.

Proof.

We consider the differential Galois group
G
of
R/k^{\prime}(x)
as a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
via the choice of
Y
. The contributions to
\mathcal{B}
and
f
came from three different sources. We go through them one by one:

(a)

Let
\mathcal{B}_{1}
be a finitely generated
k
-
subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
and
f_{1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]
a monic polynomial such that
A\in\mathcal{B}_{1}[x]_{f_{1}}^{n\times n}
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{1}[x]_{f_{1}}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is a
\delta
-subring of
R
.

(b)

Consider the ideal
I
of
k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
given by
I=\{p\in k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]|\ p(Y)=0\}
. Choose
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\in I
such that
I=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{r})
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{2}
be a finitely generated
k
-
subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
and let
f_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}[x]
be monic such that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}[x]_{f_{2}}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\subseteq k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}].

(c)

Let
\mathcal{B}_{3}
be a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}_{3}
a closed subgroup scheme of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}_{3}}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{3,k^{\prime}}=G
as closed subgroups of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
. By generic flatness (
[
Eis04
, Theorem 14.4]
), we may assume that
\mathcal{G}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}_{3}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{4}
be the
k
-subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
generated by
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}_{3}
. Furthermore, set
f_{4}=f_{1}f_{2}
and consider the finitely generated
\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}}
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]\subseteq R
. By generic flatness (
[
Eis04
, Theorem 14.4]
), there exists a nonzero (not necessarily monic)
h\in\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}}
such that
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}})_{h}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is flat over
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}})_{h}
. Write
h=\frac{h_{1}}{f_{4}^{m}}
with
h_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]
and
m\geq 1
.
With
h^{\prime}=f_{4}h_{1}\in\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]
we then have
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{f_{4}})_{h}=\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the
k
-subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
generated by
\mathcal{B}_{4}
and the inverse (in
k^{\prime}
) of the leading coefficient
b
of
h^{\prime}
.
In general, if
R\to S
is a flat ring map and
f\in R
, then
S_{f}=S\otimes_{R}R_{f}
is a flat
R_{f}
-module (
[
Sta24
,
Tag 00HI
]
). Therefore, since
\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is a flat
\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}}
-
module,
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}})_{b}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is a flat
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}})_{b}
-
module. For the monic polynomial
f=\frac{1}{b}h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}[x]

we have
(\mathcal{B}_{4}[x]_{h^{\prime}})_{b}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
and so
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is a flat
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-module.

We set
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{3,\mathcal{B}}
. Then
\mathcal{R}
is a
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebra and flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. Moreover, the canonical surjection
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\to R
is an isomorphism by item (b) above. Since
\mathcal{G}_{3}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}_{3}
, we see that
\mathcal{G}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{G}_{k^{\prime}}=(\mathcal{G}_{3,\mathcal{B}})_{k^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}_{3,k^{\prime}}=G
. In particular,
k^{\prime}(x)\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]=k^{\prime}(x)\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G]
.

The above flatness properties allow us to identify
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
with a
\delta
-subring of
R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[\mathcal{G}]
. In detail, since
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is a flat
\mathcal{B}
-algebra,
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is a flat
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-algebra and therefore
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}])
is a flat
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-algebra. This entails that the map
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\to(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}])\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)
is injective. But

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}])\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)=

\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x))\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}(k^{\prime}(x)\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}])=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}(k^{\prime}(x)\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G])=R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G].

So we can consider
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
as a subring of
R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G]
.

As explained in Section
3.1
, the closed embedding
G\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
corresponds to the morphism
k^{\prime}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\to k^{\prime}[G]=(R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}R)^{\delta},\ X\mapsto Z=Y^{-1}\otimes Y
. Moreover, the coaction
R\to R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G]
is determined by
Y\mapsto Y\otimes Z
. In summary, we see that the coaction
R\to R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G]
restricts to a map
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
. It is then clear that
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. Indeed, for a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
, every
g\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})\leq\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{T})
acts on
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
by
Y\otimes 1\mapsto Y\otimes g
.

To see that
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor, let us first show that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}R
is injective. Note that

(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)=(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x))\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x))=R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}R.

It thus suffices to show that the map
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)
is injective.
But this follows from the flatness of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}
, which in turn follows from the flatness of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to\mathcal{R}
.

We claim that the isomorphism

R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}R\to R\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G]
restricts to an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
. Injectivity being obvious, the surjectivity of
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
follows from
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]=\mathcal{B}[Z,\frac{1}{\det(Z)}]
, where
Z=Y^{-1}\otimes Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R\otimes_{k^{\prime}(x)}R)
, and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
maps
Y^{-1}\otimes Y
to
1\otimes Z
. Thus
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor. As
\delta(Y)=AY
and
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}]
, we see that, in fact,
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}=Y
.

As
(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x))\otimes_{K(x)}k^{\prime}(x)=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)=R
is
\delta
-simple, we see that also
R^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
must be
\delta
-simple. We have thus established the first claim of the lemma.

To address the second claim, let
K_{0}\subseteq k^{\prime}
be the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
. Note that there is no difficulty in arranging
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. For example, in the above construction of
\mathcal{B}
, we could choose
\mathcal{B}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Similarly, we can choose
f_{1}
so that
f_{0}
divides
f_{1}
.

Assume that
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}^{n\times n}
.
As
A\in K_{0}(x)^{n\times n}
, we may consider a Picard-Vessiot ring
R_{0}/K_{0}(x)
for
\delta(y)=Ay
. It then follows from Lemma
3.2
that
R_{0}\otimes_{K_{0}(x)}k^{\prime}(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
over
k^{\prime}(x)
. By the uniqueness of Picard-Vessiot rings, we thus have
R\simeq R_{0}\otimes_{K_{0}(x)}k^{\prime}(x)
.
We can now apply the first claim of the lemma to the inclusion
k\subseteq K_{0}
and the Picard-Vessiot ring
R_{0}/K_{0}(x)
. Then automatically
\mathcal{B}\subseteq K_{0}
.

Concerning the last claim of the lemma, note that, according to Lemma
3.9
, the closed embedding
G=G_{k^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
determined by
Y
spreads out to a closed embedding
G_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra of
k^{\prime}
. In point (c) above, we can thus choose
\mathcal{B}_{3}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{G}_{3}=G_{\mathcal{B}_{3}}
, so that
\mathcal{G}=G_{0,\mathcal{B}}
.

∎

3.3
Proto-Picard-Vessiot rings

For a linear algebraic group
G
, let
G^{t}
denote the intersection of all kernels of all characters of
G^{\circ}
, the identity component of
G
. The algebraic group
G^{t}
plays a fundamental role in the study of moduli of linear differential equations (
[
Sin93
]
) and in Hrushovski’s algorithm (
[
Hru02
]
). In regards to Hrushovski’s algorithm, the key point concerning
G^{t}
is the following. While the degree of defining equations of a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
cannot be bounded uniformly in
n
(consider, e.g., the groups
\mu_{m}
of
m
-th roots of unity in
\operatorname{GL}_{1}
), there exists a function
{\mathbf{d}}
of
n
such that for every closed subgroup
H
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
there exists a closed subgroup
G
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
defined by equations of degree at most
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
satisfying

G^{t}\leq H\leq G.

(16)

Given
H
, an algebraic group
G
satisfying (
16
) is called a
proto-Galois group
for
H
(
[
AMP22
, Def. 4.1]
).
In the first main step of Hrushovski’s algorithm a proto-Galois group for the differential Galois group is computed.

For our purpose, the mere existence of the function
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
is sufficient. To make Hrushovski’s algorithm practical (a goal that seems rather far out of reach), it is important to find small bounds. In
[
AMP22
]
it is shown that one can take
{\mathbf{d}}(n)=(4n)^{3n^{2}}
.

One can think of
G
as an “approximation” of
H
. This approximation is good in the sense that
G/G^{t}
is small. Indeed,
G^{t}
is normal in
G
and the identity component of
G/G^{t}
is a torus.
An alternative description of
G^{t}
is, as the closed subgroup of
G
generated by all unipotent elements of
G
.
In this section we introduce an analog of this notion for differential torsors: Proto-Picard-Vessiot ring. We think of them as “approximations” of Picard-Vessiot rings. We also establish a criterion that enables us to decide whether or not a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is a Picard-Vessiot ring.

In addition to the standing assumption of Section
3
that
F
is a differential field with field of constants
k=F^{\delta}
algebraically closed and of characteristic zero, throughout Section
3.3
we make the following assumptions:

•

G
is a linear algebraic group over
k
;

•

A\in F^{n\times n}
;

•

R/F
is a differential
G
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
with fundamental matrix
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
such that
R
is an integral domain;

•

F^{\prime}
is the integral closure of
F
in
R
;

•

E
is the field compositum of
\overline{F}
and the field of fractions of
R
(inside the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R
).

Note that if
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
, then
R/\mathfrak{m}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
. As we will now explain, the differential Galois group
H
of
R/\mathfrak{m}
is canonically a closed subgroup of
G
(cf. the proof of Proposition 1.15 in
[
BHHW18
]
).
The composition

R\otimes_{k}k[G]\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\otimes_{F}R\to R/\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{F}R/\mathfrak{m}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R/\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}k[H]

(17)

maps
k[G]
into
k[H]
, because the elements of
k[G]
are constant and the constants of
R/\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}k[H]
are just
k[H]
. Thus the surjection (
17
) is of the form
\pi\otimes\psi
, where
\pi\colon R\to R/\mathfrak{m}
is the canonical map and
\psi\colon k[G]\to k[H]
is a surjective map of
k
-algebras.
So
\psi
defines a closed embedding
H\to G
and we may identify
H
with a closed subscheme of
G
. We claim that

H(T)=\{g\in G(T)|\ g(\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T)=\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T\}

(18)

for every
k
-algebra
T
. In particular,
H
is a closed subgroup of
G
.

The commutative diagram

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{k}k[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi\otimes\psi}
\textstyle{R/\mathfrak{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R/\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}k[H]}

shows that
\rho(\mathfrak{m})\subseteq\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}k[G]+R\otimes_{k}\mathbb{I}(H)
, where
\mathbb{I}(H)=\ker(\psi)\subseteq k[G]
is the defining ideal of
H
. Therefore
g(\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T)\subseteq\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T
for
g\in H(T)
. Since also
g^{-1}\in H(T)
, we deduce that indeed
g(\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T)=\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T
.

Conversely, if
g\in G(T)
satisfies
g(\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T)=\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T
, then
g
induces a differential automorphism of
(R\otimes_{k}T)/(\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T)=R/\mathfrak{m}\otimes_{k}T
and thus belongs to
H(T)
, by definition of
H
.
This proves (
18
).

Now let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be another maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
. Then
R/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
F
with differential Galois group
H^{\prime}
canonically contained in
G
. We will show that
G^{t}\leq H
if and only if
G^{t}\leq H^{\prime}
.

As a first step, let us show that there exists a
g\in G(k)
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}=g(\mathfrak{m})
. Consider the morphism

k[G]\to R\otimes_{k}k[G]\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\otimes_{F}R\to R/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\otimes_{F}R/\mathfrak{m}\to S,

(19)

where
S
is a quotient of
R/\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\otimes_{F}R/\mathfrak{m}
by a maximal
\delta
-ideal. As
S
is
\delta
-simple and finitely generated over
F
, we have
S^{\delta}=k
(
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 1.17]
). Thus (
19
) maps
k[G]
into
k
and therefore defines a point
g\in G(k)
. By construction,
g(\mathfrak{m})\subseteq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
.
Since
g(\mathfrak{m})
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
, we obtain
g(\mathfrak{m})=\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
as claimed.

From (
18
), we thus deduce that
H^{\prime}=gHg^{-1}
. As
G^{t}
is normal
G
, we see that
G^{t}\leq H
if and only if
G^{t}\leq H^{\prime}
.

In summary, if
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
are maximal
\delta
-ideals in
R
, yielding differential Galois groups
H\leq G
and
H^{\prime}\leq G
respectively, then
G^{t}\leq H
if and only if
G^{t}\leq H^{\prime}
. So we can use this property to define proto-Picard-Vessiot rings.

Definition 3.12
.

The differential torsor
R/F
is
proto-Picard-Vessiot
if
for one (equivalently every) maximal
\delta
-ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
we have
G^{t}\leq H
, where
H
is the differential Galois group of
R/\mathfrak{m}
.

Clearly,
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot if
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot. Our next goal is to find a criterion to test if a differential torsor is proto-Picard-Vessiot. In the next defintion and lemma
k^{\prime}
is a field of characteristic zero.
Following
[
Fen15
]
and
[
AMP22
, Def. 2.3]
we make the below definition.

Definition 3.13
.

A geometrically reduced closed subscheme
Z
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
is
bounded
by a positive integer
d
, if there exist polynomials
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in k^{\prime}[X]
of degree at most
d
such that

Z(\overline{k^{\prime}})=\{g\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{k^{\prime}})|\ p_{1}(g)=\ldots=p_{m}(g)=0\}.

(20)

Note that, by Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz, (
20
) is equivalent to
\mathbb{I}(Z)=\sqrt{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})}
, where
\mathbb{I}(Z)\subseteq k^{\prime}[X,\frac{1}{\det{X}}]
is the defining ideal of
Z
and
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
is the ideal of
k^{\prime}[X,\frac{1}{\det{X}}]
generated by
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
G
be a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
and
Z
a geometrically reduced closed subscheme of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k^{\prime}}
such that
Z
is a
G
-torsor under right multiplication. If
G
is bounded by
d
, then also
Z
is bounded by
d
.

Proof.

A point
z\in Z(\overline{k^{\prime}})
defines an isomorphism
G_{\overline{k^{\prime}}}\to Z_{\overline{k^{\prime}}},\ g\mapsto zg
of schemes over
\overline{k^{\prime}}
.
If
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in k^{\prime}[X]
are such that

G(\overline{k^{\prime}})=\{g\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{k^{\prime}})|\ p_{1}(g)=\ldots=p_{m}(g)=0\},

then

Z(\overline{k^{\prime}})=\{g\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{k^{\prime}})|\ p_{1}(z^{-1}g)=\ldots=p_{m}(z^{-1}g)=0\}.

So
\mathbb{I}(Z_{\overline{k^{\prime}}})=\sqrt{(p_{1}(z^{-1}X),\ldots,p_{m}(z^{-1}X))}\subseteq\overline{k^{\prime}}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

We have
\mathbb{I}(Z_{\overline{k^{\prime}}})=\mathbb{I}(Z)\otimes_{k^{\prime}}\overline{k^{\prime}}
and so, if
p_{i}(z^{-1}X)\in\overline{k^{\prime}}[X]
has degree at most
d
, i.e,
p_{i}
has degree at most
d
, then
p_{i}(z^{-1}X)
can be written as a
\overline{k^{\prime}}
-linear combination of polynomials
q_{i,j}\in k^{\prime}[X]\cap\mathbb{I}(Z)
of degree at most
d
. The set of solutions of all
q_{ij}
’s in
\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{k^{\prime}})
is
Z(\overline{k^{\prime}})
. Therefore
Z
is bounded by
d
.
∎

As above, set

{\mathbf{d}}(n)=(4n)^{3n^{2}}.

The following lemma allows us to test if a differential torsor is proto-Picard-Vesssiot. Roughly speaking, it shows that
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot if
R
encodes the algebraic relations among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix up to degree
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
. If
p(Y)=0
for every
p\in F[X]
with
\deg(p)\leq{\mathbf{d}}(n)
and
p(Y)\in\mathfrak{m}
, then
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot.

Proof.

Let
H\leq G
be the differential Galois group of
R/\mathfrak{m}
and consider
H
as a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k}
via the fundamental solution matrix
\overline{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R/\mathfrak{m})
, the image of
Y
in
R/\mathfrak{m}
. Due to Corollary 4.1 of
[
AMP22
]
, there exists a closed subgroup
\tilde{G}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k}
bounded by
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
such that

\tilde{G}^{t}\leq H\leq\tilde{G}.

Let
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
be the maximal
\delta
-ideal of
F[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
whose image in
R
is
\mathfrak{m}
and let
Z^{\prime}
be the closed subscheme of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,F}
defined by
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
. The image
\tilde{Z}
of
Z^{\prime}\times\tilde{G}_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,F},(z,g)\mapsto zg
is a
\tilde{G}_{F}
-torsor. Indeed, for a point
z\in Z^{\prime}(\overline{F})
we have

\tilde{Z}(\overline{F})=Z^{\prime}(\overline{F})\tilde{G}(\overline{F})=zH(\overline{F})\tilde{G}(\overline{F})=z\tilde{G}(\overline{F}).

We know from Lemma
3.14
that
\tilde{Z}
is bounded by
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
. Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in F[X]
be polynomials of degree at most
{\mathbf{d}}(n)
such that
\mathbb{I}(\tilde{Z})=\sqrt{(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})}
. As

Z^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{Z}
, we have
\mathbb{I}(\tilde{Z})\subseteq\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
. Therefore
p_{1}(Y),\ldots,p_{m}(Y)\in\mathfrak{m}
. By assumption, the image of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
in
R
is zero. Since
R
is reduced, we see that in fact the image of
\mathbb{I}(\tilde{Z})
in
R
is zero. Geometrically, this means that
Z\subseteq\tilde{Z}
, where
Z\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{n,F}
is the closed subscheme defined by
\mathbb{I}(Z)=\{p\in F[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]|\ p(Y)=0\}
. As
Z
is a
G_{F}
-torsor and
\tilde{Z}
is a
\tilde{G}_{F}
-torsor (both under right multiplication), we must have
G\leq\tilde{G}
. Therefore,
G^{t}\leq\tilde{G}^{t}\leq H
and
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot.
∎

We now work towards a criterion to decided if a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is Picard-Vessiot.
Recall that
G^{\circ}
denotes the identity component of the algebraic group
G
and that
F^{\prime}
is the integral closure of
F
in
R
.

Lemma 3.16
.

The
F^{\prime}
-
\delta
-algebra
R/F^{\prime}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
.

Proof.

As in
[
Wat79
, Section 6.5]
, for a
k^{\prime}
-algebra
S
over a field
k^{\prime}
, let
\pi_{0}(S/k^{\prime})
denote the union of all étale
k^{\prime}
-subalgebras of
S
. In particular,
\pi_{0}(R/F)=F^{\prime}
and
\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)=k[G/G^{\circ}]
. In fact, the defining ideal
\mathbb{I}(G^{\circ})\subseteq k[G]
of
G^{\circ}
is the ideal of
k[G]
generated by
\ker(\varepsilon)\cap\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
, where
\varepsilon\colon k[G]\to k
is the counit (
[
Wat79
, Section 6.7]
).

In the diagram

\textstyle{R\otimes_{F}R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{k}k[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}R}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{k}k[G^{\circ}]}

(21)

the kernel of the left vertical map is
(f\otimes 1-1\otimes f|\ f\in F^{\prime})\subseteq R\otimes_{F}R
. The kernel of the right vertical map is
R\otimes_{k}\mathbb{I}(G^{\circ})
, the ideal of
R\otimes_{k}k[G]
generated by
\ker(\varepsilon)\cap\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
. We claim that these two kernels correspond to each other under the isomorphism.

The diagram

\textstyle{F^{\prime}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}\cdot\varepsilon}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}}

where the top isomorphism is obtained by applying
\pi_{0}
to
R\otimes_{F}R\simeq R\otimes_{k}k[G]
, commutes. The kernel of the left map is
(f\otimes 1-1\otimes f|\ f\in F^{\prime})\subseteq F^{\prime}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime}
. The kernel of the right map is
F^{\prime}\otimes_{k}(\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)\cap\ker(\varepsilon))
. This shows that the two kernels in (
21
) correspond to each other. We thus have an induced isomorphism
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}R\simeq R\otimes_{k}k[G^{\circ}]
, showing that
R/F^{\prime}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor.
∎

Let
\overline{F}R
denote the ring compositum of
\overline{F}
and
R
(inside the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R
). In other words,
\overline{F}R
is the
\overline{F}
-algebra generated by
R
.

Corollary 3.17
.

We have
\overline{F}R=R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
and
\overline{F}R/\overline{F}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor.

Proof.

Since
R/F^{\prime}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor (Lemma
3.16
), it follows from Lemma
3.7
that
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}/\overline{F}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor. As
G^{\circ}
is connected, this implies that
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
is an integral domain, i.e.,
R
is a regular
F^{\prime}
-algebra. This implies (
[
Bou90
, Chapter V, §17, Sections 4 and 5 ]
that
F^{\prime}
is relatively algebraically closed in the field of fractions
F^{\prime}(R)
of
R
and that
F^{\prime}(R)\otimes_{F}\overline{F}
is a field. Thus the canonical map
F^{\prime}(R)\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}\to E
is injective (in fact bijective) and so also
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}\to\overline{F}R
is injective.
∎

Following
[
Gro67
, Def. 4.5.2]
, for a field
k^{\prime}
and a
k^{\prime}
-algebra
R^{\prime}
, the number of irreducible components of
\operatorname{Spec}(R^{\prime}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}\overline{k^{\prime}})
is called the
geometric number of irreducible components of
R^{\prime}/k^{\prime}
.

Corollary 3.18
.

The geometric number of irreducible components of
R/F
is
[F^{\prime}:F]
.

Proof.

Because
R/F
is a differential
G
-torsor,
R\otimes_{F}{\overline{F}}
is isomorphic to
\overline{F}[G_{\overline{F}}]
(as an
\overline{F}
-algebra). The number of irreducible components of
G_{\overline{F}}
is the same as the number of connected components of
G_{\overline{F}}
and this is the same as the number of connected components of
G
. The latter is the same as the dimension
\dim_{k}\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
of
\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
as a
k
-vector space. As observed in the proof of Lemma
3.16
, we have
F^{\prime}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\otimes_{k}\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
. So
\dim_{k}\pi_{0}(k[G]/k)
must equal
[F^{\prime}:F]
.
∎

Recall that
E
denotes the field compositum of
\overline{F}
and the field of fractions of
R
(inside the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R
). The idea for the criterion to decide if a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot is to first Gauge transform the equation
\delta(y)=Ay
with fundamental sulution matrix
Y
to an equation
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
(where
A^{\prime}=BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1}
) with fundamental matrix
BY
via a matrix
B\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{F})
such that
BY\in G^{\circ}(E)
and then apply characters of
G^{\circ}
to
BY
. The following corollary shows that we can always find such a transformation matrix
B
.

Corollary 3.19
.

For
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
and
B=\vartheta(Y)^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{F})
we have
BY\in G^{\circ}(E)
.

Proof.

Set
Z=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
, considered as a scheme over
F^{\prime}
. As
R/F^{\prime}
is a
G^{\circ}
-torsor by Lemma
3.16
, the morphism

Z\times_{F^{\prime}}G^{\circ}_{F^{\prime}}\to Z\times_{F^{\prime}}Z,\ (z,g)\mapsto(z,zg)

(22)

is an isomorphism. If we consider
Z
and
G^{\circ}_{F^{\prime}}
as closed subschemes of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,F^{\prime}}
via
Y
, the inverse of (
22
) is given by
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(z_{1},z_{1}^{-1}z_{2})
. In particular, for any field extension
\widetilde{F}
of
F^{\prime}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in Z(\widetilde{F})
, we have
z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}\in G^{\circ}(\widetilde{F})
.
With
\widetilde{F}=E
,
z_{1}=\vartheta(Y)
and
z_{2}=Y
, this implies the claim.
∎

We note that there is a fixed embedding of
F^{\prime}
into
\overline{F}
derived from forming the algebraic closure of
F
inside the the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
has to be interpreted accordingly.

We denote with
I_{n}
the
n\times n
-identity matrix.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
\chi
be a character of
G^{\circ}
,
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
and
B=\vartheta(Y)^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{F})
. Then

\delta(\chi(BY))=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})(BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}\right)\chi(BY).

Proof.

We will work with the ring
E[\varepsilon]
of dual numbers, i.e.,
\varepsilon^{2}=0
. For
p\in F[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
,
C\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(E)
and
D\in E^{n\times n}
(i.e.,
C+\varepsilon D\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(E[\varepsilon])
,
we then have
p(C+\varepsilon D)=p(C)+\varepsilon\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial p}{\partial X_{ij}}(C)D_{ij}
. Therefore

p(C+\varepsilon\delta(C))=p(C)+\varepsilon\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial p}{\partial X_{ij}}(C)\delta(C_{ij})=p(C)+\varepsilon(\delta(p(C))).

(23)

For
Y^{\prime}=BY\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(E)
and
A^{\prime}=BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1}\in\overline{F}^{n\times n}
we have
\delta(Y^{\prime})=A^{\prime}Y^{\prime}
.
As
Y^{\prime}\in G^{\circ}(E)
(Corollary
3.19
) it follows from (
23
) that
Y^{\prime}+\varepsilon\delta(Y^{\prime})\in G^{\circ}(E[\varepsilon])
. We have

\chi(Y^{\prime}+\varepsilon\delta(Y^{\prime}))\chi(Y^{\prime-1})=(\chi(Y^{\prime})+\varepsilon\delta(\chi(Y^{\prime})))\chi(Y^{\prime-1})=1+\varepsilon\delta(\chi(Y^{\prime}))\chi(Y^{\prime})^{-1}.

On the other hand,

\chi(Y^{\prime}+\varepsilon\delta(Y^{\prime}))\chi(Y^{\prime-1})=\chi((Y^{\prime}+\varepsilon\delta(Y^{\prime}))Y^{\prime-1})=\chi(I_{n}+\varepsilon A^{\prime})=1+\varepsilon\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})A^{\prime}_{ij}.

Thus

\delta(\chi(Y^{\prime}))=\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})A^{\prime}_{ij}\right)\chi(Y^{\prime})

as claimed.
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.20
shows that
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})(BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}\in\overline{F}
does not depend on the lift of
\chi\in k[G^{\circ}]
to an element of
k[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

Our criterion that characterizes Picard-Vessiot rings among proto-Picard-Vessiot rings is based on the concept of
logarithmic independence
that we now define.

Definition 3.21
.

Elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
of a differential field
F
are
logarithmically independent
(over
F
) if
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}=\delta(f)/f
for
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f\in F^{\times}
implies
d_{1}=\dots=d_{m}=0
.

The significance of the above definition is the following. Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}
be nonzero elements of some
\delta
-field extension of
F
with no new constants such that
\delta(h_{i})=f_{i}h_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
. By the Kolchin-Ostrowski theorem (
[
Kol68
]
), if
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}
are algebraically dependent over
F
, then there exist
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
not all equal to zero such that
h_{1}^{d_{1}}\ldots h_{m}^{d_{m}}=f\in F
.
Applying the logarithmic derivative
h\mapsto\frac{\delta(h)}{h}
to this equation yields
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}=\delta(f)/f
.

On the other hand, if
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}=\delta(f)/f
holds, the logarithmic derivative of
\frac{h_{1}^{d_{1}}\ldots h_{m}^{d_{m}}}{f}
is zero. So
\frac{h_{1}^{d_{1}}\ldots h_{m}^{d_{m}}}{f}
is a constant
\lambda
and
h_{1}^{d_{1}}\ldots h_{m}^{d_{m}}=f\lambda\in F
. In summary, we see that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{m}
are algebraically independent over
F
if and only if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent.

Note that if
F\subseteq\widetilde{F}
is an inclusion of differential fields, then
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
may be logarithmically independent over
F
but logarithmically dependent over
\widetilde{F}
. However, from the above discussion we deduce the following.

Remark 3.22
.

If
\widetilde{F}/F
is an algebraic extension of differential fields and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
, then
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
F
if and only if
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
\widetilde{F}
.

The following lemma provides a criterion to test if a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is Picard-Vessiot. We denote with
X(G^{\circ})
the group of characters of
G^{\circ}
.

Lemma 3.23
.

Assume that
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot and
let
\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{m}
be a basis of
X(G^{\circ})
(as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module). Choose
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
and set
B=\vartheta(Y)^{-1}
and

f_{\ell}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\chi_{\ell}}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})(BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}\in\overline{F}.

for
\ell=1,\ldots,m
. Then
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot if and only if
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
\overline{F}
.

Proof.

Suppose
R/F
is not Picard-Vessiot, i.e.,
R
is not
\delta
-simple. We will show that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically dependent. As a non-trivial
\delta
-ideal of
R
, gives rise to a non-trivial
\delta
-ideal of
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
,
this implies that also
R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
is not
\delta
-simple.

From Corollary
3.17
we know that
\overline{F}R=R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
is a differential
G^{\circ}
-torsor over
\overline{F}
. Let
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
be a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
\overline{F}R=R\otimes_{F^{\prime}}\overline{F}
and set
\mathfrak{m}=\overline{\mathfrak{m}}\cap R
. Then
(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
over
\overline{F}
and
R/\mathfrak{m}
embeds into
(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
