. This implies that the field of fractions of
R/\mathfrak{m}
does not have new constants. So
R/\mathfrak{m}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring (over
F
) for
\delta(y)=Ay
and
\mathfrak{m}
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R
. Let
H
be the differential Galois group of
R/\mathfrak{m}
.
As
R/F
is proto-Picard-Vessiot, we have
G^{t}\leq H\leq G
.

The ring compositum
\overline{F}(R/\mathfrak{m})
of
\overline{F}
and
R/\mathfrak{m}
(inside the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R/\mathfrak{m})
can be identified with
(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
. In fact, using Corollary
3.17
(applied to
R/\mathfrak{m}
), we get a map
\overline{F}(R/\mathfrak{m})\to(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
which then necessarily is an isomorphism. By Corollary
3.17
, the differential Galois group of
\overline{F}(R/\mathfrak{m})=(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
is
H^{\circ}
.

As
\mathfrak{m}
is a nonzero prime ideal of
R
and
R
is an integral domain, we see that

\dim(H)=\dim(R/\mathfrak{m})<\dim(R)=\dim(G).

Therefore
H^{\circ}
is a proper subgroup of
G^{\circ}
.
As the identity component
G^{\circ}/G^{t}
of
G/G^{t}
is a torus and
H^{\circ}/G^{t}
is a proper subgroup of
G^{\circ}/G^{t}
, there exists a non-trivial character of
G^{\circ}/G^{t}
that is trivial on
H^{\circ}/G^{t}
, i.e., there exists a non-trivial character
\chi
of
G^{\circ}
such that
H^{\circ}\subseteq\ker(\chi)
.

Let
\overline{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R/\mathfrak{m})\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{n}((\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
denote the image of
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
and set
Y^{\prime}=B\overline{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{F}(R/\mathfrak{m}))=\operatorname{GL}_{n}((\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
. As
BY\in G^{\circ}(\overline{F}R)
(Corollary
3.19
), we have
Y^{\prime}\in G^{\circ}((\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}})
.
For every
h\in H^{\circ}(k)
we can write
h(Y^{\prime})=Y^{\prime}[h]
with
[h]\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(k)
. Indeed, as explained in Section
3.1
, we may identify
H^{\circ}
with a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k}
. Then

h(\chi(Y^{\prime}))=\chi(Y^{\prime}[h])=\chi(Y^{\prime})\chi([h])=\chi(Y^{\prime})

for every
h\in H^{\circ}(k)
.

Thus
\chi(Y^{\prime})=f\in\overline{F}\setminus\{0\}
by the differential Galois correspondence. Write
\chi=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{i}^{d_{i}}
with
d_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then not all
d_{i}
are zero, because
\chi
is non-trivial.
By Lemma
3.20
, we have
\delta(\chi_{i}(BY))=f_{i}\chi_{i}(BY)
for
i=1,\ldots,m
. This is an identity in
\overline{F}R
. Taking the quotient mod
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}
yields
\delta(\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime}))=f_{i}\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime})
in
(\overline{F}R)/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}=\overline{F}(R/\mathfrak{m})
. So

\frac{\delta(f)}{f}=\frac{\delta(\chi(Y^{\prime}))}{\chi(Y^{\prime})}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\frac{\delta(\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime}))}{\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime})}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}.

Thus
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically dependent over
\overline{F}
.

Conversely, assume that
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot. Then
G
is the differential Galois group of
R/F
and
E/\overline{F}
is a Picard-Vessiot extension with differential Galois group
G^{\circ}
. Suppose

f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically dependent, i.e., there are
d_{1},\dots,d_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
not all zero and
f\in\overline{F}\setminus\{0\}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}=\delta(f)/f
. Write
\chi=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{i}^{d_{i}}
. Then
\chi
is a non-trivial character of
G^{\circ}
.
For
Y^{\prime}=BY
, using Lemma
3.20
, we obtain

\frac{\delta(\chi(Y^{\prime}))}{\chi(Y^{\prime})}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\frac{\delta(\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime}))}{\chi_{i}(Y^{\prime})}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}=\frac{\delta(f)}{f}.

Thus
\delta(\frac{\chi(Y^{\prime})}{f})=0
, i.e.,
\frac{\chi(Y^{\prime})}{f}=\lambda\in k
. So
\chi(Y^{\prime})=\lambda f\in\overline{F}
.

For every
g\in G^{\circ}(k)
we can write
g(Y^{\prime})=Y^{\prime}[g]
for some
[g]\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(k)
, thereby identifying
G^{\circ}
with a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,k}
. Then

\chi(Y^{\prime})=g(\chi(Y^{\prime}))=\chi(g(Y^{\prime}))=\chi(Y^{\prime}[g])=\chi(Y^{\prime})\chi([g]),

for all
g\in G^{\circ}(k)
. This implies that
\chi
is trivial; a contradiction. Therefore,
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
\overline{F}
.
∎

Note that in case
X(G^{\circ})
is trivial, Lemma
3.23
asserts that a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is automatically Picard-Vessiot.

Remark 3.24
.

If
F=k(x)
and
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot with connected differential Galois group
G
, then the
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
in Lemma
3.23
can be chosen such that
B=\vartheta(Y)^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F)
and therefore also
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
.

Proof.

By Tsen’s theorem the field
F=k(x)
is a
C_{1}
-field and so by Steinberg’s theorem every torsor for a connected linear algebraic group over
F
is trivial (
[
Ser97a
, Chapter III, Section 2.3]
). As
R
defines a torsor for
G_{F}
, we see that there exists a morphisms
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F}(R,F)
. Because
G
is connected, the differential Galois correspondence implies that the integral closure
F^{\prime}
of
F
in
R
is equal to
F
. So
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
and
\vartheta(Y)^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(F)
as desired.
∎

The following example illustrates Lemma
3.23

Example 3.25
.

Let
a,b\in F
such that
a\in F^{\times}
is not a square in
F
. Then
F(\eta)=F[y]/(y^{2}-a)
is a differential field extension of
F
.
As in Example
3.6
we set

R=F(\eta)[y_{1},y_{2},y_{1}^{-1},y_{2}^{-1}]=F[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})=F[Y,\tfrac{1}{\det(Y)}],

where
\delta(y_{1})=(b+\eta)y_{1}
,
\delta(y_{2})=(b-\eta)y_{2}
,

p_{1}=X_{21}X_{22}-(b^{2}-a)X_{11}X_{12},\quad\quad p_{2}=X_{21}X_{12}+X_{22}X_{11}-2bX_{11}X_{12,}

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
a+\delta(b)-b^{2}-\frac{\delta(a)b}{2a}&2b+\frac{\delta(a)}{2a}\end{pmatrix}\in F^{2\times 2},

and
\delta(X)=AX
. Then
R
is an integral domain and
R/F
is a differential
G
-torsor for
\delta(y)=Ay
, where
G\leq\operatorname{GL}_{2,k}
is the group of
2\times 2
monomial matrices (as seen in Example
3.6
). As
G^{t}=1
, it is clear that
R/F
is proto-Picard Vessiot.

The algebraic group
G^{\circ}
is the diagonal torus in
\operatorname{GL}_{2,k}
and so a basis
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
of
X(G^{\circ})
is represented by
X_{11}
and
X_{22}
.
The integral closure
F^{\prime}
of
F
in
R
equals
F^{\prime}=F(\eta)
. Define
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{F^{\prime}}(R,\overline{F})
by
\vartheta(y_{1})=\vartheta(y_{2})=1
. Then

\vartheta(Y)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
b+\eta&b-\eta\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{F})\quad\text{ and }\quad\vartheta(Y)\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=Y.

We have

\delta\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}b+\eta&0\\
0&b-\eta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }\delta(Y)=AY.

(24)

Because
\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}&0\\
0&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=BY
for
B=\vartheta(Y)^{-1}
, the matrices defining the differential equations in (
24
) are related via the Gauge transformation defined by
B
, i.e.,
BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}b+\eta&0\\
0&b-\eta\end{pmatrix}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle f_{1}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial\chi_{1}}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{2})(BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}=b+\eta\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle f_{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\frac{\partial\chi_{2}}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{2})(BAB^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}=b-\eta.

So Lemma
3.23
shows that
R/F
is Picard-Vessiot if and only if
b+\eta
and
b-\eta
are logarithmically independent over
F(\eta)
.

3.4
Logarithmic independence via residues

In the previous section (Lemma
3.23
), we have seen that whether or not a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is Picard-Vessiot, depends on the logarithmic (in)dependence of certain algebraic functions.
The point of this short section is to present a criterion from
[
CS99
]
for the logarithmic independence of algebraic functions via differential forms and their residues.
In Section
4
we will then show that this criterion is preserved under many specializations.

Let
F
be a finite field extension of
k(x)
, i.e.,
F/k
is a function field of one variable.
Recall (
[
Sti09
, Def. 4.2.10]
) that the
residue

\operatorname{res}_{P}(\omega)
of a differential form
\omega
of
F/k
at a place
P
of
F/k
is defined as

\operatorname{res}_{P}(\omega)=\lambda_{-1}\in k
, where
\omega=f\,dt
with
t\in F
a uniformizing variable at
P
and
f=\sum_{i=\ell}^{\infty}\lambda_{i}t^{i}
the
P
-adic expansion of
f\in F
. In other words,
\operatorname{res}_{P}(\omega)=\operatorname{res}_{P,t}(f)
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
and set
{\mathbf{f}}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})
. Let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the (finite) set of places of
F/k
consisting of all poles of the differential forms
f_{1}dx,\ldots,f_{m}dx
.
Define

\displaystyle Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle=\left\{(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\ \bigg|\ \sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}\ \forall\,P\in{\mathcal{P}}\,\mbox{and}\,\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)=0\right\},

\displaystyle Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})

\displaystyle=\left\{(d_{1},\dots,d_{m})\in Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})\ \big|\ \mbox{$\sum_{{P}\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\right){P}$ is a principal divisor of $F/k$}\right\}.

Note that
Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
are submodules of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and thus are free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules. Let
\{(e_{1,\ell},\dots,e_{m,\ell})\mid\ell=1,\dots,n\}
be a basis of
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
write

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\right){P}=(h_{\ell})\ \text{ with }\ h_{\ell}\in F^{\times}.

For
\ell=1,\dots,n
, set

\omega_{\ell}=\left(\frac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}\right)dx.

Lemma 3.26
.

The elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
F
if and only if
\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{n}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent.
In particular, if
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})=0
, then
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
F
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is contained in the proof of
[
CS99
, Prop. 2.4]
. For the convenience of the reader we include the details.

First assume that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
F
. Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}=0
. By definition of
\omega_{\ell}
, this implies
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell}\frac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}
. For
h=\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{d_{\ell}}\in F^{\times}
, we thus have

\tfrac{\delta(h)}{h}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell}\tfrac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}\right)f_{i}.

The logarithmic independence of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
implies that
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}d_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}=0
for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
The
\mathbb{Z}
-linear independence of the
(e_{1,\ell},\dots,e_{m,\ell})
’s thus yields
d_{1}=\ldots=d_{n}=0
as desired.

Conversely, assume that
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{n}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent. Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
h\in F^{\times}
be such that
\frac{\delta(h)}{h}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}
. We will show that
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
. First note that the differential form
\frac{\delta(h)}{h}dx=\frac{dh}{h}
has only simple poles and the residues are all integers. In fact, the residue at a place
P
of
F/K
is the
P
-valuation of
h
. (This can, for example, be deduced from
[
Sti09
, Prop. 4.2.7]
.)
So
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)=\operatorname{res}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}dx)=\operatorname{res}_{P}(\frac{dh}{h})\in\mathbb{Z}
for every place
P
of
F/K
and

(h)=\sum_{P}\operatorname{res}_{P}(\tfrac{dh}{h}){P}=\sum_{P}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx){P},

where the sum is taken over all places of
F/K
. However, since
\operatorname{res}_{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}dx)=0
, unless
P
is a pole of one of the forms
f_{i}dx
, the sum can be restricted to
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. As principal divisors have degree zero (
[
Sti09
, Theorem 1.4.11]
), we see that
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and also
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
. Therefore, there exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
d_{i}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}-\Big(\tfrac{\delta(h)}{h}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}f_{i}\Big)dx=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}\Big(\tfrac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}\Big)dx-\tfrac{\delta(h)}{h}dx+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}dx=

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta(h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}})}{h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}}}dx=\frac{d(h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}})}{h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}}}.

By construction, the differential forms
\omega_{\ell}
have no simple poles; so
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}
has no simple pole. On the other hand,
\frac{d(h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}})}{h^{-1}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}^{c_{\ell}}}
has only simple poles. This is only possible if
\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}c_{\ell}\omega_{\ell}=0
.

Now the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear independence of the
\omega_{\ell}
’s yields that all
c_{\ell}
’s are zero. Hence also all
d_{i}
’s are zero and the
f_{i}
’s are logarithmically independent as desired.
∎

The following example illustrates Lemma
3.26

Example 3.27
.

Let
F=k(x,z)
with
z^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha
and
\alpha\in k
.
We assume that the discriminant
256\alpha^{3}-27
of
x^{4}+x+\alpha
is nonzero so that
x^{4}+x+\alpha
has four distinct roots
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}
in
k
.
Let
\beta\in k
with
\beta^{4}+\beta+\alpha\neq 0
. Set
b=\frac{(x-\beta)^{2}}{x^{4}+x+\alpha}=(\frac{x-\beta}{z})^{2}
and
\eta=\frac{x-\beta}{z}
. We will investigate the logarithmic independence of
f_{1}=b+\eta
and
f_{2}=b-\eta
.
(This choice of
f_{1},f_{2}
is motivated by Example
3.25
.)

We first need to find the set
{\mathcal{P}}
of poles of
f_{1}dx
and
f_{2}dx
. Note that the projective curve
C^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}_{k}
defined by the affine equation
z^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha
has a singularity at infinity.

The transformation

x=-\tfrac{v+1}{2u},\quad z=(\tfrac{v+1}{2u})^{2}-\tfrac{u}{2}

yields a smooth model
C\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{k}^{2}
defined by the affine equation
v^{2}=u^{3}-4\alpha u+1
. The inverse transformation is given by

u=2(x^{2}-z),\quad v=-4(x^{2}-z)x-1.

These transformations yield an isomorphism between
C\smallsetminus\{(0:1:1),(0:1:0)\}
and
C^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{(0:1:0)\}
. The singular point
(0:1:0)
of
C^{\prime}
has two preimages in
C
, namely
(0:1:1)
and
(0:1:0)
.
The places of
F/k
are in one-to-one correspondence with
C(k)
.
The inclusion
k(x)\subseteq F
of function fields corresponds to the morphism

C\to\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k},\ (u:v:w)\mapsto(v+w:-2u).

By the Hurwitz genus formula (
[
Sti09
, Cor. 3.5.6]
), this morphism has exactly four ramification points. They are
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}\in\mathbb{A}^{1}(k)\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
. Let
P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
denote the (unique) places of
F/k
extending the places of
k(x)
corresponding to
\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4},\alpha_{5},\alpha_{6}
. The point of
C(k)
corresponding to
P_{i}
is
(2\alpha_{i}^{2}:-4\alpha_{i}^{3}-1:1)
for
i=3,\ldots,6
.

For a place
P
of
F/k
let
\nu_{P}
denote the corresponding valuation on
F
. Similarly, every
\lambda\in k\cup\{\infty\}
defines a valuation
\nu_{\lambda}
on
k(x)
.
For
a\in k(x)
we have
\nu_{P_{i}}(a)=2\nu_{\alpha_{i}}(a)
for
i=3,\ldots,6
. For any other place
P
of
F/k
(i.e.,
P\notin\{P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}\}
) we have
\nu_{P}(a)=\nu_{\lambda}(a)
, where
\lambda
corresponds to the restriction of
P
. With this preparation, one sees immediately that the poles of
b=\frac{(x-\beta)^{2}}{x^{4}+x+\alpha}
are
P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
and
\nu_{P_{i}}(b)=-2
for
i=3,\ldots,6
.
Because
b=\eta^{2}
, it follows that the poles of
\eta
are
P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
and
\nu_{P_{i}}(\eta)=-1
for
i=3,\ldots,6
.

There are two places
P_{1},P_{2}
of
F/k
that restrict to the infinite place of
k(x)
. They correspond to the points
(0:1:0)
and
(0:1:1)
of
C(k)
. Then the poles of
x
are
P_{1},P_{2}
and
\nu_{P_{i}}(x)=-1
for
i=1,2
.

Set
{\mathcal{P}}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{6}\}
. Then the poles of
bdx
and
\eta dx
belong to
{\mathcal{P}}
, because outside of
{\mathcal{P}}
neither
b,\eta
or
x
has a singularity. As
\nu_{P_{i}}(\frac{1}{x})=1
for
i=1,2
we can use
x^{-1}
as a uniformizer at
P_{1},P_{2}
. Then
bdx=-bx^{2}d(x^{-1})
and so
\nu_{P_{i}}(bdx)=\nu_{P_{i}}(-bx^{2})=\nu_{\infty}(-bx^{2})=0
. Hence
P_{1},P_{2}
are not poles of
bdx
.

As

\nu_{P_{i}}(z^{2})=\nu_{P_{i}}(x^{4}+x+\alpha)=2\nu_{\alpha_{i}}(x^{4}+x+\alpha)=2,

for
i=3,\ldots,6
, we see that
z
is a uniformizer at
P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
.
From
z^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha
it follows that
\delta(z)=\frac{4x^{3}+1}{2z}
and so

bdx=\tfrac{(x-\beta)^{2}2z}{z^{2}(4x^{3}+1)}dz=\tfrac{2(x-\beta)^{2}}{(4x^{3}+1)z}dz.

Therefore
\nu_{P_{i}}(bdx)=-1
for
i=3,\ldots,6
. Thus the poles of
bdx
are
P_{3},P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
.

For
i=1,2
we have
\nu_{P_{i}}(b)=2
and so
\nu_{P_{i}}(\eta)=1
. It follows that

\nu_{P_{i}}(\eta dx)=\nu_{P_{i}}(-\eta x^{2}d(x^{-1}))=\nu_{P_{i}}(-\eta x^{2})=-1

for
i=1,2
. So
P_{1},P_{2}
are poles of
\eta dx
. On the other hand,

\eta dx=\frac{2\eta z}{4x^{3}+1}dz
and so
\nu_{P_{i}}(\eta dx)=0
for
i=3,\ldots,6
.
So the poles of
\eta dx
are
P_{1},P_{2}
.

In summary, we see that the poles of
f_{1}dx
as well as of
f_{2}dx
are exactly
{\mathcal{P}}
.

We next compute the residues. As
\nu_{P_{i}}(x-\alpha_{i})=2
for
i=3,\dots,6
, the
P_{i}
-adic expansion of
x-\alpha_{i}
is of the form
c_{i}z^{2}+\dots
with
c_{i}\in k^{\times}
. Substituting
x=\alpha_{i}+c_{i}z^{2}+\dots
we find

bdx=\tfrac{2(x-\beta)^{2}}{(4x^{3}+1)z}dz=\tfrac{2(\alpha_{i}-\beta+c_{i}z^{2}+\dots)^{2}}{4(\alpha_{i}+c_{i}z^{2}+\ldots)^{3}+1}\tfrac{1}{z}dz=\left(\tfrac{2(\alpha_{i}-\beta)^{2}}{4\alpha_{i}^{3}+1}\tfrac{1}{z}+\dots\right)dz.

Therefore
\operatorname{res}_{P_{i}}(bdx)=\frac{2(\alpha_{i}-\beta)^{2}}{4\alpha_{i}^{3}+1}
for
i=3,\dots,6
. We next compute
\operatorname{res}_{P_{i}}(\eta dx)
for
i=1,2
.

As
\nu_{P_{i}}(z^{2})=\nu_{P_{i}}(x^{4}+x+\alpha)=-4
, we see that
\nu_{P_{i}}(z)=-2
for
i=1,2
. So the
P_{i}
-adic expansion of
z
is of the form
z=c_{i}(x^{-1})^{-2}+\ldots
with
c_{i}\in K^{\times}
for
i=1,2
. We have

\eta dx=-\eta x^{2}d(x^{-1})=\tfrac{-(x-\beta)x^{2}}{z}d(x^{-1})=\tfrac{-(x^{-1})^{-3}+\beta(x^{-1})^{-2}}{c_{i}(x^{-1})^{-2}+\ldots}d(x^{-1})

and so
\operatorname{res}_{P_{i}}(\eta dx)=-1/c_{i}
.

Plugging
z=c_{i}(x^{-1})^{-2}+\ldots
into
z^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha
we find
(c_{i}(x^{-1})^{-2}+\ldots)^{2}=(x^{-1})^{-4}+\ldots
and so
c_{i}\in\{1,-1\}
for
i=1,2
. Suppose
c_{1}=c_{2}
. Then
\frac{z}{x^{2}}
and
\frac{1}{x}
are rational functions on
C
that take the same value in
k
on
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
and
F=k(\frac{z}{x^{2}},\frac{1}{x})
. Thus
P_{1}=P_{2}
; a contradiction. So
c_{1}\neq c_{2}
.
To find the exact value of
c_{i}
we can evaluate
\frac{z}{x^{2}}=1-\frac{2u^{3}}{(v+1)^{2}}
at the point
(0:1:1)
corresponding to
P_{2}
. This yields
c_{2}=1
and so
c_{1}=-1
. Thus
\operatorname{res}_{P_{1}}(\eta dx)=1
and
\operatorname{res}_{P_{2}}(\eta dx)=-1
.

With this information at hand we can now calculate

Z_{1}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})=\left\{(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\ \bigg|\ \sum_{i=1}^{2}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}\ \forall\,P\in{\mathcal{P}}\,\mbox{and}\,\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{2}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)=0\right\}.

Note that for
P_{i}
(
i=3,\ldots,6
), the first condition is
(d_{1}+d_{2})\frac{2(\alpha_{i}-\beta)^{2}}{4\alpha_{i}^{3}+1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, whereas for
P_{1},P_{2}
the first condition is vacuous, because
\operatorname{res}_{P_{i}}(f_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}
for
i=1,2
.
There indeed exist
\alpha,\beta\in k
such that
\frac{2(\alpha_{i}-\beta)^{2}}{4\alpha_{i}^{3}+1}\in\mathbb{Q}
for
i=3,\ldots,6
, for example,
\alpha=0
and
\beta=\frac{1}{2}
.

Set

r=\prod_{i=3}^{6}\frac{(\alpha_{i}-\beta)^{2}}{4\alpha_{i}^{3}+1}.

A direct calculation shows that

r=\frac{(\beta^{4}+\beta+\alpha)^{2}}{256\alpha^{3}-27}.
Thus, the first condition mentioned above, implies that
r\in\mathbb{Q}
if
d_{1}+d_{2}\neq 0
.

Assume that
r\notin\mathbb{Q}
. Then

Z_{1}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})=\{(-d,d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}|\ d\in\mathbb{Z}\}

and for
(-d,d)\in Z_{1}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})
we have

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\right){P}=-2d{P_{1}}+2d{P_{2}}=2d{(P_{2}-P_{1})}

and so

Z_{2}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})=\{(-d,d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}|\ 2d(P_{2}-P_{1})\text{ is a principal divisor of }F/k\}.

Recall (see e.g.,
[
Sti09
, Prop. 6.1.6]
) that the choice of a point of
C(k)
defines a bijection between the degree zero divisor class group
\operatorname{Cl}^{0}(F/k)
and
C(k)
. As usual, we choose the point to be
(0:1:0)\in C(k)
. Thus the point
(0:1:0)
corresponding to
P_{1}
is the neutral element of the group
C(k)
. Then
2d{(P_{2}-P_{1})}
is principal, i.e., equal to zero in
\operatorname{Cl}^{0}(F/k)
, if and only if
2d(0:1:1)=0
in
C(k)
.
Therefore
Z_{2}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})
is trivial if and only if the point
(0,1)
is not a torsion point of the elliptic curve
v^{2}=u^{3}-4\alpha u+1
.
In this case
f_{1},f_{2}
are logarithmically independent over
F
by Lemma
3.26
. It is not easy to exactly determine the values of
\alpha
for which
(0,1)
is a torsion point, but this question will be further discussed in Example
4.28
.

If
(0,1)
is a torsion point, then
a basis of
Z_{2}((f_{1},f_{2}),{\mathcal{P}})
is given by
e_{1}=(-e,e)
, where
2e
is the order of
(0,1)
in
C(k)
. One can then write
2e{(P_{2}-P_{1})}=(h_{1})
with
h_{1}\in F^{\times}
. In this case,
f_{1},f_{2}
are logarithmically independent if and only if

\tfrac{\delta(h_{1})}{h_{1}}-(-e)f_{1}-ef_{2}=\tfrac{\delta(h_{1})}{h_{1}}+2e\eta

is nonzero.
The latter questions will also be further discussed in Example
4.28
.

3.5
Exponential bounds for linear differential operators

A solution
h
of a homogeneous scalar linear differential equation over
k(x)
is an
exponential solution
if its logarithmic derivative
\delta(h)/h
lies in
k(x)
, i.e.,
h
satisfies
\delta(h)=ah
, with
a\in k(x)
. The goal of this section is to describe a fairly explicit bound on the degrees of the logarithmic derivatives of the exponential solutions.

We will work with the (non-commutative) ring
k(x)[\delta]
of linear differential operators with coefficients in
k(x)
(as defined in
[
vdPS03
, Section 2.1]
).
Thus a (scalar) linear differential equation

\delta^{n}(y)+a_{n-1}\delta^{n-1}(y)+\ldots+a_{0}y=0

with
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\in k(x)
, can be written more succinctly as
Ly=0
, where

L=\delta^{n}+a_{n-1}\delta^{n-1}+\ldots+a_{0}\in k(x)[\delta]

is the corresponding linear differential operator.

A nonzero solution
h
of
Ly=0
(in some Picard-Vessiot extension of
k(x)
) is called an
exponential solution
of
Ly=0
if
\delta(h)/h\in k(x)
. The reader is referred to Section 4.1 of
[
vdPS03
]
for more background on exponential solutions and their computation.

Recall that the
degree
of a nonzero rational function
a=\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}\in k(x)
with
a_{1},a_{2},\in k[x]
relatively prime, is defined as
\deg(a)=\max\{\deg(a_{1}),\deg(a_{2})\}
. By convention,
\deg(0)=-\infty
.

Definition 3.28
.

An integer
N
is an
exponential bound
for
Ly=0
, if

\deg(\delta(h)/h)\leq N
for any exponential solution
h
of
Ly=0
(belonging to some Picard-Vessiot extension of
k(x)
).

The main point of this section is to describe an exponential bound
N(L)
for
Ly=0
that is well-behaved under specialization. This bound is also described in the second paragraph on page 109 of
[
vdPS03
]
.

The elements
u\in k(x)
of the form
u=\delta(h)/h
for some exponential solution
h
of
Ly=0
are exactly the solutions of the Riccati equation associated with
Ly=0
(
[
vdPS03
, Def. 4.6]
).
Another description of these
u
’s is, as the set of elements of
k(x)
such that
\delta-u\in k(x)[\delta]
is a right-hand factor of
L
.

To describe the bound
N(L)
we need to introduce some notation from
[
vH97b
]
and
[
vH97a
]
. Set
\bar{\delta}=x\delta
. Then
k(x)[\delta]=k(x)[\bar{\delta}]
, i.e., every operator in
\delta
can be rewritten as an operator in
\bar{\delta}
. Set

{{\mathbb{E}}=\bigcup_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}k[x^{-1/r}]}

and let
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
.
The
ramification index

\operatorname{ram}(e)
of
e
is the minimal
r
such that
e\in k[x^{-1/r}]
.
For
L\in k(x)[\bar{\delta}]
, or more generally, for
L\in k((x))[\bar{\delta}]
we set

S_{e}(L)=L(\bar{\delta}+e)\in k((x^{-1/r}))[\bar{\delta}]
and
write

S_{e}(L)=\sum_{i\geq\ell}x^{i/r}N_{i,e,L}(\bar{\delta}),

where
N_{i,e,L}\in k[T]
(with
T
a new variable) and
N_{\ell,e,L}\neq 0
. Following
[
vH97b
, Section 3.4]

N_{\ell,e,L}
is called the
Newton polynomial
of
S_{e}(L)
at
0
for slope
0
. Let
m_{e,0}(L)
be the multiplicity of
0
in
N_{\ell,e,L}(T)
. Following
[
vH97a
, Def. 3.1]
, we define the generalized exponents of
L
:

Definition 3.29
.

An element
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
is a
generalized exponent
of
L
at
0
if
m_{e,0}(L)>0
.

The number
m_{e,0}(L)
is called the
multiplicity
of
e
in
L
.
By (3.3) on page 542 of
[
vH97a
]
, we have

\sum_{e\in{\mathbb{E}}}m_{e,0}(L)=\operatorname{ord}(L)(=n).

(25)

In particular,
L
only has finitely many generalized exponents at
0
.

Example 3.30
.

Let us determine the generalized exponents for the linear differential operator
L=\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{x}\delta+1-(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2}\in k(x)[\delta]
corresponding to Bessel’s differential equation, where
\alpha
is an arbitrary element of
k
. Rewriting
L
in terms of
\bar{\delta}
, we have

L=\frac{1}{x^{2}}\bar{\delta}^{2}+1-(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2}
. So, after normalizing (which does not effect the generalized exponents), the relevant operator is
L^{\prime}=\bar{\delta}^{2}+x^{2}-\alpha^{2}
. For
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
we have

L^{\prime}(\bar{\delta}-e)=(\bar{\delta}-e)^{2}+x^{2}-\alpha^{2}=\bar{\delta}^{2}-2e\bar{\delta}-x\delta(e)+e^{2}+x^{2}-\alpha^{2}.

Thus, choosing
e\in\{\alpha,-\alpha\}
, we have
L^{\prime}(\bar{\delta}-e)=\bar{\delta}^{2}-2e\bar{\delta}+x^{2}
, which has Newton polynomial
T^{2}-2eT
. By (
25
), the generalized exponents of
L
are
\alpha
and
-\alpha
. In case
\alpha=0
, the generalized exponent
e=0
has multiplicity two.

Remark 3.31
.

Due to Lemma 3.4 of
[
vH97a
]
, if
\bar{\delta}-u\in k((x))[\bar{\delta}]
is a right-hand factor of
L\in k(x)[\bar{\delta}]\subseteq k((x))[\bar{\delta}]
, then
u=e+u^{\prime}
, where
e\in k[x^{-1}]
is a generalized exponent of
L
at
0
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
and
u^{\prime}\in xk[[x]]
. However, this can also be seen directly: If
L=L_{1}\cdot(\bar{\delta}-u)
is a factorization in
k((x))[\bar{\delta}]
, and
u
is written as
u=e+u^{\prime}
with
e\in k[x^{-1}]
and
u^{\prime}\in xk[[x]]
, then
S_{e}(L)=S_{e}(L_{1})\cdot(\bar{\delta}-u^{\prime})
, which has a Newton polynomial vanishing at zero, because for every power of
x
occurring in
S_{e}(L_{1})u^{\prime}
, there is a smaller power of
x
occurring in
S_{e}(L_{1})\bar{\delta}
.

In order to define the generalized exponents at a point
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)=k\cup\{\infty\}
different from
0
, we introduce (following
[
vH97a
, Section 3.4]
) the
k
-algebra automorphism

l_{\lambda}:k(x)[\delta]\rightarrow k(x)[\delta]

as follows: if
\lambda\in k
, then
l_{\lambda}(x)=x+\lambda
and
l_{\lambda}(\delta)=\delta
, otherwise
l_{\infty}(x)=1/x
and
l_{\infty}(\delta)=-x^{2}\delta
. For
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
set
m_{e,\lambda}(L)=m_{e,0}(l_{\lambda}(L))
.

Definition 3.32
.

If
m_{e,\lambda}(L)>0
, then
e
is called a
generalized exponent of
L
at
\lambda
and
m_{e,\lambda}(L)
is called the multiplicity of
e
in
L
at
\lambda
.

Since we are only interested in the factors of
L\in k(x)[\delta]
that are of the form
\delta-u
with
u\in k(x)
, we only need to consider the generalized exponents
e
of
L
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
.
However, generalized exponents with ramification index larger than
1
are necessary for (
25
) to hold and this formula is crucial for showing that our exponential bound is preserved under many specializations (Proposition
4.3
). We will next explain how Remark
3.31
can be used to obtain the possible principal parts of an exponential solution of
L
.

Before proceeding, let us recall some terminology for rational function. The
principal part
of an element
u\in k(x)
at
\lambda\in k
is
\sum_{i=\ell}^{-1}u_{i}(x-\lambda)^{i}
, when
u=\sum_{i=\ell}^{\infty}u_{i}(x-\lambda_{i})^{i}
is written as an element of
k((x-\lambda))
. The
residue

\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u)
of
u
at
\lambda
is
\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u)=u_{-1}
.
The
order

\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(u)
of
u
at
\lambda
is
\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(u)=-\ell
, where
u_{\ell}\neq 0
. By convention,
\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(0)=0
.

The residue at infinity is defined as
\operatorname{res}_{\infty}(u)=\operatorname{res}_{0}(-\frac{1}{x^{2}}u(\frac{1}{x}))
, while the order at infinity is
\operatorname{ord}_{\infty}(u)=\operatorname{ord}_{0}(u(\frac{1}{x}))
. These definitions are such that the sum of all residues of
u
is zero (see e.g.
[
Sti09
, Cor. 4.3.3]
).

The principal part of
u
at
\infty
is
\sum_{i=\ell}^{1}u_{i}x^{-i}
, when
u=\sum_{i=\ell}^{\infty}u_{i}x^{-i}
is written as an element of
k((x^{-1}))
. This definition is such that
\operatorname{res}_{\infty}(u)
can be determined (as
-u_{1}
) from the principal part at infinity.

Assume that
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
is a generalized exponent of
L
at
\lambda
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
. We set

\operatorname{pp}(e,\lambda)=\begin{cases}\frac{l_{-\lambda}(e)}{x-\lambda}&\text{ if }\lambda\in k\\
-\frac{l_{\infty}(e)}{x}&\text{ if }\lambda=\infty.\end{cases}

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
u\in k(x)
be such that
\delta-u
is a right-hand factor of
L
. Then, for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)
, the principal part of
u
at
\lambda
is of the form
\operatorname{pp}(e,\lambda)
for some generalized exponent
e
of
L
at
\lambda
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
.

Proof.

We write
L=L_{1}\cdot(\delta-u)
, with
L_{1}\in k(x)[\delta]
. First assume that
\lambda\in k
. Applying
l_{\lambda}
yields

l_{\lambda}(L)=l_{\lambda}(L_{1})\cdot(\delta-\l_{\lambda}(u))=L_{2}\cdot(\bar{\delta}-xl_{\lambda}(u))

for some
L_{2}\in k(x)[\bar{\delta}]
.
By Remark
3.31
,
xl_{\lambda}(u)=e+u^{\prime}
, where
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
is a generalized exponent of
L
at
\lambda
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
and
u^{\prime}\in xk[[x]]
. Applying
l_{-\lambda}
to
xl_{\lambda}(u)=e+u^{\prime}
, we find
u=\frac{l_{-\lambda}(e)}{x-\lambda}+\frac{l_{-\lambda}(u^{\prime})}{x-\lambda}
.
Thus
\frac{l_{-\lambda}(e)}{x-\lambda}
is the principal part of
u
at
\lambda
.

Now let us consider the case
\lambda=\infty
.
We have

l_{\infty}(L)=l_{\infty}(L_{1})\cdot(-x^{2}\delta-l_{\infty}(u))=l_{\infty}(L_{1})\cdot(-x\bar{\delta}-\l_{\infty}(u))=L_{2}\cdot\big(\bar{\delta}-\tfrac{-l_{\infty}(u)}{x}\big).

Therefore, as above, by Remark
3.31
, we have
\frac{-l_{\infty}(u)}{x}=e+u^{\prime}
, where
e
is a generalized exponent of
L
at
\infty
with
\operatorname{ram}(e)=1
and
u^{\prime}\in xk[[x]]
. Applying
(l_{\infty})^{-1}=l_{\infty}
, we obtain
u=-\frac{l_{\infty}(e)+l_{\infty}(u^{\prime})}{x}
. This implies the claim.
∎

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(k)=k\cup\{\infty\}
we set

\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)=\left\{\operatorname{pp}(e,\lambda)\mid\mbox{$e\in{\mathbb{E}}$ is a generalized exponent of $L$ at $\lambda$ with $\operatorname{ram}(e)=1$}\right\}.

With
\operatorname{Sing}(L)\subseteq k
we denote the set of all singular points of
L
, i.e., the elements of
k
that are poles of one of the coefficients
a_{i}
of
L
.
Write
\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}=\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{m}\}
and define

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Phi_{L}:\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda_{1})\times\dots\times\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda_{m})&\longrightarrow&k\\
(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})&\longmapsto&-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda_{i}}(f_{i})\end{array}

and

N(L)=\max\{\{0\}\cup(\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{L})\cap\mathbb{Z})\}+\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}}\max_{f\in\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)}\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(f).

Lemma 3.34
.

The number
N(L)
is an exponential bound for
Ly=0
.

Proof.

Let
h
be an exponential solution of
Ly=0
and set
u=\delta(h)/h\in k(x)
. We have to show that
\deg(u)\leq N(L)
. As
u
is a solution of the Riccati equation associated with
Ly=0
, it follows from (4.3) on page 107 of
[
vdPS03
]
, that
u
is of the form

u=\frac{\delta(p_{1})}{p_{1}}+q_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}},

(26)

where
p_{i},q_{i}\in k[x]
,
\deg(p_{2})<\deg(q_{2})
, the zeros of
q_{2}
are in
\operatorname{Sing}(L)
and the zeros of
p_{1}
are not in
\operatorname{Sing}(L)
.

Note that the partial fraction decomposition of
\frac{\delta(p_{1})}{p_{1}}
is of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{n_{i}}{x-\lambda^{\prime}_{i}}
, with
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
n_{1}+\ldots+n_{d}=\deg(p_{1})
and
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\in k\smallsetminus\operatorname{Sing}(L)
. The partial fraction decomposition of
\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}
is a sum of terms of the form
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{\lambda^{\prime}_{i}}{(x-\lambda)^{i}}
with
\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\in k
and
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)
. The polynomial
q_{1}
can be thought of as the contribution at
\infty
.

By Lemma
3.33
we have

\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(u)\leq\max_{f\in\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)}\{\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(f)\}

for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}(k)
.
As
\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(q_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}})\leq\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(u)
for
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}
, this yields

\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(q_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}})\leq\max_{f\in\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)}\{\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(f)\}
for
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}
. Thus

\deg(q_{1}+\tfrac{p_{2}}{q_{2}})\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}}\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(q_{1}+\tfrac{p_{2}}{q_{2}})\leq\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}}\max_{f\in\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)}\operatorname{ord}_{\lambda}(f).

(27)

To also bound
\deg(p_{1})
, let
B\subseteq k
be the set of poles of
u
that are not in
\operatorname{Sing}(L)
. Then
B
is the set of zeros of
p_{1}
. Moreover,
q_{1}
and
\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}
have residue zero at any point of
B
.
Therefore

\sum_{\lambda\in B}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u)=\sum_{\lambda\in B}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(\tfrac{\delta(p_{1})}{p_{1}})=\deg(p_{1}).

As
\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup B\cup\{\infty\}
contains all poles of
u
,
we have

0=\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup B\cup\{\infty\}}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u)=\deg(p_{1})+\sum_{\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(L)\cup\{\infty\}}\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u).

On the other hand,
\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(u)=\operatorname{res}_{\lambda}(f)
for some
f\in\operatorname{PP}(L,\lambda)
.
Therefore
\deg(p_{1})\in\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{L})\cap\mathbb{Z}
. So
\deg(p_{1})\leq\max\{\{0\}\cup(\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{L})\cap\mathbb{Z})\}
.
Combing this with (
26
) and (
27
) yields the claim of the lemma.
∎

Example 3.35
.

Let us determine the exponential bound
N(L)

from Lemma
3.34
for the linear differential operator
L=\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{x}\delta+1-(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2}\in k(x)[\delta]
corresponding to Bessel’s differential equation. Note that
\operatorname{Sing}(L)=\{0\}
.
We already determined the generalized exponents of
L
at
0
in Example
3.30
. They are
\alpha
and
-\alpha
. Thus
\operatorname{PP}(L,0)=\{\frac{\alpha}{x},\frac{-\alpha}{x}\}
.

To compute
N(L)
, we also need to determine the generalized exponents at
\infty
. We have
l_{\infty}(L)=(-x^{2}\delta)^{2}-x^{3}\delta+1-(\alpha x)^{2}=x^{2}\bar{\delta}^{2}+1-(\alpha x)^{2}
. So, after normalizing, the relevant operator is
L^{\prime}=\bar{\delta}^{2}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}-\alpha^{2}
. For
e\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we have
L^{\prime}(\bar{\delta}-e)=\bar{\delta}^{2}-2e\bar{\delta}-x\delta(e)+e^{2}+\frac{1}{x^{2}}-\alpha^{2}
. Let
i
and
-i
denote the roots of the polynomial
T^{2}+1
in
k
. For
e=\frac{i}{x}-\frac{1}{2}
we find

L^{\prime}(\bar{\delta}-e)=\bar{\delta}^{2}-(\frac{2i}{x}-1)\bar{\delta}+\frac{1}{4}-\alpha^{2}
, which has Newton polynomial
2iT
. Similarly, for
e=\frac{-i}{x}-\frac{1}{2}
, one finds the Newton polynomial
-2iT
.
Thus, by (
25
), the generalized exponents of
L
at
p=\infty
are
\frac{i}{x}-\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{-i}{x}-\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore
\operatorname{PP}(L,\infty)=\{i+\frac{1}{2x},\ -i+\frac{1}{2x}\}
. The image of
\Phi_{L}\colon\operatorname{PP}(L,0)\times\operatorname{PP}(L,\infty)\to k
is
\{-\alpha-\frac{1}{2},\ \alpha-\frac{1}{2}\}
.

Let us first assume that
\alpha-\frac{1}{2}
is not an integer. Then
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{L})\cap\mathbb{Z}=\emptyset
and it follows that
N(L)=1
. More precisely, if
u\in k(x)
is a solution of the Riccati equation associated with
Ly=0
and
u=\frac{\delta(p_{1})}{p_{1}}+q_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}
is written as in (
26
), then the proof of Lemma
3.34
shows that
\deg(p_{1})=0
, i.e.,
\frac{\delta(P_{1})}{P_{1}}=0
. Moreover, the only possibilities for
q_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{q_{2}}
are
i+\frac{\alpha}{x},\ -i+\frac{\alpha}{x},\ i-\frac{\alpha}{x},\ -i-\frac{\alpha}{x}
. These are not solutions of the Riccati equation

\delta(u)+u^{2}+\tfrac{1}{x}u+1-(\tfrac{\alpha}{x})^{2}=0

associated with
Ly=0
. Thus, there are no exponential solutions in this case.

Now assume that
\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\max\{\{0\}\cup(\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{L})\cap\mathbb{Z})\}=|\alpha|-\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore
N(L)=|\alpha|+\frac{1}{2}
.
In this case there are indeed exponential solutions. The interested reader can find their explicit form in the Appendix of
[
Kol68
]
.

3.6
Linear relations

Let
F
be a differential field and
R/F
a Picard-Vessiot ring. For a vector
v\in R^{\ell}
satisfying
\delta(v)=A^{\prime}v
for some
A^{\prime}\in F^{\ell\times\ell}
we set

\operatorname{LinRel}(v,F)=\{p\in F[y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}]\mid\mbox{$p$ is linear homogeneous and $p(v)=0$}\}.

Then
\operatorname{LinRel}(v,F)
is an
F
-vector space of dimension at most
\ell
; the vector space of linear relations among the entries of
v
. The main goal of this section is to describe, in the case
F=k(x)
, with the help of Section
3.5
, a bound
N
such that
\operatorname{LinRel}(v,F)
has a basis consisting of elements of the form
a_{1}y_{1}+\ldots+a_{\ell}y_{\ell}
, with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in k(x)
and
\deg(a_{i})\leq N
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

By the cyclic vector lemma, any linear differential system
\delta(y)=Ay
, with
A\in k(x)^{n\times n}
is equivalent to a scalar linear differential equation
Ly=0
with
L=\delta^{n}+a_{n-1}\delta^{n-1}+\ldots+a_{0}\in k(x)[\delta]
, i.e., there
exists a matrix
T\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(k(x))
such that
T^{-1}AT+\delta(T^{-1})T
is of the form

\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\ 0&1&0\\
&&&0&1\\
-a_{0}&-a_{1}&\dots&\dots&-a_{n-1}\end{pmatrix}\in k(x)^{n\times n}.

We call such a
T
a
transformation matrix
from
\delta(y)=Ay
to
Ly=0
. Furthermore,
\deg(T)
is defined as the maximum of all degrees of entries of
T
.

To a linear differential system
\delta(y)=Ay
, with
A\in k(x)^{n\times n}
, one can associate, for every
i=1,\ldots,n
, a new linear differential system
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{i}A)y
with
\bigwedge^{i}A\in k(x)^{{n\choose i}\times{n\choose i}}
. If one works with differential modules (as in
[
vdPS03
, Section 2.2]
) and
M
is the differential module associated with
\delta(y)=Ay
, then
\bigwedge^{i}M
is the differential module associated with
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{i}A)y
.

Lemma 3.36
.

Let
v\in R^{\ell}
be a solution of
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
, where
A^{\prime}\in k(x)^{\ell\times\ell}
and
R/k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring (not necessarily for
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
). Let
d
be the dimension of the
k(x)
-vector space generated by the entries of
v
and let
T\in k(x)^{m\times m}

(m={\ell\choose d})
be a transformation matrix from
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{d}A^{\prime})y
to
Ly=0
, where
L\in k(x)[\delta]
. Furthermore, let
N
be an exponential bound for
L
.

Then
\operatorname{LinRel}(v,k(x))
has a
k(x)
-basis consisting of elements of the form
a_{1}y_{1}+\ldots+a_{\ell}y_{\ell}
, with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in k(x)
and

\deg(a_{i})\leq 2m\deg(T)+m(m-1)N
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

Proof.

Write
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell})^{t}\in R^{\ell}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
are linearly independent over
k(x)
. For
i=d+1,\dots,\ell
, write

v_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{i,j}v_{j},\,\,a_{i,j}\in k(x).

(28)

Then
\big(y_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{i,j}y_{j}\big)_{i=d+1,\dots,\ell}
is a
k(x)
-basis of
\operatorname{LinRel}(v,k(x))
and our goal is to bound
\deg(a_{i,j})
.
Let
V
be the
k
-subspace of
R^{\ell}
generated by
\{g(v)|\ g\in G(k)\}
, where
G
is the differential Galois group of
R/k(x)
. We claim that
\dim_{k}V=d
. Set

\operatorname{Rel}(V)=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell})^{t}\in k(x)^{\ell}|\ a_{1}u_{1}+\ldots+a_{\ell}u_{\ell}=0\ \forall\ u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell})^{t}\in V\}.

From (
28
) it follows that
u_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{i,j}u_{i}
for every
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell})^{t}\in V
and
i=d+1,\ldots,\ell
. On the other hand,
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}
are
k(x)
-linearly independent. Thus
\operatorname{Rel}(V)
is a
k(x)
-vector space of dimension
\ell-d
.

We will show (cf.
[
BBH88
, Prop. 1.5]
) that also
\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{Rel}(V)=\ell-\dim_{k}V
. Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}
be a basis of
V
and let
B\in R^{\ell\times r}
be the matrix with columns
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}
.
As
b_{1},\ldots,b_{r}
are solutions of
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
, their
k
-linear independence, implies their
E
-
linear independence, where
E
is the field of fractions of
R
(
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 1.7]
).
Thus there exists an
r\times r
-submatrix
C\in\operatorname{GL}_{r}(E)
of
B
. As
g\in G(k)
acts on
B
and
C
via right multiplication with a matrix in
\operatorname{GL}_{r}(k)
, we see that
BC^{-1}
is fixed by
g
. So
BC^{-1}\in k(x)^{\ell\times r}
by the differential Galois correspondence. We have

\operatorname{Rel}(V)=\{a\in k(x)^{\ell}|\ aB=0\}=\{a\in k(x)^{\ell}|\ aBC^{-1}=0\}.

Since
BC^{-1}\in k(x)^{\ell\times r}
has linearly independent columns, we see that
\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{Rel}(V)=\ell-r=\ell-\dim_{k}(V)
. Therefore
\dim_{k}V=d
as claimed.

Let
b_{1},\ldots,b_{d}
be a
k
-basis of
V
.
For a vector or a matrix
w
, denote by
w^{(i)}
the
i
-th row of
w
.
For
I=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
with
1\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots<n_{d}\leq\ell
, set
b_{I}=\det((b_{j}^{(n_{i})})_{1\leq i,j\leq d})\in R
. A calculation, that is probably best understood by using the exterior power of the associated differential module (
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 2.27]
), shows that

\delta(b_{I})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\\
1\leq m_{1}<m_{2}<\dots<m_{d}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}a_{I,J}b_{J},

(29)

where
a_{I,J}\in k(x)
and
\bigwedge^{d}A^{\prime}=(a_{I,J})\in k(x)^{m\times m}
.
That is, the vector
(b_{I})_{I}\in R^{m}
is a solution of
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{d}A^{\prime})y
.
We extend the definition of the
a_{i,j}
’s from (
28
) to all values of
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
by

a_{i,j}=\begin{cases}1\text{ if }i=j,\\
0\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

for
i=1,\ldots,d
and
j=1,\ldots,d
. Then
(\ref{eq: vj})
is true for all values of
i\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
and so

b_{n}^{(i)}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{i,j}b_{n}^{(j)}
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
and
n=1,\ldots,d
, or in matrix form

\Big(b_{j}^{(i)}\Big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell\atop 1\leq j\leq d}=\Big(a_{i,j}\Big)_{1\leq i\leq\ell\atop 1\leq j\leq d}\Big(b_{j}^{(i)}\Big)_{1\leq i\leq d\atop 1\leq j\leq d}.

For
I=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
with
1\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<\dots<n_{d}\leq\ell
, we thus have

b_{I}=c_{I}b_{(1,2,\dots,d)},

(30)

with
c_{I}=\det((a_{n_{i},j})_{1\leq i,j\leq d})\in k(x)
. In particular,

c_{I}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ for }I=(1,2,\dots,d),\\
(-1)^{d-j}a_{i,j}&\text{ for }I=(1,\dots,j-1,j+1,\dots,d,i),\end{cases}

(31)

where
1\leq j\leq d
and
d<i\leq\ell
.
From (
29
) and (
30
) it follows that
b_{(1,2,\dots,d)}\in R
is exponential over
k(x)
, i.e.,
\delta(b_{(1,2,\dots,d)})/b_{(1,2,\dots,d)}\in k(x)
.

As
T
transforms
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{d}A^{\prime})y
into
Ly=0
, the solution
(b_{I})_{I}\in R^{m}
of
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{d}A^{\prime})y
is transformed to a vector
T^{-1}(b_{I})_{I}=(h,\delta(h),\ldots,\delta^{\ell-1}(h))^{t}\in R^{\ell}
with
L(h)=0
. Using
(
30
), we see that
h=b_{(1,2,\dots,d)}c
with
c\in k(x)
. It follows that
h
is an exponential solution of
L(y)=0
. Write
\delta(h)=a_{1}h
with
a_{1}\in k(x)
. Then also
\delta^{i}(h)=a_{i}h
with
a_{i}\in k(x)
for
i=1,\ldots,m-1
. So

T^{-1}(b_{I})_{I}=h\begin{pmatrix}1\\
a_{1}\\
\vdots\\
a_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{ or }\quad(b_{I})_{I}=hT\begin{pmatrix}1\\
a_{1}\\
\vdots\\
a_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}.

The last equation implies
b_{I}=hT^{(I)}(1,a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})^{t}
, where
T^{(I)}
denotes the
I
-th row of
T
.
Combining this with (
30
) and (
31
) yields

(-1)^{d-j}a_{i,j}=\frac{T^{(I)}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
a_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}}{T^{((1,2,\dots,d))}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
a_{m-1}\end{pmatrix}},

(32)

where
I=(1,\dots,j-1,j+1,\dots,d,i)
.

We claim that
\deg(a_{i})\leq i\deg(a_{1})
for
i=1,\ldots,m-1
. Indeed, writing
a_{1}=\frac{p_{1}}{q}
with
p_{1},q\in k[x]
relatively prime, we will show by induction on
i
, that
a_{i}=\frac{p_{i}}{q^{i}}
with
p_{i}\in k[x]
and
\deg(p_{i})\leq i\deg(a_{1})
. We have

a_{i+1}=\delta(a_{i})+a_{i}a_{1}=\frac{\delta(p_{i})q^{i}-ip_{i}q^{i-1}\delta(q)}{q^{2i}}+\frac{p_{i}p_{1}}{q^{i+1}}=\frac{\delta(p_{i})q-ip_{i}\delta(q)+p_{i}p_{1}}{q^{i+1}}.

So
\deg(p_{i+1})=\deg(\delta(p_{i})q-ip_{i}\delta(q)+p_{i}p_{1})\leq(i+1)\deg(a_{1}).

Recall that the degree of a rational function satisfies
\deg(ab)\leq\deg(a)+\deg(b)
and
\deg(a+b)\leq\deg(a)+\deg(b)
for
a,b\in k(x)
.
From
\deg(a_{i})\leq i\deg(a_{1})
, we obtain

\deg(T^{(I)}(1,a_{1},\ldots,a_{m-1})^{t})\leq\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}(\deg(T)+i\deg(a_{1}))\leq m\deg(T)+\tfrac{m(m-1)}{2}\deg(a_{1}).

Using (
32
), we find

\deg(a_{i,j})\leq 2m\deg(T)+m(m-1)\deg(a_{1}).

(33)

As
a_{1}=\delta(h)/h
, with
h
an exponential solution of
Ly=0
, this implies the claim of the lemma.
∎

4
Specializations

In this section we prove our main specialization result (Theorem
4.26
). To this end, we first show that various criteria and properties discussed in Section
3
are preserved under many specializations.
Throughout Section
4
we make the following assumptions:

•

k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero;

•

\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra that is an integral domain;

•

\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
;

•

K
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
.

We usually identify a
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
with the corresponding
k
-algebra morphism
c:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow k
. We denote with
b^{c}
the image of
b\in\mathcal{B}
under
c
. For a polynomial or Laurent polynomial
p
with coefficients in
\mathcal{B}
, we denote with
p^{c}
the polynomial obtained from
p
by applying
c
to its coefficients. A similar notation applies to fractions of polynomials with coefficients in
\mathcal{B}
(assuming that the denominator does not specialize to zero under
c
). Moreover,
M^{c}=\{p^{c}|\ p\in M\}
for any set
M
such that
p^{c}
makes sense for any
p\in M
. In a similar spirit, if
v
is a tuple or matrix such that the specialization
c
can be applied to the entries of
v
, then
v^{c}
is the tuple or matrix obtained from
v
by applying
c
to the entries of
v
.

We now provide an outline of the proof of the main specialization result (respectively Theorem B from the introduction). Starting from an inclusion of algebraically closed fields
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
and a differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
,
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
with differential Galois group
G
and Picard-Vessiot ring
R/k^{\prime}(x)
, we first spread out (using Lemma
3.11
)
R
and
G
into nice families
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{G}
over a parameter space
\mathcal{X}
. Our goal then is to show that the fibre
\mathcal{R}^{c}
over
c
is Picard-Vessiot for all
c
in an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. This is achieved via two main steps, corresponding to the two main steps of Hrushovski’s algorithm. The first main step is to show, using the criterion of Lemma
3.15
, that
\mathcal{R}^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot for all
c
in an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. The key ingredient for this is Theorem
4.24
, which, roughly speaking, states that for a fixed degree
d
, a basis of the space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a solution matrix for
\delta(y)=Ay
specializes to a basis of the space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a solution matrix for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
for all
c
in an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. Theorem
4.24
is proved in Section
4.5
, using Section
3.6
(via a reduction to the study of linear relations) and Section
4.1
, where it is shown that the exponential bound from Section
3.5
is preserved under specialization on an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

The second main step of the proof is to find, inside the ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
over which
\mathcal{R}^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot, an ad
\times
Jac-open subset over which
\mathcal{R}^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot. The criterion of Lemma
3.23
allows us to decide if a proto-Picard-Vessiot ring is Picard-Vessiot by testing if certain algebraic functions are logarithmically independent.
To apply this criterion, we thus need to show that logarithmic independence of algebraic functions is preserved on an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of the parameter space (Theorem
4.10
. This is proved in Section
4.3
, where it is shown, building on specialization results for divisors from Section
4.2
, that the criterion of Section
3.4
to test the logarithmic independence of algebraic functions is well-behaved under specialization.

4.1
The exponential bound under specialization

In this section we show that the exponential bound from Lemma
3.34
is preserved on an ad-open subset of the parameter space.

We first note a remark regarding the possibility of extending
\mathcal{B}
if necessary that will be used repeatedly in what follows.

Remark 4.1
.

Assume we are given a family
\mathcal{F}
over
\mathcal{X}
and we would like to show that a certain property holds of the fibre
\mathcal{F}_{c}
for all
c
in a

•

Zariski open,

•

ad-open,

•

Jac-open or

•

ad
\times
Jac-open

subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. Then we can, without loss of generality, replace
\mathcal{B}
with
a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}
-subalgebra of
K
.

Proof.

In the applications below it will always be clear what the family
\mathcal{F}
is. Let
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
be a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}
-subalgebra of
K
. We assume here that we can base change the family
\mathcal{F}
over
\mathcal{X}
to a family
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
such that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{c^{\prime}}=\mathcal{F}_{\phi(c^{\prime})}
for all
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
, where
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is the morphism corresponding to the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. By Chevalley’s theorem, if
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
, then
\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
contains a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. By Lemma
2.7
, a similar statement holds for ad-open, Jac-open, and ad
\times
Jac-open subsets of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.
∎

Let
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
be a monic polynomial and let

\mathcal{L}=\delta^{n}+a_{n-1}\delta^{n-1}+\ldots+a_{0}

be a linear differential operator with
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq K(x)
. So
\mathcal{L}\in K(x)[\delta]

and for any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
we have a linear differential operator

\mathcal{L}^{c}=\delta^{n}+a_{n-1}^{c}\delta^{n-1}+\ldots+a_{0}^{c}\in k(x)[\delta].

Example 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}=k[\alpha]
(with
\alpha
transcendental over
k
),
f=x
and
\mathcal{L}=\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{x}\delta+1-(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\delta]
.
From Example
3.35
we know that
N(\mathcal{L})=1
and
N(\mathcal{L}^{c})=1
for any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)=k
with
c-\frac{1}{2}\notin\mathbb{Z}
. On the other hand,
N(\mathcal{L}^{c})=|c|+\frac{1}{2}
in case
c-\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
So
N(\mathcal{L})
specializes well only on an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
N(\mathcal{L}^{c})=N(\mathcal{L})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. In particular,
N(\mathcal{L})
is an exponential bound for
\mathcal{L}^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.1
), we may assume that
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and that all coefficients of all generalized exponents at points in
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
belong to
\mathcal{B}
.
There exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
,

(i)

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})^{c}=\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L}^{c})
and
\lambda_{1}^{c}\neq\lambda_{2}^{c}
for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})
(one can use resultants for this step);

(ii)

\operatorname{ram}(e^{c})=\operatorname{ram}(e)
and
\operatorname{ord}_{0}(e^{c})=\operatorname{ord}_{0}(e)
for every generalized exponent
e
of
\mathcal{L}
at every point of
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
;

(iii)

m_{e^{c},\lambda^{c}}(\mathcal{L}^{c})=m_{e,\lambda}(\mathcal{L})
for every
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
and every generalized exponent
e
of
\mathcal{L}
at
\lambda
.

For (iii), note that the units of the ring
\mathcal{B}((x))
of formal Laurent series over
\mathcal{B}
, are exactly those Laurent series whose lowest nonzero coefficient is a unit in
\mathcal{B}
. So
f
may not be a unit in
\mathcal{B}((x))
. However, if
b\in\mathcal{B}
is the lowest nonzero coefficient of
f
, then
f
is a unit in
\mathcal{B}_{b}((x))
and therefore
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{b}((x))
. It follows that for
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
and
e\in\mathcal{B}[x^{-1/r}]
, the operator
S_{e}(l_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}))\in K((x^{1/r}))[\bar{\delta}]
has coefficients in
\mathcal{B}_{b}((x^{1/r}))
. Thus, all that is required to guarantee (iii), is that
c
does not specialize
b
or any coefficient of the Newton polynomial of
S_{e}(l_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}))
for slope
0
to zero.

Assume that
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
. By (iii),
e^{c}
is a generalized exponent of
\mathcal{L}^{c}
at
\lambda^{c}
with multiplicity
m_{e,\lambda}(\mathcal{L})
, for every generalized exponent
e
of
\mathcal{L}
at
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
. From formula (
25
), it follows that every generalized exponent of
\mathcal{L}^{c}
at
p^{c}
is equal to
e^{c}
for some generalized exponent
e
of
\mathcal{L}
at
\lambda
. Hence
\operatorname{PP}(\mathcal{L}^{c},\lambda^{c})=\operatorname{PP}(\mathcal{L},\lambda)^{c}
for every
\lambda\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathcal{L})\cup\{\infty\}
. Furthermore,
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}^{c}})=\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})^{c}
.

In general,
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})^{c}\cap\mathbb{Z}
can be larger than
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})\cap\mathbb{Z}
. However, if
\Gamma
is the subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B})
generated by
\{1\}\cup\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})
, then
\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})^{c}\cap\mathbb{Z}=\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})\cap\mathbb{Z}
for
c\in W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
.
Therefore, for
\mathcal{U}
an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
and
c\in\mathcal{U}
, we have

\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}^{c}})\cap\mathbb{Z}=\operatorname{Im}(\Phi_{\mathcal{L}})\cap\mathbb{Z}
and consequently
N(\mathcal{L}^{c})=N(\mathcal{L})
.
∎

4.2
Divisors under specialization

Our next goal is to show that logarithmic independence of algebraic functions is preserved under many specializations. To show that the criterion for the logarithmic independence from Lemma
3.26
is preserved under many specializations, we first need to know that principal divisors and residues are preserved under many specializations.

Let
F/K
be a function field of one variable, i.e., a finitely generated field extension of transcendence degree one.
Let
C
be a smooth projective model of the function field
F/K
, i.e.,
C
is a smooth projective (irreducible) curve over
K
with function field
F
. We fix a closed embedding of
C
into
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{K}=\operatorname{Proj}(K[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]).
So
C
is defined by a homogeneous prime ideal
I
of
K[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we can assume that
I
is generated by homogeneous polynomials with coefficients in
\mathcal{B}
, and so
\mathcal{I}=I\cap\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
is a homogeneous prime ideal of
\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
such that
\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=I
in
\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=K[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
.

Set
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Proj}(\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]/\mathcal{I})
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}
is the closed subscheme of
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
defined by
\mathcal{I}
. Then
\mathcal{C}
is an integral scheme of finite type over
k
, projective over
\mathcal{X}
. Since
(\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]/\mathcal{I})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=K[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]/I
, we have
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)=C
. Thus the generic fibre of
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{X}
is smooth, geometrically irreducible and of dimension one. These three properties are generic properties. (For geometric irreducibly see
[
Sta24
,
Tag 0559
]
, for fibre dimension see
[
Sta24
,
Tag 05F7
]
and for smoothness see
[
Gro67
, Prop. 17.7.11]
.) Thus there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}
such that for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
, the fibre
\mathcal{C}_{u}
over
u
is a smooth geometrically irreducible curve (over the residue field at
u
). Hence, replacing
\mathcal{B}
with a localization of
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may (and henceforth do) assume that
\mathcal{C}_{u}
is a smooth geometrically irreducible curve for all
u\in\mathcal{X}
. In line with our notation for specializations we set

C^{c}=\mathcal{C}_{c}
for
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
. Note that, concretely,
C^{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
is the closed subscheme defined by the homogeneous polynomials obtained by applying
c
to a set of homogeneous generators of
\mathcal{I}
.

Throughout Section
4.2
we assume that
\mathcal{C}
is as described above. In particular,
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
is an integral scheme of finite type over
k
, projective over
\mathcal{X}
and all fibres
C^{c}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n}_{k}
are smooth, irreducible curves.

In the sequel, we will usually identify the places of
F/K
with
C(K)
(and similarly for
k(C^{c})/k
and
C^{c}(k)
). The discrete valuation on
F/K
(or
k(C^{c})/k
) corresponding to a point (respectively place)
P
is denoted
\nu_{P}
.

For every
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
we have a specialization map

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\to C^{c}(k),\ P\mapsto P^{c},

where
P^{c}\colon\operatorname{Spec}(k)\to\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is such that the composition of
P^{c}
with the projection to
\mathcal{C}
is the composition of
\operatorname{Spec}(k)\xrightarrow{c}\mathcal{X}
with
P\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{C}
.

A rational function
h
on
\mathcal{C}
yields a rational function
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
on
C
via the projection
C=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)\to\mathcal{C}
. Note that
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
is well-defined because
for a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{C}
, the inverse image
\mathcal{U}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)
of
\mathcal{U}
under the projection
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)\to\mathcal{C}
is nonempty, since the generic point of
\mathcal{U}
and the point of
\operatorname{Spec}(K)
map to the generic point of
\mathcal{X}
.

Roughly speaking, the following lemma shows that principal divisors are preserved under specialization on a nonempty Zariski open subset. We think this lemma is well-known to the experts, but we were not able to locate a suitable reference.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
h
be a nonzero rational function on
\mathcal{C}
and let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r}
and
P_{1},\ldots,P_{s}
be the zeros and poles of
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
in
C(K)
. Assume that
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{r},P_{1},\ldots,P_{s}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(K)=C(K)
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{U}

•

h
yields a rational function
h^{c}
on
C^{c}
via the projection
C^{c}=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)\to\mathcal{C}
;

•

Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c},P_{1}^{c},\ldots,P_{s}^{c}
are all distinct;

•

Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c}
is the set of zeros of
h^{c}
and
P_{1}^{c},\ldots,P_{s}^{c}
is the set of poles of
h^{c}
;

•

\nu_{Z_{i}^{c}}(h^{c})=\nu_{Z_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
and
\nu_{P_{i}^{c}}(h^{c})=\nu_{P_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
for all
i
.

In particular, if
(h^{\operatorname{gen}})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}Z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{s}e_{j}P_{j}
, then
(h^{c})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}Z^{c}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{s}e_{j}P^{c}_{j}
.

Proof.

Assume that
h
is given by a regular function on a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{C}
. For
h^{c}
to be well-defined, we need to know that
\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)\subseteq\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)=C^{c}
is nonempty.
Since
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{X}
is a dominant morphism of schemes of finite type over
k
, the image contains a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
by Chevalley’s theorem. Then
\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is nonempty for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}(k)
because there is an element of
\mathcal{V}
mapping to
c\in\mathcal{X}
. This takes care of the first item in the lemma.

For the remaining three items, we choose a linear form
\ell=a_{0}y_{0}+\ldots+a_{n}y_{n}\in K[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
such that the hyperplane
H\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n}_{K}
defined by
\ell=0
does not contain any of the
Z_{i}
’s or
P_{i}
’s. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may assume that
\ell\in\mathcal{B}[y_{0},\ldots,y_{n}]
. In fact, after localizing
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we can assume that
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathcal{B}^{n+1}
is a column of an invertible matrix in
\mathcal{B}^{(n+1)\times(n+1)}
. Thus, after a linear change of coordinates on
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{B}}^{n}
, we can assume that
\ell
is given by
\ell=y_{0}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}
be the open subscheme of
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
where
x_{0}
does not vanish. So
\mathcal{U}_{0}
can be identified with
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}])
. Recall (see
[
Mil17
, Chapter 7, Section d]
or
[
Str99
, Prop. 3.4]
) that a point
P\in\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{B})
, i.e., a morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
(over
\mathcal{B}
) can be identified with a submodule
L
of
\mathcal{B}^{n+1}
such that
L
is a direct summand (i.e., there exists a submodule
M
of
\mathcal{B}^{n+1}
such that
\mathcal{B}^{n+1}=L\oplus M
) and
L
is locally free of rank
1
. The point
P
lies in
\mathcal{U}_{0}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
(i.e.,
\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
factors through
\mathcal{U}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
) if and only if
L
has a basis vector of the form
(b_{0},\ldots,b_{n})\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
with
b_{0}=1
. As the
Z_{i}
’s and
P_{i}
’s (considered as elements of
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{K}(K)
) do not lie in
H(K)
, we see that, after replacing
\mathcal{B}
with a localization of
\mathcal{B}
, we may assume that all the
Z_{i}
’s and
P_{i}
’s (considered as elements of
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{B})
) lie in
\mathcal{U}_{0}(\mathcal{B})
.

Consider the open subscheme
\mathcal{C}_{0}=\mathcal{C}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
of
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathcal{C}_{0}
identifies with a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}
and the
Z_{i}
’s and
P_{i}
’s identify with elements of
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{B}^{n}
. On the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
where the product of suitable differences of coordinates of the
Z_{i}
’s and
P_{i}
’s does not vanish, the elements
Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c},P_{1}^{c},\ldots,P_{s}^{c}
are all distinct.

Let
Z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathcal{B}^{n}\subseteq K^{n}
be one of the
Z_{i}
’s. Assume that
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
has a zero of multiplicity
m\geq 1
at
Z
, i.e.,
m=\nu_{Z}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
. So
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
is contained in the
m
-th power
\mathfrak{m}_{C,Z}^{m}
of the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{C,Z}
of the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{C,Z}.

Let
C_{0}
be the open subscheme of
C
where
y_{0}
does not vanish, in other words,
C_{0}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)
. Then
K(C)=K(C_{0})
and
\mathcal{O}_{C,Z}=\mathcal{O}_{C_{0},Z}
. As the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{C_{0},Z}
of
\mathcal{O}_{C_{0},Z}
is generated by the images of
y_{1}-z_{1},\ldots,y_{n}-z_{n}
, we see that
\mathfrak{m}_{C_{0},Z}^{m}
is generated by the set
M
consisting of the images of
m
-fold products of elements from
\{y_{1}-z_{1},\ldots,y_{n}-z_{n}\}
. So we can write
h^{\operatorname{gen}}=f_{1}m_{1}+\ldots+f_{d}m_{d}
with
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in\mathcal{O}_{C_{0},Z}\subseteq K(C_{0})
and
m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}\in M
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may assume that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}
come from rational functions on
\mathcal{C}
, i.e.,
f_{i}=h_{i}^{\operatorname{gen}}
for some rational function
h_{i}
on
\mathcal{C}
for
i=1,\ldots,d
.

Recall that
k(\mathcal{B})
denotes the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
. Note that the function field
k(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
(considered as a scheme over
k
) agrees with the function field
k(\mathcal{B})(\mathcal{C}_{k(\mathcal{B})})
of
\mathcal{C}_{k(\mathcal{B})}=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k(\mathcal{B}))
(considered as a scheme over
k(\mathcal{B})
) and the latter is contained in the function field
K(C)
(considered as a scheme over
K
). In fact,
K(C)=k(\mathcal{B})(\mathcal{C}_{k(\mathcal{B})})\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}K
. So the identity
h^{\operatorname{gen}}=f_{1}m_{1}+\ldots+f_{d}m_{d}
that holds in
K(C)
, can be interpreted as an identity

h=h_{1}m_{1}+\ldots+h_{d}m_{d}

(34)

that holds in
k(\mathcal{C})
.

We already observed in the beginning of the proof that a rational function
h
on
\mathcal{C}
can be specialized to a rational function
h^{c}
on
C^{c}
for all
c
in some nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. To specialize (
34
), we would like to know that if
h^{\operatorname{gen}}\in\mathcal{O}_{C_{0},\mathfrak{z}}
, then
h^{c}\in\mathcal{O}_{C_{0}^{c},Z^{c}}
, where
C_{0}^{c}
is the open subscheme of
C^{c}
where
y_{0}
does not vanish, i.e.,
C_{0}^{c}=\mathcal{C}_{0}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)
.

But
h^{\operatorname{gen}}\in\mathcal{O}_{C_{0},Z}
means that
h
can locally be described by a fraction
\frac{p}{q}
of two polynomials
p,q\in\mathcal{B}[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]
such that
q
does not vanish at
Z
. Thus, on the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
, where
q(Z)\in\mathcal{B}
does not vanish, the rational function
h^{c}
is locally described by the fraction
\frac{p^{c}}{q^{c}}
and so
h^{c}\in\mathcal{O}_{C_{0}^{c},Z^{c}}
.

Since the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{C_{0}^{c},Z^{c}}
of
\mathcal{O}_{C_{0}^{c},Z^{c}}
is generated by the images of
y_{1}-c(z_{1}),\ldots,y_{n}-c(z_{n})
, the specialization

h^{c}=h_{1}^{c}m_{1}^{c}+\ldots+h^{c}_{d}m^{c}_{d}
of (
34
) shows that
h^{c}\in\mathfrak{m}_{C_{0}^{c},Z^{c}}^{m}
. Therefore
\nu_{Z^{c}}(h^{c})\geq m=\nu_{Z}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
for all
c
in a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

From this inequality for the zeros, we can immediately derive a similar inequality for the poles, because a point is a pole of multiplicity
m
of a rational function if and only if it is a zero of multiplicity
m
of the inverse of the rational function. So there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}

•

h^{c}
is well-defined;

•

Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c},P_{1}^{c},\ldots,P_{s}^{c}
are all distinct;

•

\nu_{{Z}_{i}^{c}}(h^{c})\geq\nu_{{Z}_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
; in particular,
Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c}
are zeros of
h^{c}
;

•

\nu_{P^{c}_{i}}(h^{c})\leq\nu_{P_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
for all
i=1,\ldots,s
; in particular,
P_{1}^{c},\ldots,P_{s}^{c}
are poles of
h^{c}
.

Note that the statement of the lemma is trivial in case
h
is constant (i.e.,
h
lies in
K
) since in that case there are no zeros or poles. We may thus assume that
h
is not constant. Under this assumption we will now show that

[K(C):K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})]=[k(C^{c}):k(h^{c})]

(35)

for all
c
in a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. Since
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
is transcendental over
K
, the field extension
K(C)/K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
is finite and we can choose
\eta\in K(C)
such that
K(C)=K(h^{\operatorname{gen}},\eta)
. Let
q\in K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})[y]
be the minimal polynomial of
\eta
over
K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
. Replacing
\eta
with a
K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
-multiple of
\eta
if necessary, we can assume that
q\in K[h^{\operatorname{gen}}][y]
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we can indeed assume that
q\in\mathcal{B}[h^{\operatorname{gen}}][y]
. Let
p\in\mathcal{B}[x,y]
be such that
p(h^{\operatorname{gen}},y)=q
. By Gauss’s lemma,
p\in K[x,y]
is irreducible. In fact, since
p
is monic as a polynomial in
y
, we see that
p
is a prime element of
\mathcal{B}[x,y]
. So
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p))
is an integral scheme and
\mathcal{D}_{K}=\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)=\operatorname{Spec}(K[x,y]/(p))
has function field
K(h^{\operatorname{gen}},\eta)=K(C)
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}])
be an open affine subscheme of
\mathcal{C}
. Then

\mathcal{V}_{K}=\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K)

is a nonempty affine open subscheme of
C=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)
. Moreover
\mathcal{D}_{K}
and
\mathcal{V}_{K}
are birationally equivalent (over
K
). In other words, the fields of fractions of

\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
and
\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
are isomorphic as field extensions of
K
, where
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
stand for the images of
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p)
respectively.
Such an isomorphism and its inverse is given by fractions of polynomials with coefficients in
K
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may assume that all coefficients belong to
\mathcal{B}
. Then there exist nonzero elements
b\in\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]
and
a\in\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]
such that
\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]_{b}
and
\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]_{a}
are isomorphic over
\mathcal{B}
.
In other words, there exist an open subscheme
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{D}
and an open subscheme
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
are isomorphic over
\mathcal{B}
. Note that under this isomorphism, the rational function
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
on
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
corresponds to
\overline{x}
.

By Chevalley’s theorem, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{3}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}_{3}
is contained in the image of the vertical maps in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V^{\prime}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

For
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(k)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, the fibre
\pi_{2}^{-1}(c)
is a nonempty open subscheme of

C^{c}
and therefore has function field
k(C^{c})
. On the other hand, the fibre
\pi_{2}^{-1}(c)
is an open subscheme of the spectrum of
(\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p))\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k=k[x,y]/(p^{c})
.
The isomorphism
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\simeq\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism
\pi_{1}^{-1}(c)\simeq\pi_{2}^{-1}(c)
. Under the corresponding isomorphism of functions fields,
h^{c}\in k(C^{c})
corresponds to the image of
x
in the field of fractions of
k[x,y]/(p^{c})
. The degree of the field of fractions of
k[x,y]/(p^{c})
over the image of
x
is the degree of
p
as a polynomial in
y
, which equals
[K(C):K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})]
. Thus (
35
) holds for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
.

Set
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}
. By
[
Sti09
, Theorem 1.4.11]
, the degree of the zero divisor and the degree of the pole divisor of
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
is equal to
[K(C):K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})]
and similarly for
h^{c}
in place of
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
. Thus

\displaystyle[K(C):K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{Z_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\nu_{Z^{c}_{i}}(h^{c})\leq[k(C^{c}):k(h^{c})]=[K(C):K(h^{\operatorname{gen}})]

for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. This implies that
\nu_{Z_{i}^{c}}(h^{c})=\nu_{Z_{i}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
and that
h^{c}
has no zeros other than
Z_{1}^{c},\ldots,Z_{r}^{c}
. A similar argument applies for the poles instead of the zeros. We conclude that
\mathcal{U}
has the desired properties. ∎

Roughly speaking, the following lemma shows that residues are preserved under specialization on a nonempty Zariski open subset.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
h
and
t
be rational functions on
\mathcal{C}
such that
t^{\operatorname{gen}}
is a uniformizer at
P\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(K)=C(K)
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{U}

•

h^{c},t^{c}
and
\operatorname{res}_{P,t^{\operatorname{gen}}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})^{c}
are well-defined;

•

t^{c}
is a uniformizer at
P^{c}
and

•

\operatorname{res}_{P^{c},t^{c}}(h^{c})=\operatorname{res}_{P,t^{\operatorname{gen}}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})^{c}
.

Proof.

Note that uniformizers are characterized by the property that their valutation is one. It thus follows from Lemma
4.4
that there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}
such that
h^{c}
and
t^{c}
are well-defined and that
t^{c}
is a uniformizer at
P^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

If
\nu_{P}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})\geq 0
, then
\operatorname{res}_{P,t^{\operatorname{gen}}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})=0
and the claim follows from Lemma
4.4
. We may thus assume that
\nu_{P}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})=-m
, with
m\geq 1
.

From the uniqueness of the
P
-adic representation, it follows that there exist uniquely determined elements
a_{-m},\ldots,a_{-1}\in K
such that
\nu_{P}(h^{\operatorname{gen}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{-i}(t^{\operatorname{gen}})^{-i})\geq 0
and that in this situation
a_{-1}=\operatorname{res}_{P,t^{\operatorname{gen}}}(h^{\operatorname{gen}})
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may assume that
a_{-m},\ldots,a_{-1}\in\mathcal{B}
. By Lemma
4.4
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
the rational function
f=h-\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{-i}t^{-i}
on
\mathcal{C}
has a well-defined specialization
f^{c}=h^{c}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}a^{c}_{-i}(t^{c})^{-i}
with
\nu_{P^{c}}(f^{c})\geq 0
. It follows that
a_{-1}^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c},t^{c}}(h^{c})
.
∎

The following lemma explains the origin of the Jac-open subset that occurs in our main specialization result (Theorem
4.26
).
We denote with
\operatorname{Div}^{0}(-)
the group of divisors of degree zero.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(K)=C(K)
. Then there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
and all
d_{1},\ldots,d_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
d_{1}P_{1}+\ldots+d_{r}P_{r}\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C)
is not principal, then
d_{1}P_{1}^{c}+\ldots+d_{r}P^{c}_{r}\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C^{c})
is not principal.

Proof.

Let
J
be the Jacobian of
C
and let
\varphi\colon C\to J
be a canonical embedding. For more background on Jacobian varieties see e.g.,
[
Mil86
]
or
[
BLR90
, Chapter 9]
. Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.1
), there exists a commutative separated group scheme
\mathcal{J}
of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
and a closed embedding
\phi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{J}
over
\mathcal{X}
such that

•

\phi_{K}=\varphi
(in particular,
\mathcal{J}
is of Jacobian type) and

•

there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the base change
\phi^{c}\colon C^{c}\to\mathcal{J}^{c}
of
\phi
via
c\colon\operatorname{Spec}(k)\to\mathcal{X}
is a canonical embedding of
C^{c}
into its Jacobian
\mathcal{J}^{c}
for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

For every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C^{c}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{J}^{c}(k)}

where the vertical maps are the specialization maps. Let
\Gamma
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{B})
generated by
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{B})
and set
\mathcal{V}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathcal{J},\Gamma)
(notation as in Section
2.1
).

Then
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}
is an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.
Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
and assume that
d_{1}P_{1}+\ldots+d_{r}P_{r}\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C)
is not principal. Then
d_{1}P_{1}+\ldots+d_{r}P_{r}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{J}(K)=J(K)
is nonzero because
\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C)\to J(K)
is surjective with kernel the subgroup of principal divisors. Since the specialization map
\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{J}^{c}(k)
is injective on
\Gamma
, we see that
d_{1}P^{c}_{1}+\ldots+d_{r}P^{c}_{r}\in\mathcal{J}^{c}(k)
is nonzero.
Therefore
d_{1}P^{c}_{1}+\ldots+d_{r}P^{c}_{r}\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C^{c})
is not principal.
∎

4.3
Logarithmic independence under specialization

In this section we show that logarithmic independence is preserved on an Ad
\times
Jac-open subset of the parameter space.

Let
F
be a finite field extension of
K(x)
. So
F/K
is a function field of one variable, considered as a differential field via
\delta=\frac{d}{dx}
. Let
\eta\in F
be such that
F=K(x,\eta)
and the minimal polynomial of
\eta
over
K(x)
has coefficients in
\mathcal{B}[x]
. Let
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
be a monic polynomial such that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
is a differential subring of
F
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{B}[x,y]\subseteq K(x)[y]
be the minimal polynomial of
\eta
over
K(x)
.
By Gauss’s lemma
p\in K[x,y]
is irreducible and by
the Bertini-Noether theorem (
[
FJ08
, Prop. 9.4.3]
) there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
p^{c}\in k[x,y]
is irreducible for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Then
