\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[y]/(p))\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=k(x)[y]/(p^{c})

is a finite field extension of
k(x)
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Replacing
\mathcal{B}
with a localization of
\mathcal{B}
, we mays assume that
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{X}(k)
. For
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
we set

F^{c}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)

and for
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
we set
h^{c}=h\otimes 1\in F^{c}
. The following lemma explains that this notation is consistent with the notation from Section
4.2
. Let
C
be a smooth projective model of
F/K

and let
\mathcal{C}
and
C^{c}
be as described in Section
4.2
.

Lemma 4.7
.

After enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, there exists a nonzero element
b\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
such that
\operatorname{Spec}((\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta])_{b})
is isomorphic (over
\mathcal{B}
) to an open subscheme of
\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
we have
F^{c}\simeq k(C^{c})
and for
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
the rational function
h^{c}\in k(C^{c})
as defined in Lemma
4.4
corresponds to
h^{c}=h\otimes 1
as defined above.

Proof.

The argument is quite similar to the argument used towards the end of the proof of Lemma
4.4
. We therefore only replicate the main points.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p))
be the closed subscheme of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{\mathcal{B}}
defined by
p
.
Then
\mathcal{D}
is an integral scheme of finite type over
k
such that the geometric generic fibre
\mathcal{D}_{K}=\mathcal{D}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)=\operatorname{Spec}(K[x,y]/(p))
of
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{X}
is birationally equivalent (over
K
) to
C
.

Let
\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}])
be an open affine subscheme of
\mathcal{C}
. Then

\mathcal{V}_{K}=\mathcal{V}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K)

is a nonempty affine open subscheme of
C=\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)
and
\mathcal{D}_{K}
and
\mathcal{V}_{K}
are birationally equivalent (over
K
). In other words, the fields of fractions of

\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
and
\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
are isomorphic as field extensions of
K
, where
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
stand for the images of
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p)
respectively.
Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we can find elements
b\in\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]
and
a\in\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]
such that

\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]_{b}
and
\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]_{a}
are isomorphic over
\mathcal{B}
.

Localizing both sides one more time, we get an isomorphism between
\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]_{bf}
and
\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]_{a^{\prime}}
for some
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]
. This establishes the first claim of the lemma since
\mathcal{V^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}]_{a^{\prime}})
is an open subscheme of
\mathcal{C}
and

\mathcal{B}[\overline{x},\overline{y}]_{bf}=(\mathcal{B}[x,y]/(p))_{bf}=(\mathcal{B}[x][\eta])_{bf}=(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta])_{b}.

By Chevalley’s theorem, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}
is contained in the image of the vertical maps in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}((\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta])_{b})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V^{\prime}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

For
c\in\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(k)\subseteq\mathcal{X}
the fibre
\pi_{1}^{-1}(c)
is the spectrum of
(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta])_{b}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k=(k[x,y]/(p^{c}))_{(bf)^{c}}
; a ring with field of fractions
F^{c}=k(x)[y]/(p^{c})=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
.
On the other hand, the fibre
\pi_{2}^{-1}(c)=\mathcal{V}^{\prime}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)
is an open subscheme of
\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k)=C^{c}
and therefore has function field
k(C^{c})
. Thus the function fields
F^{c}
and
k(C^{c})
of the fibres are isomorphic. On each fibre,
h^{c}
is the interpretation of
h
as a rational function on the fibre. ∎

In the sequel, we will consider elements of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
as rational functions on
\mathcal{C}
as explained in Lemma
4.7
. Note that if
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
is interpreted as a rational function on
\mathcal{C}
, then
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
is simply
h
, considered as an element of
F
. In this situation, we will therefore usually write
h
instead of
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
and
P\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq C(K)
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\operatorname{res}_{P}(hdx)^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(h^{c}dx)
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

After enlarging
\mathcal{B}
and replacing
f
with a multiple of
f
if necessary, we can write
hdx=h_{1}dt
, where
h_{1},t\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
and
t
is a uniformizer at
P
. We then have
h=h_{1}\delta(t)
and so
h^{c}=h_{1}^{c}\delta(t^{c})
, i.e.,
h^{c}dx=h_{1}^{c}dt^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

By Lemma
4.5
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
t^{c}
is a uniformizer at
P^{c}
and
\operatorname{res}_{P^{c},t^{c}}(h_{1}^{c})=\operatorname{res}_{P,t}(h_{1})^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Therefore,

\operatorname{res}_{P}(hdx)^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P}(h_{1}dt)^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P,t}(h_{1})^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c},t^{c}}(h_{1}^{c})=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(h_{1}^{c}dt^{c})=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(h^{c}dx).

∎

We next show that the
\mathbb{Z}
-modules
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
(as defined in Section
3.4
) are preserved under many specializations.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
{\mathbf{f}}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
be a tuple of elements of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
and let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of poles of the differential forms
f_{1}dx,\ldots,f_{m}dx
on
C
. Assume that
{\mathcal{P}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(K)=C(K)
.
Then there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{U}

•

{\mathcal{P}}^{c}
is the set of poles of the differential forms
f_{1}^{c}dx,\ldots,f_{m}^{c}dx
on
C^{c}
and

•

Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})=Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
.

Moreover, there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that, in addition to the above two properties, we also have
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})=Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
for all
c\in\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

To resolve the first item, let us first discuss how the poles of a differential form
\omega=hdx
are determined by
h
and
x
. To see if
\omega
has a pole at a place
P
, one can write
\omega=h_{1}dt
with
t=t_{P}
a uniformizer at
P
and then see if
\nu_{P}(\omega)=\nu_{P}(h_{1})
is negative. But
hdx=h_{1}dt
entails
h_{1}=h\delta_{t}(x)
, where
\delta_{t}
is the derivation determined by
\delta_{t}(t)=1
. So
\nu_{P}(h_{1})=\nu_{P}(h)+\nu_{P}(\delta_{t}(x))
.
Using
[
Sti09
, Prop. 4.2.7]
, we see that

\nu_{P}(\delta_{t}(x))=\begin{cases}\nu_{P}(x)-1\ \text{ if }\ \nu_{P}(x)\neq 0,\\
\nu_{P}(x-a_{P})-1\ \text{ if }\ \nu_{P}(x)=0,\end{cases}

where
a_{P}
denotes the value of
x
at
P
, i.e., the image of
x
in the residue field
\mathcal{O}_{P}/\mathfrak{m}_{P}
.
Thus
\omega=hdx
has a pole at
P
if and only if (
\nu_{P}(x)\neq 0
and
\nu_{P}(h)+\nu_{P}(x)-1\leq-1
) or (
\nu_{P}(x)=0
and
\nu_{P}(h)+\nu_{P}(x-a_{P})-1\leq-1
).

Now let
h
be one of the
f_{i}
’s. After enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.1
), we may assume that all zeros and poles of
x
and
h
lie in
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})
. Then, by Lemma
4.4
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the principal divisors defined by
x
and
h
are preserved under all specializations belonging to
\mathcal{U}_{1}
. Similarly, by the same lemma, and after enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we can also assume that for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
and every pole
P
of
h
we have
\nu_{P}(x-a_{P})=\nu_{P^{c}}((x-a_{P})^{c})=\nu_{P^{c}}(x^{c}-a_{P^{c}})
.

In particular, for
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
, every zero or pole of
x^{c}\in k(C^{c})
is the specialization of a zero or pole of
x\in F/K
. For a place
Q
of
k(C^{c})/k
that is not the specialization of a zero or pole of
x
or of a pole of
h
,
we then have

\nu_{Q}(x^{c})=0
and
\nu_{Q}(h^{c})+\nu_{Q}(x^{c}-a_{Q})-1\geq 0+0=0
.
So, by the above consideration,
Q
is not a pole
h^{c}dx
.

By construction, for those places
Q
of
k(C^{c})/k
that come from zeros or poles of
x
or from poles of
h
, all the relevant valuations are preserved under specialization and so we see that for
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
the poles of
h^{c}dx
are exactly the specializations of the poles of
hdx
.
Thus we can find a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the first item of the lemma is satisfied for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

By Lemma
4.8
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{3}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f_{i}^{c}dx)
for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
i=1,\ldots,m
. Thus
Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})\subseteq Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}
. For the reverse inclusion, let
\Gamma
be the finitely generated subgroup of
(\mathcal{B},+)
generated by
1
and
\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)

(P\in{\mathcal{P}},\ i\in\{1,\ldots,m\})
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a nonempty ad-open subset contained in
\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
and
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
, i.e.,
d_{P,c}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f_{i}^{c}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
and
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}d_{P,c}=0
.
Then

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)-d_{P,c}\right)^{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)^{c}-d_{P,c}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f_{i}^{c}dx)-d_{P,c}=0

for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
.
As
c\in W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
, we can conclude that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)-d_{P,c}=0
. In particular,
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}d_{P,c}=0
. Thus
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and so
Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})=Z_{1}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Our next goal is to find a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{4}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})\subseteq Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{U}_{4}
.

Let
(e_{1,\ell},\ldots,e_{m,\ell})_{1\leq\ell\leq n}
be a basis of the
\mathbb{Z}
-module
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
write

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}e_{\ell,P}P=(h_{\ell})
, with
h_{\ell}\in F^{\times}
and
e_{\ell,P}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}
. Replacing
f
with a mulitple and enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary, we may assume that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n},h_{1}^{-1},\ldots,h_{n}^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
. By Lemma
4.4
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{4}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
(h_{\ell}^{c})=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}e_{\ell,P}P^{c}
for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
and all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{4}
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{U}_{4}
and
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
. Then there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
d_{i}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}a_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Moreover, since
\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\right)^{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)^{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f_{i}^{c}dx)

for all
P\in{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore

\displaystyle((h_{1}^{a_{1}}\ldots h_{n}^{a_{n}})^{c})

\displaystyle=((h_{1}^{c})^{a_{1}}\ldots((h_{n})^{c})^{a_{n}})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}a_{\ell}(h_{\ell}^{c})=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}a_{\ell}e_{\ell,P}P^{c}=

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{\ell}e_{i,\ell}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)P^{c}=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)P^{c}=

\displaystyle=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f^{c}_{i}dx)P^{c}.

This shows that
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})\in Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
. So
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})\subseteq Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{U}_{4}
.

For the reverse inclusion, we can use Lemma
4.6
to find an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{V}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for all
c\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
and all
d_{P}\in\mathbb{Z},\ (P\in{\mathcal{P}})
, if
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}d_{P}P\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C)
is not principal, then
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}d_{P}{P}^{c}\in\operatorname{Div}^{0}(C^{c})
is not principal.
For all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}\cap W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{U}_{4}\cap\mathcal{V}_{1}
we then have

Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})=Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})
.
∎

We are now prepared to show that logarithmic independence is preserved on an Ad
\times
Jac-open subset.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
be logarithmically independent over
F
. Then there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
f_{1}^{c},\ldots,f_{m}^{c}
are logarithmically independent over
F^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Set
{\mathbf{f}}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
and
let
{\mathcal{P}}
be the set of poles of
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in F
. Fix a basis
\{(e_{1,\ell},\ldots,e_{m,\ell})|\ \ell=1,\ldots,n\}
of
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
and for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
write

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)\right)P=(h_{\ell})\ \text{ with }\ h_{\ell}\in F^{\times}.

(36)

After enlarging
\mathcal{B}
and replacing
f
with a multiple if necessary, we can assume that

•

the set
{\mathcal{P}}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{C}(K)=C(K)
and

•

h_{1},\ldots,h_{n},h_{1}^{-1},\ldots,h_{n}^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
.

Let
d
be the degree of
\eta
over
K(x)
. As
a_{\ell}=\frac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
, we can find
r,s\in\mathbb{N}
and
b_{i,j,\ell}\in\mathcal{B}
such that

a_{\ell}=\frac{1}{f^{r}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{i,j,\ell}x^{i}\eta^{j}\right)

for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
. Since
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
F
, it follows from Lemma
3.26
that the differential forms
a_{1}dx,\ldots,a_{n}dx
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent. Note that the latter condition is equivalent to: If
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
d_{1}b_{i,j,1}+\ldots+d_{n}b_{i,j,n}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq s,\ 1\leq j\leq d
, then
d_{1}=\ldots=d_{n}=0
.

Let
\Gamma
be the subgroup of
\mathbb{G}_{a}(\mathcal{B})=(\mathcal{B},+)
generated by all
b_{i,j,\ell}
’s and let
\mathcal{U}_{1}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
be the ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
defined by
\Gamma
. By Lemma
4.9
there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
{\mathcal{P}}^{c}
is the set of poles of
{\mathbf{f}}^{c}
and
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})=Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Moreover, by Lemmas
4.4
and
4.8
, we can find a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{3}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that the principal divisors defined by
h_{\ell},\ (\ell=1,\ldots,n)
are preserved under specialization and
\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}dx)^{c}=\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f_{i}^{c}dx)\ (i=1,\ldots,m,\ P\in{\mathcal{P}})
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
contained in
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}_{3}
. We claim that
f_{1}^{c},\ldots,f_{m}^{c}
are logarithmically independent over
F^{c}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Using that
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
, we can specialize (
36
) to

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}\operatorname{res}_{P^{c}}(f^{c}_{i}dx)\right)=(h^{c}_{\ell})

for
\ell=1,\ldots,n
. Furthermore,
a_{\ell}^{c}=\left(\frac{\delta(h_{\ell})}{h_{\ell}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}f_{i}\right)^{c}=\frac{\delta(h^{c}_{\ell})}{h^{c}_{\ell}}-\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i,\ell}f^{c}_{i}
.
Using that
Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}}^{c},{\mathcal{P}}^{c})=Z_{2}({\mathbf{f}},{\mathcal{P}})
, the criterion of Lemma
3.26
states that
f_{1}^{c},\ldots,f_{m}^{c}
are logarithmically independent over
F^{c}
if and only if
a_{1}^{c},\ldots,a_{n}^{c}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent.
Note that the degree of
\eta^{c}
over
k(x)
equals
d
, the degree of
\eta
over
K(x)
(cf. the discussion before Lemma
4.7
). As

a_{\ell}^{c}=\frac{1}{(f^{c})^{r}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{i,j,\ell}^{c}x^{i}(\eta^{c})^{j}\right),

we see that
a_{1}^{c},\ldots,a_{n}^{c}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent if and only if
d_{1}b^{c}_{i,j,1}+\ldots+d_{n}b^{c}_{i,j,n}=0
for
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
1\leq i\leq s,\ 1\leq j\leq d
implies
d_{1}=\ldots=d_{n}=0
. But
d_{1}b^{c}_{i,j,1}+\ldots+d_{n}b^{c}_{i,j,m}=0
implies, by the injectivity of
\Gamma\to k,\ \gamma\mapsto\gamma^{c}
, that
d_{1}b_{i,j,1}+\ldots+d_{n}b_{i,j,n}=0
.
Thus
a_{1}^{c},\ldots,a_{n}^{c}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent and
f_{1}^{c},\ldots,f_{m}^{c}
are logarithmically independent as desired.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

In case
\eta=1
, i.e.,
F=K(x)
, the Jac-open subset is not needed, i.e., the set
\mathcal{U}
in Theorem
4.10
can be chosen to be ad-open.

Proof.

In this case we can choose
\mathcal{C}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}
and so
C^{c}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
for all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
. In this case Lemma
4.6
is trivial because all degree zero divisors are principal. Thus Lemma
4.6
does not create a Jac-open set and also the subsequent results (Lemma
4.9
and Theorem
4.10
) can make do without the Jac-open set.
∎

4.4
The setup for specializing Picard-Vessiot rings

Let
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
be an inclusion of algebraically closed fields. In Lemma
3.11
we have seen how a Picard-Vessiot ring
R/k^{\prime}(x)
can be spread out to a differential torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. In this section we discuss some first properties of
\mathcal{R}
and its specializations
R^{c}
.
We now describe our framework for specializing Picard-Vessiot rings.

Notation 4.12
.

•

k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero;

•

\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra and an integral domain;

•

\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
;

•

K
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions
k(\mathcal{B})
of
\mathcal{B}
;

•

\mathcal{G}
is an affine group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
;

•

f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
is a monic polynomial and
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential ring with respect to
\delta=\frac{d}{dx}
;

•

\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{n\times n}
;

•

\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
such that
\mathcal{R}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
;

•

R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
, where
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)=\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\mathcal{B},k)
and the tensor product is formed using the extension of
c\colon\mathcal{B}\to k
to
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
determined by
c(x)=x
;

•

Y^{c}=\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{c})
is the image of
\mathcal{Y}
in
R^{c}
;

•

A^{c}\in k(x)^{n\times n}
is obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by applying
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
to the coefficients of
\mathcal{A}
;

•

G^{c}=\mathcal{G}_{c}
is the algebraic group over
k
obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by base change via
c\colon\mathcal{B}\to k
;

•

R^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple;

•

Y^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{\operatorname{gen}})
is the image of
\mathcal{Y}
in
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
;

•

G^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{G}_{K}
is the algebraic group over
K
obtained from
\mathcal{G}
by base change via
\mathcal{B}\to K
.

Furthermore, for
h\in\mathcal{R}
, denote with
h^{c}=h\otimes 1\in R^{c}
the image of
h
in
R^{c}
.
Similarly,
h^{\operatorname{gen}}=h\otimes 1\in R^{\operatorname{gen}}
denotes the image of
h
in
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
.

For the remainder of Section
4
we assume Notation
4.12
.

Notation
4.12
gives a precise meaning to the idea of a family of potential Picard-Vessiot rings for a family of linear differential equations with prescribed potential differential Galois groups. (Lemmas
4.14
and
4.15
below elaborate this point.) We think of
\mathcal{R}
as defining the family
(R^{c})_{c\in\mathcal{X}(k)}
and we would like to compare the individual
R^{c}
’s with the ring
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
at the generic fibre.

We now explore some first consequences of Notation
4.12
.

Lemma 4.13
.

The ring
\mathcal{R}
is an integral domain and the morphisms
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
and
\mathcal{R}\to R^{\operatorname{gen}}
are injective.

Proof.

Suppose that
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a nonzero element of the kernel of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to\mathcal{R}
. Then the image
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
of
h
in
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is zero but also a unit. Thus
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
must be the zero ring. This contradicts the assumption that
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is
\delta
-simple.

Assume that
b\in\mathcal{B}
lies in the kernel of
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
. Then the image of
b
in
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is zero. But then the second diagram of Lemma
3.3
implies that
b=0
.

As
\mathcal{R}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
it is clear that
\mathcal{R}\to R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is injective. From this it follows that
\mathcal{R}
is an integral domain because differentially simple rings are integral domains.
∎

Because
\mathcal{R}\to R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is injective, it is not really necessary to distinguish between an element
h\in\mathcal{R}
and its image
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
in
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
.
However, we will sometimes use the notation
h^{\operatorname{gen}}
to emphasize that we are considering
h
as an element of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
.

At the generic fibre we have a Picard-Vessiot ring:

Lemma 4.14
.

The
K(x)
-
\delta
-algebra
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
with fundamental matrix
Y^{\operatorname{gen}}
and differential Galois group
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-
torsor,
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is a differential
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
-
torsor by Lemma
3.7
(i). But
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K[x]_{f}
and
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=K[x]_{f}
. Thus
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is a differential
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
-torsor by Lemma
3.7
(ii).

As
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\mathcal{Y},\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}]
and
\delta(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{Y}
, we have
R^{\operatorname{gen}}=K(x)[Y^{\operatorname{gen}},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})}]

and
\delta(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})=\mathcal{A}Y^{\operatorname{gen}}
. Because
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
is assumed to be
\delta
-simple, we see that
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
.

It follows from
[
BHHW18
, Prop. 1.12]
that the differential Galois group of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
.
∎

So, reversing the idea of Lemma
3.11
, we can think of
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
as having been obtained by “spreading out” the Picard-Vessiot ring
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
. At the special fibres we either have nothing or a ring that “looks like” a Picard-Vessiot ring:

Lemma 4.15
.

If
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
is such that
R^{c}
is not the zero ring, then
R^{c}/k(x)
is a differential
G^{c}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
with fundamental matrix
Y^{c}
. Moreover, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}
is not the zero ring for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Assume that
R^{c}
is not the zero ring. As in Lemma
4.14
, using Lemma
3.7
, one sees that
R^{c}/k(x)
is a differential
G^{c}
-
torsor. Furthermore,
R^{c}=k(x)[Y^{c},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{c})}]
and
\delta(Y^{c})=A^{c}Y^{c}
.
Let
T
be a
k
-algebra and
g\in G^{c}(T)
. When interpreted as an element of
\mathcal{G}(T)
,
g
acts on
\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}T)
by multiplication with a matrix
[g]\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(T)
. Thus
g
also acts on
Y^{c}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{c}\otimes_{k}T)
by multiplication with
[g]
. This proves the first claim of the lemma.

For the second claim, note that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
by Lemma
4.13
and that
\mathcal{R}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-algebra. By
[
Bou72
, Cor. 3, Chapter V, §3.1]
(applied with
b=1
) there exists a nonzero
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
such that any morphism
\psi
(of rings) from
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
into an algebraically closed field with
\psi(h)\neq 0
, extends to a morphism on
\mathcal{R}
. Without loss of generality we may assume
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]
. Let
a\in\mathcal{B}
be the leading coefficient of
h
and let
\mathcal{U}
be the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
defined by
a
, i.e.,
\mathcal{U}
is the complement of the vanishing locus of
a
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
. We will show that
R^{c}
is not the zero ring. Taking
\psi
as
\psi\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\xrightarrow{c}k(x)\to\overline{k(x)}
, we see that
\psi(h)\neq 0
and so
\psi
extends to a morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{R}\to\overline{k(x)}
. Then
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to\overline{k(x)},\ r\otimes s\mapsto\psi(r)s
is a morphism of rings. Thus
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
cannot be the zero ring.
∎

We can do slightly better:

Lemma 4.16
.

There exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}
is an integral domain for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

The key fact from algebraic geometry that we will use is that being geometrically integral “spreads out” from the generic fibre (
[
Poo17
, Theorem 3.2.1 (ii)]
). More concretely, if
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
is an inclusion of finitely generated
k
-algebras, such that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is an integral domain (where, as usual,
K
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions
k(\mathcal{B})
of
\mathcal{B}
), then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
such that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\overline{k(\mathfrak{p})}
is an integral domain for every
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{U}
.

To apply this to our situation, note that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is an integral domain because
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)=R^{\operatorname{gen}}
and the latter is an integral domain.

By the first paragraph, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k
is an integral domain for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. As

R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k[x]_{f^{c}})\otimes_{k[x]_{f^{c}}}k(x)=(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k)\otimes_{k[x]_{f^{c}}}k(x),

we see that
R^{c}
is a localization of the integral domain
R\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. So
R^{c}
is either the zero ring or an integral domain. Thus the claim follows from Lemma
4.15
.
∎

The following example illustrates Lemma
4.16
.

Example 4.17
.

Let
a
be a unit of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
such that
a
is not a square in
k(\mathcal{B})(x)
. Then
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[y]/(y^{2}-a)=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
is an integral domain and naturally a differential ring (with
\delta(\eta)=\frac{\delta(a)}{2a}\eta
). Let
b\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. As in Example
3.6
set

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta][y_{1},y_{2},y_{1}^{-1},y_{2}^{-1}]=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\mathcal{Y},\tfrac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}],

where
\delta(y_{1})=(b+\eta)y_{1}
,
\delta(y_{2})=(b-\eta)y_{2}
,

p_{1}=X_{21}X_{22}-(b^{2}-a)X_{11}X_{12},\quad\quad p_{2}=X_{21}X_{12}+X_{22}X_{11}-2bX_{11}X_{12,}

\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
a+\delta(b)-b^{2}-\frac{\delta(a)b}{2a}&2b+\frac{\delta(a)}{2a}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{2\times 2},

and
\delta(X)=\mathcal{A}X
. Then
\mathcal{R}
is an integral domain and
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the group scheme of
2\times 2
monomial matrices over
\mathcal{B}
(as seen in Example
3.6
).

We claim that for
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)

the
k(x)
-
\delta
-algebra
R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is an integral domain if and only if
a^{c}
is not a square in
k(x)
.
Note that
\mathcal{R}
is a Laurent-polynomial ring in
y_{1},y_{2}
over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
. Therefore,
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is a Laurent polynomial ring over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
. The latter is an integral domain, if and only if
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=k(x)[y]/(y^{2}-a^{c})
is an integral domain and this is the case if and only if
a^{c}
is not a square in
k(x)
.

Thus, for example, for
\mathcal{B}=k[\alpha,\beta]
, with
\alpha,\beta
algebraically independent over
k
and
a=x^{2}+\alpha x+\beta
, we see that
R^{c}
is an integral domain if and only if
c\in\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{X}(k)
is the Zariski open subset where the discriminant
\alpha^{2}-4\beta
does not vanish.

By Lemma
4.15
the
R^{c}
’s “look like” Picard-Vessiot rings for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
with differential Galois group
G^{c}
. The only missing piece of information is whether or not
R^{c}
is
\delta
-simple. If
R^{c}
is
\delta
-simple, then
R^{c}/k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring with differential Galois group
G^{c}
.

The main question is: Are there “many”
c
’s in
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot?
(In Theorem
4.26
we will see that the answer is yes.)

The following example illustrates that the set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}
is Picard-Vessiot is in general not constructible.

Example 4.18
.

Let
\mathcal{B}=k[\alpha]
, with
\alpha
a variable over
k
and set
f=x
. Let

\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2}-1&-\frac{1}{x}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{2\times 2}=k[\alpha,x]_{x}^{2\times 2}

be the companion matrix of Bessel’s differential equation
\delta^{2}(y)+\frac{1}{x}\delta(y)+(1-(\frac{\alpha}{x})^{2})y=0
. Let
X
be a
2\times 2
matrix of indeterminates over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. Consider
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
as a
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebra via
\delta(X)=\mathcal{A}{X}
. Then

\delta(\det(X)-\tfrac{1}{x})=\delta(\det(X))+\tfrac{1}{x^{2}}=-\tfrac{1}{x}\det(X)+\tfrac{1}{x^{2}}=-\tfrac{1}{x}(\det(X)-\tfrac{1}{x})

and so
(\det(X)-\frac{1}{x})
is a
\delta
-ideal of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
and
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/(\det(X)-\frac{1}{x})
is a
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebra. Let
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
denote the image of
X
in
\mathcal{R}
. Let
\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}=\operatorname{SL}_{2,\mathcal{B}}
be the group scheme
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
over
\mathcal{B}
. Then
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
: For a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
, a
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{T})
defines a
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
-
\delta
-automorphism
g\colon\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}
by
g(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)=(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)(1\otimes g)
.
The corresponding morphism
\rho\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebras (as in Lemma
3.3
) is given by
\rho(\mathcal{Y})=(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)(1\otimes T)
, where
T\in\mathcal{B}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]^{2\times 2}
is the matrix of coordinate functions on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. The morphism
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}],\ a\otimes b\mapsto(a\otimes 1)\cdot\rho(b)
is an isomorphism, its inverse is determined by
1\otimes T\mapsto(\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1)^{-1}(1\otimes\mathcal{Y})
. So
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
.

From a more geometric perspective, if
\mathcal{Q}
is any ring (e.g.,
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
) and
a\in\mathcal{Q}^{\times}
, it is clear that the closed subscheme
\mathcal{Z}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2,\mathcal{Q}}
defined by
\det(X)-a
is an
\operatorname{SL}_{2,\mathcal{Q}}
-torsor: For any
\mathcal{Q}
-algebra
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, the map

\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\times\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}),\ (z,g)\mapsto(z,zg)
is bijective with inverse
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto(z_{1},z_{1}^{-1}z_{2})
.

The differential Galois group of Bessel’s equation is
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
if
\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\notin\mathbb{Z}
and the multiplicative group otherwise (Appendix of
[
Kol68
]
). The significance of the condition
\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\notin\mathbb{Z}
is also explained in Example
3.35
. It follows that
R^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)/K(x)
is Picard-Vessiot. Moreover,
R^{c}
is Picard-Vessiot if and only if
c\in k\smallsetminus\{\frac{1}{2}+m|\ m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

The following three lemmas will be useful later on.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{R}
be nonzero. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
h^{c}\in R^{c}
is nonzero for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Applying
[
Bou72
, Cor. 3, Chapter V, §3.1]
to the inclusion
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
, we find a nonzero element
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
such that every morphism

\psi
(of rings) from
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
into an algebraically closed field with
\psi(h^{\prime})\neq 0
, extends to a morphism
\psi
on
\mathcal{R}
with
\psi(h)\neq 0
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}[x]
. Let
a\in\mathcal{B}
be the leading coefficient of
h^{\prime}
and let
\mathcal{U}
be the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
defined by
a
.

For
c\in\mathcal{U}
let
\psi
be defined by
\psi\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\xrightarrow{c}k(x)\to\overline{k(x)}
. Then
\psi(h^{\prime})\neq 0
and
\psi
extends to
\mathcal{R}
with
\psi(h)\neq 0
. We can thus define a morphism
\psi^{\prime}\colon R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to\overline{k(x)},\ r\otimes s\mapsto\psi(r)s
. Then
\psi^{\prime}(h^{c})=\psi(h)\neq 0
. Thus
h^{c}\neq 0
.
∎

Recall (Definition
3.4
) that the fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
defines a morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
of group schemes over
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

There exists a nonzero element
b\in\mathcal{B}
such that the base change of
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
via
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}_{b}
is a closed embedding.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
. As in the proof of Lemma
4.14
we see that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/k(\mathcal{B})(x)
is a differential
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
.
From this we deduce that the morphism
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k(\mathcal{B})}
determined by
\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1
is a closed embedding as follows: Assume
\mathcal{T}
is a
k(\mathcal{B})
-
algebra and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{T})=\operatorname{Hom}_{k(\mathcal{B})}(k(\mathcal{B})[\mathcal{G}],\mathcal{T})
is in the kernel of
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{T})\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{T})
, i.e.,
g
acts trivially on
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}\mathcal{T}
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}k(\mathcal{B})[\mathcal{G}]
denote the coaction and
e\in\operatorname{Hom}(k(\mathcal{B})[\mathcal{G}],\mathcal{T})=\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{T})
the identity element. As
g
acts trivially, the maps

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}k(\mathcal{B})[\mathcal{G}]\xrightarrow{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes g}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}\mathcal{T}

and

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}k(\mathcal{B})[\mathcal{G}]\xrightarrow{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes e}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})}\mathcal{T}

agree (with
a\mapsto a\otimes 1
). As the
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-linear extension of
\rho^{\prime}
is an isomorphism, it follows that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes g=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes e
and therefore
g=e
. This shows that
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}(\mathcal{T})\to\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{T})
is injective for every
k(\mathcal{B})
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
. Therefore
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k(\mathcal{B})}
is a closed embedding (
[
Wat79
, Section 15.3]
).

The morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
determined by
\mathcal{Y}
, after base change via
\mathcal{B}\to k(\mathcal{B})
, yields the morphism
\mathcal{G}_{k(\mathcal{B})}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,k(\mathcal{B})}
determined by
\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1
(and, as we have just seen, the latter is a closed embedding). So the map
\psi\colon\mathcal{B}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\to\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
, dual to the morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
, becomes surjective after the base change
\mathcal{B}\to k(\mathcal{B})
. In other words, the map
S^{-1}\psi\colon S^{-1}\mathcal{B}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]\to S^{-1}\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is surjective, where
S
is the multiplicatively closed subset of all nonzero elements of
\mathcal{B}
.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
be generators of
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
as a
\mathcal{B}
-algebra and let
\frac{f_{1}}{s_{1}},\ldots,\frac{f_{m}}{s_{m}}\in S^{-1}\mathcal{B}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
be such that
(S^{-1}\psi)(\frac{f_{i}}{s_{i}})=\frac{a_{i}}{1}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Then there exist
s_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{m}^{\prime}\in S
such that
s_{i}^{\prime}(\psi(f_{i})-s_{i}a_{i})=0
for
i=1,\ldots,m
. Thus
b=s_{1}\ldots s_{m}s_{1}^{\prime}\ldots s_{m}^{\prime}
has the desired property.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}
denote the integral closure of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
in
\mathcal{R}
and for
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
let
k(x)^{\prime}
denote the integral closure of
k(x)
in
R^{c}
. Then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to k(x)^{\prime}\subseteq R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is an isomorphism for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Let
m
denote the geometric number of irreducible components of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
. As
\overline{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}=\overline{K(x)}
, it is clear that the geometric number of irreducible components of
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)/k(\mathcal{B})(x)
is also
m
.

We claim that the (vector space) dimension
\dim_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
equals
m
.
The inclusion
k(\mathcal{B})(x)=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)\subseteq\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
can be identified with the localization of the inclusion
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
at the multiplicatively closed subset of all nonzero elements of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. As integral closure commutes with localization (
[
Sta24
,
Tag 0307
]
), the integral closure
k(\mathcal{B})(x)^{\prime}
of
k(\mathcal{B})(x)
in
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
is
k(\mathcal{B})(x)^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
. As integral closure commutes with separable algebraic base change (
[
Sta24
,
Tag 0CBF
]
), it follows that the integral closure
K(x)^{\prime}
of
K(x)
in

(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x))\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}K(x)=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)=R^{\operatorname{gen}}

is
K(x)^{\prime}=(\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x))\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}K(x)
. As
[K(x)^{\prime}:K(x)]=m
by Corollary
3.18
, it follows that
\dim_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)=m
as claimed.

It is known that
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-module (
[
Eis04
, Cor. 13.13]
). Thus, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{V}_{1}
of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f})
such that
\dim_{k(\mathfrak{p})}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})
is constant on
\mathcal{V}_{1}
(e.g., using
[
Eis04
, Theorem 14.4]
). As
\dim_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)=m
, we must have
\dim_{k(\mathfrak{p})}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})=m
for all
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{1}
.

For a morphism of schemes of finite type the number of geometrically irreducible components of the fibres is constant on a nonempty Zariski open subset (
[
Sta24
,
Tag 055A
]
).
Applying this to the dual of the inclusion
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
shows that there exists a non-empty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{V}_{2}
of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f})
such that the number of geometrically irreducible components of
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})
is constant on
\mathcal{V}_{2}
. As the number of geometrically irreducible components of
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathcal{B})(x)
is
m
, we see that the number of geometrically irreducible components of
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})
is
m
for all
\mathfrak{p}
in
\mathcal{V}_{2}
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq K
, being a composition of a localization with a field extension, makes
K
a flat
\mathcal{B}
-module. Thus the map
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K\to\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is injective. We already noted in the proof of Lemma
4.16
that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is an integral domain and so
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}K
is an integral domain. As in loc. cit. it follows that there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is an integral domain for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{B}[x]
be a nonzero polynomial such that
D(h)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}
and let
\mathcal{U}
be the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
where the leading coefficient of
h
does not vanish. We will show that

\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to k(x)^{\prime}
is an isomorphism for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
and let
\mathfrak{p}
be the kernel of
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
. Then
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(\mathfrak{p})=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=R^{c}
. As
c
does not vanish on the leading coefficient of
h
we have
\mathfrak{p}\in\mathcal{V}_{1}\cap\mathcal{V}_{2}
. So
\dim_{k(x)}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=m
and the geometric number of connected components of
R^{c}
is
m
. It thus follows from Corollary
3.18
that
[k(x)^{\prime}:k(x)]=m
. Therefore,
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
and
k(x)^{\prime}
have the same degree
m
over
k(x)
. It thus suffices to show that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to k(x)^{\prime}
is injective. But as
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
, the ring
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is an integral domain and therefore a field, since it is integral over
k(x)
.
∎

The following remark, which is important from a technical perspective and will be used repeatedly in what follows, specializes Remark
4.1
to the context of Notation
4.12
.

Remark 4.22
.

Assume that in the setup of Notation
4.12
we would like to show that there exists

•

an ad-open,

•

a Jac-open or

•

an ad
\times
Jac-open

subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that

•

the differential
k(x)
-algebra
R^{c}
has a certain property for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
or

•

that for given
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\mathcal{R}
, the elements
f_{1}^{c},\ldots,f_{m}^{c}\in R^{c}
have a certain property,

then we can, without loss of generality, replace
\mathcal{B}
with an integral domain
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is finitely generated and algebraic over
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Note that
k(\mathcal{B})\subseteq k(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\subseteq\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}=K
. Set

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}\subseteq\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)=R^{\operatorname{gen}}.

Then
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is not the zero ring and so
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}
-
torsor over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}
by Lemma
3.7
.
We next verify that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}
has all the properties listed in Notation
4.12
. The matrix
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Y}\otimes 1\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
is such that
\delta(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}[\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})}]
. Since
\mathcal{G}
acts on
\mathcal{Y}
through matrix multiplication, also
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
acts on
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
through matrix multiplication. Thus
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}/\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
with fundamental matrix
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
.
As
\mathcal{R}
is a flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
, we see that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}
. Moreover,

R^{\prime{\operatorname{gen}}}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}}K(x)=(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}}K(x)=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)=R^{\operatorname{gen}}

is
\delta
-simple.

Similarly, if
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)\to\mathcal{X}(k)
is the morphism induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}(k)
is such that
\phi(c^{\prime})=c
, then

{R^{\prime}}^{c^{\prime}}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}[x]_{f}}k(x)=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=R^{c}.

Furthermore, for
i=1,\ldots,m
, the image
f_{i}^{c^{\prime}}
of
f_{i}
in
R^{\prime c^{\prime}}
agrees with the image
f_{i}^{c}
of
f_{i}
in
R^{c}
.

Thus, if there exists a subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(k)
of one of the three kinds listed in the remark such that one of the two properties of the remark is true for all
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, then Lemma
2.7
yields a subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
of the corresponding kind such that
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\phi(\mathcal{U}^{\prime})
and the corresponding property holds for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.
∎

4.5
Algebraic relations under specialization

Recall that we assume Notation
4.12
for the remainder of Section
4
. The main result of this section (Theorem
4.24
) is that a basis of the vector space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
specializes to a basis of the vector space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
for all
c
in an ad-open subset of the parameter space. This is in spirit similar to Theorem B from the introduction. However, the present result is weaker because we have to fix the degree of the algebraic relations.

By considering the vector of all monomials of degree at most
d
in the entries of a fundamental solution matrix, the study of the algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix can be reduced to the study of the linear relations satisfied by the entries of one solution vector. We therefore, first study the behavior of linear relations under specialization.
Combining the main result of Section
3.6
with the main result of Section
4.1
we obtain:

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
be such that
v^{\operatorname{gen}}\in(R^{\operatorname{gen}})^{\ell}
is a solution of
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A^{\prime}}y
, with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in K(x)^{\ell\times\ell}
. Then there exists a positive integer
N
and an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
the following holds: If
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
is a maximal
\delta
-
ideal of
R^{c}
and
\overline{v^{c}}\in(R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c})^{\ell}
is the image of
v^{c}
in
R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c}
, then
the
k(x)
-vector space
\operatorname{LinRel}(\overline{v^{c}},k(x))
has a basis consisting of elements of the form
a_{1}y_{1}+\ldots+a_{\ell}y_{\ell}
, with
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in k(x)
and
\deg(a_{i})\leq N
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

Proof.

For
i=1,\dots,\ell
, let
\mathcal{L}_{i}\in K(x)[\delta]
be a monic differential operator, equivalent to
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{i}\mathcal{A}^{\prime})y
and let
T_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}_{{\ell\choose i}}(K(x))
be a corresponding transformation matrix. (See Section
3.6
.) Enlarging
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.22
), we can assume that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
,
T_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}_{i}

(1\leq i\leq\ell)
have coefficients in
\mathcal{B}[x]_{ff_{1}}
, where
f_{1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]
is a monic polynomial. So, for a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
, the matrix
T_{i}^{c}
lies in
\operatorname{GL}_{{\ell\choose i}}(k(x))
and is a transformation matrix from
\delta(y)=(\bigwedge^{i}\mathcal{A}^{\prime})^{c}y=(\bigwedge^{i}\mathcal{A}^{\prime c})y
to
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{c}y=0
. Note that
v^{c}
is a solution of
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}^{\prime c}y
for every
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

By Proposition
4.3
, for each
i=1,\dots,\ell
, there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{i}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
N(\mathcal{L}_{i})
is an exponential bound for
\mathcal{L}_{i}^{c}y=0
for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{i}
.
Note that
\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{LinRel}(\overline{v^{c}},k(x))
lies between
1
and
\ell
for any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
. It thus follows from Lemma
3.36
that

N=\max_{1\leq i\leq\ell}\left\{2{\ell\choose i}\deg(T_{i})+{\ell\choose i}\left({\ell\choose i}-1\right)N(\mathcal{L}_{i})\right\}

and
\mathcal{U}
any ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\ldots\cap\mathcal{U}_{\ell}
have the required property.
∎

Let
F
be a differential field,
A\in F^{n\times n}
and
R/F
a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
with fundamental matrix
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R)
. For a fixed positive integer
d
, we set

\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y,d,F)=\{p\in F[X]\mid p(Y)=0,\ \deg(p)\leq d\}.

Then
\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y,d,F)
is a finite dimensional
F
-vector space; the vector space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of
Y
.

The following theorem shows that a basis of the vector space of algebraic relations of degree at most
d
among the entries of a fundamental solution matrix is preserved on an ad-open subset of the parameter space.

Theorem 4.24
.

For any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
, let
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
be a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R^{c}
and let
\overline{Y^{c}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c})
denote the image of
Y^{c}
in
R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c}
.
Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x][X]
be a
K(x)
-
basis of
\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y^{{\operatorname{gen}}},d,K(x))
.
Then there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c}
is a
k(x)
-
basis of
\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},d,k(x))

for any
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

The main step of the proof is to show that there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that

\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},d,k(x))\leq\dim_{K(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y^{{\operatorname{gen}}},d,K(x))

(37)

for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

Let
{\mathbf{m}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{r}
denote all monomials in
X
of degree at most
d
(so
r={n^{2}+d\choose d}
)
and let
\ell
be the dimension of the
K(x)
-vector space generated by
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{r}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})
(inside
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
).
We may assume, without loss of generality, that
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})
are
K(x)
-linearly independent. We see that
m=\dim_{K(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y^{{\operatorname{gen}}},d,K(x))=r-\ell
.

As
\delta(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})=\mathcal{A}Y^{\operatorname{gen}}
, the vector
({\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{r}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}))^{t}\in(R^{\operatorname{gen}})^{r}
is a solution of
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}_{1}y
for some
\mathcal{A}_{1}\in K(x)^{r\times r}
. Since
{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})

(\ell+1\leq i\leq r)
can be expressed as a
K(x)
-linear combination of
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})
, it follows that the vector
({\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}))^{t}
is a solution of
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}y
for some
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in K(x)^{\ell\times\ell}
.
Set
v=({\mathbf{m}}_{1}(\mathcal{Y}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(\mathcal{Y}))^{t}\in\mathcal{R}^{\ell}
.

By Proposition
4.23
, there exists a positive integer
N
and an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
the following holds: If
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R^{c}
and
\widetilde{v^{c}}\in(R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}})^{\ell}
is the image of
v^{c}
in
R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
, then
\operatorname{LinRel}(\widetilde{v^{c}},k(x))
has a
k(x)
-basis of the form

a_{1}y_{1}+\ldots+a_{\ell}y_{\ell}
with
a_{i}\in k(x)
and
\deg(a_{i})\leq N
for
i=1,\ldots,\ell
.

Recall that the
wronskian

\operatorname{wr}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s})
of elements
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
of some differential ring
R
, is defined as the determinant of the wronskian matrix

\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}&\ldots&h_{s}\\
\delta(h_{1})&\ldots&\delta(h_{s})\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\delta^{s-1}(h_{1})&\ldots&\delta^{s-1}(h_{s})\end{pmatrix}.

The crucial property of the wronskian is that, if
R
is a field, then
h_{1},\ldots,h_{s}
are
R^{\delta}
-linearly independent if and only if
\operatorname{wr}(h_{1},\ldots,h_{s})
is nonzero (
[
vdPS03
, Lemma 1.12]
).

Because
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}}),\dots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})\in R^{\operatorname{gen}}
are
K(x)
-linearly independent, the elements

x^{j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})\ (0\leq j\leq\ell N,\ 1\leq i\leq\ell)
of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
are
K
-linearly independent. Thus their wronskian is nonzero.
Let
w\in\mathcal{R}
be the wronskian of
x^{j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(\mathcal{Y})\ (0\leq j\leq\ell N,\ 1\leq i\leq\ell)
.
By Lemma
4.19
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
w^{c}\in R^{c}
is nonzero for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.8
, there exists, for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
, a maximal
\delta
-ideal
\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
of
R^{c}
such that
w^{c}\notin\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
. Let
\widetilde{Y^{c}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}})
denote the image of
Y^{c}
in
R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
. As the image of
w
in
R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
is nonzero for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
, the family
x^{j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})\ (0\leq j\leq\ell N,\ 1\leq i\leq\ell)
is
k
-linearly independent.

We claim that
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(\widetilde{Y^{c}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})
are
k(x)
-linearly independent for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
. Suppose this is not the case. Then
\operatorname{LinRel}(\widetilde{v^{c}},k(x))
is nonzero for some
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
. By the choice of
N
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
above, there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\in k(x)
with
\deg(a_{i})\leq N
and
a_{1}{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})+\ldots+a_{\ell}{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})=0
. Clearing denominators, we find that the family
x^{j}{\mathbf{m}}_{i}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})\ (0\leq j\leq\ell N,\ 1\leq i\leq\ell)
is
k
-linearly dependent; a contradiction.

Thus
{\mathbf{m}}_{1}(\widetilde{Y^{c}}),\ldots,{\mathbf{m}}_{\ell}(\widetilde{Y^{c}})\in R^{c}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{m}_{c}}
are
k(x)
-linearly independent. This implies that

\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(\widetilde{Y^{c}},d,k(x))\leq r-\ell=\dim_{K(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y^{{\operatorname{gen}}},d,K(x)).

Since
\dim_{k(x)}\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},d,k(x))
does not depend on the choice of the maximal
\delta
-ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
of
R^{c}
, we obtain (
37
) with
\mathcal{U}_{1}
an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
.

As
p_{i}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})=0
, also
p_{i}^{c}(Y^{c})=(p_{i}(\mathcal{Y}))^{c}=0
for
i=1,\ldots,m
and
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
. So
p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c}\in\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},d,k(x))
for any
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

The
K(x)
-linear independence of
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x][X]
can be expressed through the non-vanishing of determinants. Thus, there exists a nonempty Zariski open
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c}\in k(x)[X]
are
k(x)
-linearly independent for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Combining this with (
37
), we see that, for
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
, equality holds in (
37
) and that
p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c}
is a
k(x)
-basis of
\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},d,k(x))
.
We can thus choose
\mathcal{U}
as any ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
∎

4.6
Picard-Vessiot rings under specialization

Recall that we assume Notation
4.12
for the remainder of Section
4
. In this section we prove our main specialization result, namely, that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for all
c
in an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Combining the criterion of Lemma
3.15
with Theorem
4.24
, we first show that
R^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot for all
c
in an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proposition 4.25
.

There exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot for all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4.16
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}
is an integral domain for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

Let
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}\in K[x][X]
be a
K(x)
-basis of
\operatorname{AlgRel}(Y^{\operatorname{gen}},{\mathbf{d}}(n),K(x))
. Extending
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.22
), we may assume that
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x][X]
.
As
p_{i}(\mathcal{Y})=0
, also
p_{i}^{c}(Y^{c})=(p_{i}(\mathcal{Y}))^{c}=0
for
i=1,\ldots,m
and all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

For
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
let
\mathfrak{m}_{c}
be a maximal
\delta
-ideal of
R^{c}
and let
\overline{Y^{c}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c})
denote the image of
Y^{c}
in
R^{c}/\mathfrak{m}_{c}
. By Theorem
4.24
, there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
p_{1}^{c},\dots,p_{m}^{c}
is a
k(x)
-basis of
\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},{\mathbf{d}}(n),k(x))
for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
.

We will show that
R^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Let
p\in k(x)[X]
with
\deg(p)\leq{\mathbf{d}}(n)
and
p(Y^{c})\in\mathfrak{m}_{c}
. According to Lemma
3.15
, it suffices to show that
p(Y^{c})=0
.

As
p(\overline{Y^{c}})=0
, i.e.,
p\in\operatorname{AlgRel}(\overline{Y^{c}},{\mathbf{d}}(n),k(x))
, we have
p=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}p_{i}^{c}
for appropriate
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\in k(x)
.
Therefore,
p(Y^{c})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}p_{i}^{c}(Y^{c})=0
.
Thus, we can choose
\mathcal{U}
as any ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
.
∎

We next make the step from proto-Picard-Vessiot to Picard-Vessiot. Roughly, this corresponds to the second main step of Hrushovski’s algorithm, which is more or less the Compoint-Singer algorithm from
[
CS99
, Section 2.5]
.
Besides Proposition
4.25
, key ingredients for the proof are the characterization of Picard-Vessiot rings among proto-Picard-Vessiot rings from Lemma
3.23
and the preservation of logarithmic independence under specialization from Theorem
4.10
.
The following theorem is our main specialization result.
We assume the setup of Notation
4.12
.

Theorem 4.26
.

There exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
. In particular, the set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot, is Zariski dense in
\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Proof.

Extending
\mathcal{B}
if necessary (Remark
4.22
), we can assume that the morphism
\mathcal{G}\to\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
defined by
\mathcal{Y}
is a closed embedding (Lemma
4.20
). We can thus consider
\mathcal{G}
as a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
. Note that the equations defining
\mathcal{G}
as a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,\mathcal{B}}
, also define
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
as a closed subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{n,K}
. Let
\{\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{m}\}
be a basis of
X((G^{\operatorname{gen}})^{\circ})
and let
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}\in K[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
be such that the image of
q_{i}
in
K[(G^{\operatorname{gen}})^{\circ}]
agrees with
\chi_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,m
.
Let
K(x)^{\prime}
denote the integral closure of
K(x)
in
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
and let
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K(x)^{\prime}}(R^{\operatorname{gen}},\overline{K(x)})
. Note that
K(x)^{\prime}
is canonically embedded into
\overline{K(x)}
by forming
\overline{K(x)}
inside the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
. As
\vartheta(R^{\operatorname{gen}})
is a finite field extension of
K(x)
, there exists an
\eta\in\overline{K(x)}
such that
\vartheta(R^{\operatorname{gen}})=K(x)[\eta]=K(x,\eta)
. In particular,
B=\vartheta(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{K(x)})
has coefficients in
K(x)[\eta]
.
Replacing
\eta
with a
K(x)
-
multiple of
\eta
if necessary, we can assume that the minimal polynomial
p\in K(x)[y]
of
\eta
over
K(x)
has coefficients in
K[x]
.

Extending
\mathcal{B}
and replacing
f
by a multiple of
f
if necessary (Remark
4.22
), we may assume that

•

the coefficients of
p,q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
are all in
\mathcal{B}
,

•

\delta(\eta)\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
, so that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
is a differential subring of
K(x,\eta)
and

•

B,B^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]^{n\times n}
.

By
[
Fen21
, Prop. 3.5]
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
the images
\chi_{1}^{c},\ldots,\chi_{m}^{c}
of
q_{1}^{c},\ldots,q_{m}^{c}
in
k[(G^{c})^{\circ}]
are a basis of
X((G^{c})^{\circ})
for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

As
p\in\mathcal{B}[x,y]
is irreducible in
K(x)[y]
and monic in
y
, it follows from Gauss’s lemma that
p
is irreducible in
K[x,y]
. By the Bertini-Noether theorem (
[
FJ08
, Prop. 9.4.3]
), there exits a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
p^{c}(x,y)
is irreducible in
k[x,y]
, and hence also irreducible in
k(x)[y]
, for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. So, for
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
, the ring
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=k(x)[y]/(p^{c})
is a field, in fact, a finite field extension of
k(x)
.

For
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
, let
k(x)^{\prime}
denote the integral closure of
k(x)
in
R^{c}
.
Our next goal is to construct, using
\vartheta
, elements
\vartheta^{c}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{k(x)^{\prime}}(R^{c},\overline{k(x)})
.
Recall (Lemma
4.13
) that we may regard
\mathcal{R}
as a
\delta
-subring of
R^{\operatorname{gen}}
. So the morphism
\vartheta\colon R^{\operatorname{gen}}\to K(x,\eta)
of
K(x)^{\prime}
-
algebras restricts to a morphism
\vartheta\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{\prime}
-algebras. Thus for
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
we can base change
\vartheta\colon\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
via
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
to a morphism
\theta^{c}\colon R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
of
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
-algebras. By Lemma
4.21
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{3}
of
\mathcal{U}_{2}
such that the map
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to k(x)^{\prime}\subseteq R^{c}
is an isomorphism. So composing
\theta^{c}
with a
\mathcal{B}[x]^{\prime}_{f}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=k(x)^{\prime}
embedding of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
into
\overline{k(x)}
, we obtain a morphism
\vartheta^{c}\colon R^{c}\to\overline{k(x)}
of
k(x)^{\prime}
-algebras. Note that
B^{c}=\vartheta^{c}(Y^{c})^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\overline{k(x)})
agrees with the image of
B
under
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\to\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to\overline{k(x)}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
.

For
\ell=1,\ldots,m
we set

f_{\ell}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial q_{\ell}}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})(B\mathcal{A}B^{-1}+\delta(B)B^{-1})_{ij}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\subseteq K(x,\eta)\subseteq\overline{K(x)}.

As
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is Picard-Vessiot, it follows from Lemma
3.23
and Remark
3.22
that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
are logarithmically independent over
K(x,\eta)
.

For an element
a
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
and
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
, let us write
a^{c}=a\otimes 1\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]}k(x)
for the image of
a
in
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]}k(x)\hookrightarrow\overline{k(x)}
.
For
\ell=1,\ldots,m
, we then have

f_{\ell}^{c}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial q_{\ell}^{c}}{\partial X_{ij}}(I_{n})(B^{c}A^{c}(B^{c})^{-1}+\delta(B^{c})(B^{c})^{-1})_{ij}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\hookrightarrow\overline{k(x)}.

By Theorem
4.10
, there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
contained in
\mathcal{U}_{3}
such that
f_{1}^{c},\dots,f_{m}^{c}
are logarithmically independent over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
. Then
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
is of the form
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}_{4}\cap\mathcal{V}
with
\mathcal{U}_{4}
ad-open and
\mathcal{V}
Jac-open.

By Proposition
4.25
, there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{5}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is proto-Picard-Vessiot for any
c\in\mathcal{U}_{5}
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{6}
be an ad-open subset of
\mathcal{U}_{4}\cap\mathcal{U}_{5}
. Then
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{6}\cap\mathcal{V}
is ad
\times
Jac-open.
Now Lemma
3.23
implies that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for any
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

The last statement of the theorem follows from Theorem
2.24
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
4.26
shows that if the algebraic group
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
over
K
is such that
(G^{\operatorname{gen}})^{t}=G^{\operatorname{gen}}
(e.g.,
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
is semisimple or unipotent), then the Jac-open subset is not needed, i.e., the set of good specializations contains an ad-open subset.
However, we can do much better than that:

Corollary 4.27
.

Assume that in the setup of Notation
4.12
the generic differential Galois group
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
is connected. Then there exists an ad-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

If
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
is connected, then the
\vartheta\in\operatorname{Hom}_{K(x)}(R^{\operatorname{gen}},\overline{K(x)})
in the proof of Theorem
4.26
can be chosen such that
B=\vartheta(Y^{\operatorname{gen}})^{-1}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(K(x))
by Remark
3.24
. We can therefore choose
\eta=1
and so
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\subseteq K(x)
. By Remark
4.11
, the Jac-open subset
\mathcal{V}
is then not needed so that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}=\mathcal{U}_{5}
is ad-open and therefore also
\mathcal{U}
is ad-open.
∎

While Jac-open subsets do not occur when the generic differential Galois is connected, they do indeed occur in the non-connected case. This is illustrated in the following example.

Example 4.28
.

Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be variables over
k
and set
\mathcal{B}=k[\alpha,\beta]
. In line with Notation
4.12
we also set
K=\overline{k(\alpha,\beta)}
.

Let
a=\frac{(x-\beta)^{2}}{x^{4}+x+\alpha}
,
f=(x^{4}+x+\alpha)(x-\beta)
and consider the differential equation

\delta^{2}(y)-\big(2a+\tfrac{\delta(a)}{2a}\big)\delta(y)+\big(a^{2}-a-\tfrac{\delta(a)}{2}\big)y=0

(38)

over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
. Note that the companion matrix
\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{2\times 2}
of (
38
) is of the form described in Examples
3.6
and
4.17
with
b=a
.

As
a
is not a square in
k(\alpha,\beta)(x)
, we see that
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[y]/(y^{2}-a)=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta]
is an integral domain and a differential ring.
As in the previous examples we set

\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\eta][y_{1},y_{2},y_{1}^{-1},y_{2}^{-1}]=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/(p_{1},p_{2})=\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[\mathcal{Y},\tfrac{1}{\det(\mathcal{Y})}],

where

p_{1}=X_{21}X_{22}-(a^{2}-a)X_{11}X_{12}\quad\quad p_{2}=X_{21}X_{12}+X_{22}X_{11}-2aX_{11}X_{12},

\delta(y_{1})=(a+\eta)y_{1}
,
\delta(y_{2})=(a+\eta)y_{2}
and

\delta(\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{A}\mathcal{Y}
.

We know from Example
3.6
that
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
is a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
where
\mathcal{G}
is the group scheme of
2\times 2
monomial matrices over
\mathcal{B}
.
Therefore,

R^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)=K(x,\eta)[y_{1},y_{2},y_{1}^{-1},y_{2}^{-1}]=K(x)[Y^{\operatorname{gen}},\tfrac{1}{Y^{\operatorname{gen}}}]

is a differential
G^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{G}_{K}
-torsor.
Example
3.25
shows that
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is Picard-Vessiot if and only if
f_{1}=a+\eta
and
f_{2}=a-\eta
are logarithmically independent over
K(x,\eta)
.
From Example
3.27
we know that
f_{1},f_{2}
are logarithmically independent over
K(x,\eta)
if the point
(0,1)
is not a torsion point on the elliptic curve (over
K
) defined by
v^{2}=u^{3}-4\alpha u+1
.

Combining Example 2.4 (i) of
[
Zan14a
]
and Proposition 12.1 of
[
Zan14b
]
we see that the latter is the case and so
R^{\operatorname{gen}}/K(x)
is Picard-Vessiot. (Note that in the notation of these references
\infty_{+}=(0,1)
and
\infty_{-}
is the neutral element.)

Set
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{A}^{2}_{k}
.
We would like to determine an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)=k^{2}
such that
R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
. To this end, we follow the same strategy as at the generic point.
Example
4.17
shows that
R^{c}
is an integral domain provided that
256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27\neq 0
. For these
c
’s, Example
3.25
shows that
R^{c}
is Picard-Vessiot if and only if
f_{1}^{c}=a^{c}+\eta^{c}
and
f_{2}^{c}=a^{c}-\eta^{c}
are logarithmically independent over
F^{c}=k(x,\eta^{c})=k(x)[y]/(y^{2}-a^{c})
. With
z^{c}=\frac{x-\beta^{c}}{\eta^{c}}
, we have

F^{c}=k(x,z^{c})
and
(z^{c})^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha^{c}
.

Set
\mathcal{U}=W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)\cap\mathcal{V}
, where
\Gamma
is the subgroup of
(\mathcal{B},+)
generated by
(\beta^{4}+\beta+\alpha)^{2}
and
256\alpha^{3}-27
and
\mathcal{V}
is the set of all
c=(\alpha^{c},\beta^{c})\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27\neq 0
and
(0:1:1)
is not a torsion point of the elliptic curve
{\mathcal{E}}^{c}
with affine equation:
u^{2}=v^{3}-4\alpha^{c}v+1
.
So
\mathcal{U}
is an ad
\times
Jac-open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
. We will show that
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Because
c\in W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
, we have

(\beta^{c})^{4}+\beta^{c}+\alpha^{c}\neq 0,\,\,256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27\neq 0,\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,\frac{((\beta^{c})^{4}+\beta^{c}+\alpha^{c})^{2}}{256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27}\notin\mathbb{Q}.

From Example
3.27
with
\alpha,\beta
being replaced by
\alpha^{c},\beta^{c}
respectively, we see that
Z_{1}((f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}),{\mathcal{P}})=\{(d,-d)\mid d\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where
{\mathcal{P}}=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{6}\}
are all poles of
f_{1}^{c}dx
and
f_{2}^{c}dx
.
In particular,
P_{1},P_{2}
are places of the function field
F^{c}=k(x,z^{c})
with
(z^{c})^{2}=x^{4}+x+\alpha^{c}
and
P_{1}
is the place corresponding to the neutral element
(0:1:0)\in E^{c}(k)
, while
P_{2}
corresponds to the point
(0:1:1)\in E^{c}(k)
. For each
(d,-d)\in Z_{1}((f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}),{\mathcal{P}})
,

\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}d\operatorname{res}_{P}(f_{i}^{c}dx)\right)P=2d(P_{2}-P_{1})

Furthermore, as
c\in\mathcal{V}
,
2d(P_{2}-P_{1})
is not a torsion point of
{\mathcal{E}}^{c}(k)
if
d\neq 0
. Thus
Z_{2}((f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}),{\mathcal{P}})
is trivial and therefore
f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}
are logarithmically independent (see Example
3.27
for details). So
R^{c}/k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

In the following, we also show that there are infinitely many
c=(\alpha^{c},\beta^{c})\in k^{2}=\mathcal{X}(k)
with
256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27\neq 0
such that
R^{c}
is not Picard-Vessiot, i.e.,
f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}
are logarithmically dependent.
By Example 2.4 (i) of
[
Zan14a
]
, there exist infinitely many
\alpha^{c}\in k
with
256(\alpha^{c})^{3}-27\neq 0
such that
(0,1)
is a torsion point on the elliptic curve
{\mathcal{E}}^{c}
. For every such
\alpha^{c}\in k
, let
m_{c}\geq 2
denote the order of
(0,1)
. Now
m_{c}(0,1)
is the neutral element of
{\mathcal{E}}^{c}
, if and only if the divisor
m_{c}(P_{2}-P_{1})
is principal. Thus, there exist an
h_{c}\in F^{c}
such that
(h_{c})=m_{c}(P_{2}-P_{1})
. Note that
\frac{dx}{z^{c}}
is a regular differential of
F^{c}
. The only poles of
\frac{dh^{c}}{h_{c}}=\frac{\delta(h_{c})}{h_{c}}dx
are
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. Moreover, these poles are simple with residues
-m_{c}
and
m_{c}
respectively. On the other hand,
\frac{m_{c}x}{z^{c}}dx=\frac{m_{c}x^{2}}{z^{c}}d(x^{-1})
and the
P_{i}
-adic expansion of
z^{c}
is
z^{c}=c_{i}(x^{-1})^{-1}+\ldots
for
i=1,2
with
c_{1}=-1
and
c_{2}=1
(Example
3.27
). It follows that also the differential
\frac{m_{c}x}{z^{c}}dx-\frac{\delta(h_{c})}{h_{c}}dx
is regular.
As
F^{c}/k
has genus one, the space of regular differentials is one dimensional. Therefore, there exists an
s\in k
such that
\frac{m_{c}x}{z^{c}}-\frac{\delta(h_{c})}{h_{c}}=\frac{s}{z^{c}}
. If we set
\beta^{c}=\frac{s}{m_{c}}\in k
, then

m_{c}(f_{1}^{c}-f_{2}^{c})=m_{c}\eta^{c}=\tfrac{m_{c}(x-\beta^{c})}{z^{c}}=\tfrac{\delta(h_{c})}{h_{c}},

so that indeed
f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}
are logarithmically dependent for
c=(\alpha^{c},\beta^{c})\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

As the above
c
’s are not really given explicitly, let us present at least one explicit
c\in W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
such that
R^{c}/k(x)
is not Picard-Vessiot. (In this sense the set
\mathcal{V}
is necessary.)

Set
c=(\alpha^{c},\beta^{c})=(-\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{4},-\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{8})
. Then

256\alpha^{c}-27=5,\quad((\beta^{c})^{4}+\beta^{c}+\alpha^{c})^{2}=\frac{37249(\sqrt{-3}+1)^{2}}{262144}.

So
c=(\alpha^{c},\beta^{c})\in W_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbb{G}_{a},\Gamma)
but
f_{1}^{c},f_{2}^{c}
are logarithmically dependent. In fact, one has that

2(f_{1}^{c}-f_{2}^{c})dx=4\frac{(x-\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{8})dx}{\sqrt{x^{4}+x-\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{4}}}=\frac{\delta(h)}{h},

where

h=p+q\sqrt{x^{4}+x-\frac{1+\sqrt{-3}}{4}}
with

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=(2x^{4}-x^{2}+x)\sqrt{3}+\sqrt{-1}(2x^{4}+4x^{3}+x^{2}+x+1),

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=(2x^{2}-1)\sqrt{3}+\sqrt{-1}(2x^{2}+4x+1).

5
Applications of the specialization theorem

In this section, we present some applications of our specialization theorem (Theorem
4.26
). The main application, which in fact motivated this entire paper, is the proof of Matzat’s conjecture. Besides Matzat’s conjecture, we also present the proofs for the results announced in the introduction and a very short proof of the solution of the inverse problem.

5.1
Exceptional parameter values

In this section we deduce the results announced in the introduction from Theorem
4.26
.

5.1.1
Exceptional parameter values for the algebraic relations among the solutions in a family of linear differential equations

Let
k\subseteq k^{\prime}
be an inclusion of algebraically closed fields of characteristic zero and let
R/k^{\prime}(x)
be a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
with
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
. Then
R
is of the form
R=k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathfrak{m}
for some maximal differential ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
, where
\delta(X)=AX
. Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
be a generating set of the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
. Fix a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of
k^{\prime}
and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
a monic polynomial such that
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}^{n\times n}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
. Set
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
and for
c_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
let
