\mathfrak{m}^{c_{0}}
denote the ideal of
k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
generated by
p_{1}^{c_{0}},\ldots,p_{m}^{c_{0}}
.

Corollary 5.1
.

There exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{c_{0}}
is a maximal differential ideal of
k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
for every
c_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
, where
k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
as a differential ring with respect to the derivation determined by
\delta(X)=A^{c_{0}}X
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.11
, there exists

•

a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and contained in the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
;

•

a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
f_{0}
divides
f
in
\mathcal{B}[x]
;

•

an affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
;

•

a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=Ay

such that

\mathcal{R}
is a flat
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-module,

\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple, where
K\subseteq k^{\prime}
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq R
.

Let
Y
denote the image of
X
in
R=k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathfrak{m}
and let
\mathcal{Y}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(\mathcal{R})
be the fundamental matrix corresponding to
Y
under the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq R
. Then

\mathfrak{m}=\left\{p\in k^{\prime}(x)[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]\ \big|\ p(Y)=0\right\}

and for

\mathcal{I}=\{p\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]\ \big|\ p(\mathcal{Y})=0\}

we have
\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)=\mathfrak{m}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\subseteq k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

Set
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
and let
\phi\colon\mathcal{X}(k)\to\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
be the map induced by the inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.

We claim that there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset

\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)

such that
\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}\subseteq k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
,
where the tensor product
\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is formed using
c\colon\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
. As
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}\in\mathcal{I}
, we have
\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
for all
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
.

Set

\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\{p\in k(\mathcal{B})(x)[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]\ \big|\ p(\mathcal{Y})=0\}.

As
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\otimes_{k(\mathcal{B})(x)}k^{\prime}(x)=\mathfrak{m}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\subseteq k^{\prime}(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}],

we must have
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\subseteq k(\mathcal{B})(x)[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]
.

Let
q_{1},\ldots,q_{d}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
be such that
\mathcal{I}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{d})
. As
q_{i}\in\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})
, we can find a nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}
and a monic
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
bf^{\prime}q_{i}\in(p_{1},\ldots,p_{m})\subseteq\mathcal{B}[x][X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
for
i=1,\ldots,d
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}(k)
where
b
does not vanish. Then
q_{i}^{c}\in(p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c})\subseteq k(x)[X,\frac{1}{\det(X)}]
for
i=1,\ldots,d
for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Therefore

\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{d})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(q_{1}^{c},\ldots,q_{d}^{c})\subseteq(p_{1}^{c},\ldots,p_{m}^{c})=\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}

and so
\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
as claimed. In particular, since
\mathcal{I}
is a differential ideal, also
\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}
is a differential ideal for all
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Theorem
4.26
applied to the differential torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
yields an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is
\delta
-simple for every
c\in\mathcal{V}
. For
c\in\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V}
we then have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathcal{I})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)

\displaystyle=k(x)[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathcal{I}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=

\displaystyle=k(x)[X,\tfrac{1}{\det(X)}]/\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}

is
\delta
-simple, i.e.,
\mathfrak{m}^{\phi(c)}
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal. By Lemma
2.7
, there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}_{0}\subseteq\phi(\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{V})
. Then
\mathfrak{m}^{c_{0}}
is a maximal
\delta
-ideal for all
c_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

If, in the context of Corollary
5.1
, the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) is connected, then the set
\mathcal{U}_{0}
can be chosen to be ad-open.

Proof.

If the differential Galois group
G
of
\delta(y)=Ay
is connected, also the differential Galois group
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
of
\mathcal{R}^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is connected because
G=(G^{\operatorname{gen}})_{k^{\prime}}
by Lemma
3.2
. Thus, by Corollary
4.27
, the ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
in the proof of Corollary
5.1
can be chosen to be ad-open. Then by Lemma
2.7
also
\mathcal{U}_{0}
can be chosen to be ad-open.
∎

5.1.2
Families of not solvable algebraic groups

We next work towards a proof of Corollary B1 from the introduction. A linear differential equation is solvable by Liouvillian functions if and only if the identity component of the differential Galois group is solvable (
[
vdPS03
, Theorem 1.43]
). For the proof of Corollary B1 we need to know that the contrapositive of this property spreads out from the generic fibre to an open subset of the parameter space.

Throughout Section
5.1.2
we make the following assumptions:

•

k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero;

•

\mathcal{B}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra that is an integral domain;

•

K=\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions
k(\mathcal{B})
of
\mathcal{B}
;

•

\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
;

•

\mathcal{G}
is an affine group scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{X}
is dominant, i.e., the dual map
\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]
is injective.

•

\xi
is the generic point of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}=\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(k(\xi))
is the generic fibre (an algebraic group over
k(\xi)=k(\mathcal{B})
) whereas
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\xi}}=\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\operatorname{Spec}(K)
is the geometric generic fibre (an algebraic group over
K
). Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is the fibre over
c\in\mathcal{X}
(an algebraic group over the residue field of
c
).

The main goal of this subsection is to prove the following:

Proposition 5.3
.

If
(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\xi}})^{\circ}
is not solvable, then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
is not solvable for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

The proof of Proposition
5.3
is given at the end of Section
5.1.2
. We will need some properties of the derived subgroup of an algebraic group. For more background see
[
Mil17
, Section 6 d]
or
[
Wat79
, Section 10.1]
.
Let
k^{\prime}
be a field of characteristic zero and let
G
be an algebraic group over
k^{\prime}
. The
derived group

\mathcal{D}(G)
of
G
can be defined as the smallest closed normal subgroup of
G
such that
G/\mathcal{D}(G)
is abelian. Alternatively, it can also be described as the closed subgroup of
G
generated by the commutator map
G\times G\to G,\ (g,h)\mapsto ghg^{-1}h^{-1}
(
[
Mil17
, Prop. 6.18]
). More generally, for every
n\geq 1
, consider the map

\phi_{n}\colon G^{2n}\to G,\ (g_{1},h_{1},\ldots,g_{n},h_{n})\mapsto g_{1}h_{1}g_{1}^{-1}h_{1}^{-1}\ldots g_{n}h_{n}g_{n}^{-1}h_{n}^{-1}

and its dual
\phi_{n}^{*}\colon k^{\prime}[G]\to\otimes^{2n}k^{\prime}[G]
.
So
\ker(\phi_{n}^{*})
is the defining ideal of the closure of the image of
\phi_{n}
.
One has
\ker(\phi_{1}^{*})\supseteq\ker(\phi_{2}^{*})\supseteq\ldots
and the defining ideal
\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{D}(G))\subseteq k^{\prime}[G]
of
\mathcal{D}(G)
is
\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{D}(G))=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\ker(\phi_{n}^{*})
. In fact,
\mathbb{I}(\mathcal{D}(G))=\ker(\phi_{n}^{*})
for some
n
(
[
Mil17
, Prop. 6.20]
).

The algebraic group
G
is
perfect
if
\mathcal{D}(G)=G
. Thus
G
is perfect if and only of
\phi_{n}^{*}
is injective for some
n\geq 1
. In other words,
G
is perfect if and only if
\phi_{n}
is dominant for some
n\geq 1
.

The higher derived groups
\mathcal{D}^{i}(G)
are defined recursively by
\mathcal{D}^{i}(G)=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{D}^{i-1}(G))
. Then
G\supseteq\mathcal{D}(G)\supseteq\mathcal{D}^{2}(G)\supseteq\ldots
is a descending sequence of closed subgroups (the derived series) that must eventually terminate, say at
\mathcal{D}^{n}(G)
. If
\mathcal{D}^{n}(G)=1
is the trivial group, then
G
is called
solvable
. Otherwise,
\mathcal{D}^{n}(G)
is a nontrivial perfect closed subgroup of
G
.

Lemma 5.4
.

If
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is perfect and nontrivial, then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is perfect and nontrivial for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

As above, for
n\geq 1
consider the scheme morphism

\phi_{n}\colon\mathcal{G}^{2n}\to\mathcal{G},\ (g_{1},h_{1},\ldots,g_{n},h_{n})\mapsto g_{1}h_{1}g_{1}^{-1}h_{1}^{-1}\ldots g_{n}h_{n}g_{n}^{-1}h_{n}^{-1}.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is perfect
(\phi_{n})_{\xi}\colon\mathcal{G}_{\xi}^{n}\to\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is dominant for some
n\geq 1
. By
[
Gro66
, Theorem 9.6.1 (ii)]
, the set of all
c\in\mathcal{X}
such that
(\phi_{n})_{c}\colon\mathcal{G}_{c}^{n}\to\mathcal{G}_{c}
is dominant is constructible. As it contains the generic point
\xi
, this set thus contains a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}
. So for every
c\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
, the algebraic group
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is perfect.

By generic freeness (
[
Sta24
,
Tag 051S
]
), there exists nonzero
b\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]_{b}
is a free
\mathcal{B}_{b}
-module, say of rank
\kappa
.
Then,
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k(\mathfrak{p})
is a
k(\mathfrak{p})
-vector space of dimension
\kappa
for every prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)
. The assumption that
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is nontrivial means that
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k(\mathcal{B})
is not reduced to
k(\mathcal{B})
. So
\kappa>1
and
\mathcal{B}[\mathcal{G}]\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}k(\mathfrak{p})
is not reduced to
k(\mathfrak{p})
for any
\mathfrak{p}\in D(b)
, i.e.,
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is nontrivial for
c\in D(b)
. Thus
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\cap D(b)
has the required property.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

If
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is not solvable, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is not solvable for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
is not solvable, there exists an
n\geq 1
such that
H=\mathcal{D}^{n}(\mathcal{G}_{\xi})
is nontrivial and perfect. The closed subgroup
H
of
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
spreads out to a closed subgroup
\mathcal{H}
over a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}
. In detail, there exists a nonempty affine Zariski open
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{X}
and a closed subgroup scheme
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=H
. This follows, for example, from
[
Gro66
, Theorems 8.8.2 and 8.10.5]
.

Lemma
5.4
applied to
\mathcal{H}
, yields a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{c}
is nontrivial and perfect for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
. Thus, for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
, the algebraic group
\mathcal{G}_{c}
contains the closed nontrivial perfect subgroup
\mathcal{H}_{c}
and can therefore not be solvable.
∎

We are now prepared to prove the main result of this subsection.

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Because solvability (
[
Mil17
, Cor. 6.31]
) and the formation of the identity component (
[
Mil17
, Prop. 1.34]
) is compatible with base change, we see that
(\mathcal{G}_{\xi})^{\circ}
is not solvable. As in the proof of Lemma
5.5
, the closed subgroup
H=(\mathcal{G}_{\xi})^{\circ}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\xi}
spreads out to a closed subgroup scheme over a nonempty Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{X}
, i.e., there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}
and a closed subgroup
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}=\mathcal{G}\times_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\xi}=(\mathcal{G}_{\xi})^{\circ}
. Because
\mathcal{H}_{c}
is (geometrically) connected, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{c}
is (geometrically) connected for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
(
[
Gro66
, Theorem 9.7.7]
). Moreover, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{3}
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
\dim(\mathcal{G}_{c})=\dim(\mathcal{G}_{\xi})
and
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{c})=\dim(H_{\xi})(=\dim(\mathcal{G}_{\xi}))
for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{3}
(
[
Sta24
,
Tag 05F7
]
). In summary, this shows that we can find a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{U}_{1}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{c}=(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
for all
{c}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
.

Applying Lemma
5.5
to the affine group scheme
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{U}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{H}\times_{\mathcal{U}_{1}}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
over
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
yields a nonempty Zariski open susbset
\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{H}_{c}=(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
is not solvable for all
{c}\in\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}
. In particular,
(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
is not solvable for every
c\in\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{X}(k)
.
∎

5.1.3
Exceptional parameter values for solving in a family of linear differential equations

Throughout Subsection
5.1.3
we make the following assumptions:

•

k\subseteq k^{\prime}
is an inclusion of algebraically closed fields of characteristic zero;

•

\delta(y)=Ay
is a linear differential equation over
k^{\prime}(x)
, i.e.,
A\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
;

•

\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq k^{\prime}
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra and
f_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
is a monic polynomial such that
A\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}^{n\times n}
;

•

\mathcal{X}_{0}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B}_{0})
;

•

for
c_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
we denote with
A^{c_{0}}\in k(x)^{n\times n}
the matrix obtained from
A
by applying
c_{0}\colon\mathcal{B}_{0}\to k
to the coefficients of the entries of
A
.

We first treat the case of solving in Liouvillian extensions. Recall that a subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
is called ad
\times
Jac-closed (or ad-closed) if its complement is ad
\times
Jac-open (or ad-open).

Corollary 5.6
.

Assume that the differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) does not have a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions, then the set of all
c_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that the differential equation
\delta(y)=A^{c_{0}}y
(over
k(x)
) has a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions, is contained in an ad
\times
Jac-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
.

Proof.

Let
R/k^{\prime}(x)
be a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
. By Lemma
3.11
, there exists

•

a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
containing
\mathcal{B}_{0}
and contained in the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
;

•

a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
f_{0}
divides
f
in
\mathcal{B}[x]
;

•

an affine group scheme
\mathcal{G}
of finite type over
\mathcal{B}
;

•

a differential
\mathcal{G}
-torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=Ay
such that

\mathcal{R}
is a flat
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-module,
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple, where
K
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq R
.

Note that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
K(x)
with differential Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{K}
(Lemma
4.14
). So it follows from Lemma
3.2
that
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq R
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
k^{\prime}(x)
with differential Galois group
\mathcal{G}_{k^{\prime}}
. Because
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) does not have a basis consisting of Liouvillian functions, we know that
(\mathcal{G}_{k^{\prime}})^{\circ}
is not solvable (
[
vdPS03
, Theorem 1.43]
). As the formation of the identity component (
[
Mil17
, Prop. 1.34]
) and solvability (
[
Mil17
, Cor. 6.31]
) is compatible with base change, this implies that
(\mathcal{G}_{K})^{\circ}
is not solvable.

Set
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
. By Proposition
5.3
, there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}_{1}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
is not solvable for every
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}
.

Theorem
4.26
applied to the differential torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=Ay
yields an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{2}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is Picard-Vessiot for all
c\in\mathcal{U}_{2}
. By Lemma
2.7
, there exists an ad
\times
Jac-open subset
\mathcal{U}_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that
\mathcal{U}_{0}
is contained in the image
\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
under
\mathcal{X}(k)\to\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
. For any
c_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
, there thus exists a
c\in\mathcal{U}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}_{2}
mapping to
c_{0}
. We then have
A^{c_{0}}=A^{c}
and
R^{c}/k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
with differential Galois group
G^{c}=\mathcal{G}_{c}
. As
(\mathcal{G}_{c})^{\circ}
is not solvable, we see that
\delta(y)=A^{c_{0}}y
does not have a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions for every
c_{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Thus the set of all
c_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that
\delta(y)=A^{c_{0}}y
has a basis of solutions consisting of Liouvillian functions, is contained in the complement of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

If, in the context of Corollary
5.6
, the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) is connected, we can make do with an ad-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
.

Proof.

As in Remark
5.2
, we see that the differential Galois group
G^{\operatorname{gen}}
of
\mathcal{R}^{\operatorname{gen}}
is connected and so
\mathcal{U}_{2}
can be chosen to be ad-open by Corollary
4.27
.
∎

We next treat the case of solving in algebraic extensions.

Corollary 5.8
.

Assume that the differential equation
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
k^{\prime}(x)
) does not have a basis of solutions consisting of algebraic functions, then the set of all
c_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
such that the differential equation
\delta(y)=A^{c_{0}}y
(over
k(x)
) has a basis consisting of algebraic functions, is contained in an ad
\times
Jac-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
.

Proof.

This could be deduced directly from Corollary
5.1
. Alternatively, noting that a differential equation has a basis of algebraic solutions if and only if its differential Galois group is finite, this can be proved exactly like Corollary
5.6
but replacing Proposition
5.3
with the following statement: With the notation of Subsection
5.1.2
, if
\mathcal{G}_{\overline{\xi}}
is finite, then there exists a nonempty Zariski open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
\mathcal{G}_{c}
is finite for every
c\in\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Remark 5.9
.

Again, if in the context of Corollary
5.8
, the differential Galois group of
\delta(y)=Ay
(over
K(x)
) is connected, then we can make do with an ad-closed subset of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(k)
.

5.2
A short solution of the inverse problem

The
solution of the inverse problem
states that every linear algebraic group over
k
is a differential Galois group over
k(x)
. Using the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence, this was proved for
k=\mathbb{C}
already in 1979 (
[
TT79
]
) but it took more than 25 years and contributions of many authors to finally solve the inverse problem for an arbitrary algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero (
[
Har05
]
).

In this short section, we explain how Theorem
4.26
can be used to deduce the solution of the inverse problem over
k
, from the solution of the inverse problem over
\mathbb{C}
. Of course, our solution is only short if one accepts Theorem
4.26
and the solution of the inverse problem over
\mathbb{C}
, as given.

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Then every linear algebraic group over
k
is a differential Galois group over
k(x)
.

Proof.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
. Then
G
descends to a finitely generated field, i.e., there exists a subfield
k_{0}
of
k
finitely generated over
\mathbb{Q}
and a linear algebraic group
G_{0}
over
k_{0}
such that
(G_{0})_{k}=G
.
Passing to the algebraic closure
k_{1}\subseteq k
of
k_{0}
, we obtain a countable algebraically closed field
k_{1}
and a linear algebraic group
G_{1}
over
k_{1}
such that
(G_{1})_{k}=G
.

By Lemma
3.2
, it suffices to show that
G_{1}
is a differential Galois group over
k_{1}(x)
. Since
k_{1}
is countable, there exists an embedding of
k_{1}
into
\mathbb{C}
that we now fix.

By
[
TT79
]
, there exists a Picard-Vessiot ring
R/\mathbb{C}(x)
for some equation
\delta(y)=Ay
, (
A\in\mathbb{C}(x)^{n\times n}
) with differential Galois group
(G_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}
. The Picard-Vessiot ring
R/\mathbb{C}(x)
can now be spread out by Lemma
3.11
, i.e., there exists

•

a finitely generated
k_{1}
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathbb{C}
,

•

a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
with
A\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{n\times n}

•

and a differential
(G_{1})_{\mathcal{B}}
-torsor
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=Ay
such that
\mathcal{R}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
and
\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple, where
K
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
.

Set
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
.
By the specialization theorem (Theorem
4.26
), there exists a
c\in\mathcal{X}(k_{1})
such that
R^{c}=\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k_{1}(x)
is Picard-Vessiot. So
R^{c}/k_{1}(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=A^{c}y
with differential Galois group
((G_{1})_{\mathcal{B}})^{c}=((G_{1})_{\mathcal{B}})_{k_{1}}=G_{1}
. In particular,
G_{1}
is a differential Galois group over
k_{1}(x)
as desired. ∎

5.3
Proof of Matzat’s conjecture

In this section we show that every differential embedding problem of finite type over
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
has a solution (Theorem
5.14
) and deduce Matzat’s conjecture from this.
The idea for the proof is to first solve the differential embedding problem over a sufficiently large field of constants
k^{\prime}\supseteq k
and then to go back down to
k
using Theorem
4.26
.

Throughout Section
5.3
we assume that
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Let us recall some background on differential embedding problems. Let
F
be a differential field with
F^{\delta}=k
. If
R\subseteq S
is an inclusion of Picard-Vessiot rings over
F
, then there is a restriction map
G(S/F)\twoheadrightarrow G(R/F)
on the differential Galois groups which is a quotient map, i.e., the dual map is injective.

Definition 5.11
.

A
differential embedding problem
of finite type over
F
is a pair
(\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H,\ R)
, consisting of a quotient map
\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H
of linear algebraic groups over
k
and a Picard-Vessiot ring
R/F
with differential Galois group
H
. A
solution
is a Picard-Vessiot ring
S/F
containing
R
together with an isomorphism
G\simeq G(S/F)
such that

\textstyle{G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{G(S/F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H=G(R/F)}

(39)

commutes. In other words, the restriction map
G(S/F)\twoheadrightarrow G(R/F)
realizes the given morphism
\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H
.

The attribute “of finite type” in Definition
5.11
refers to the assumption that
G
(and hence
H
) is a group scheme of finite type (over
k
). In general, to deal with Matzat’s conjecture, it is important to allow more general differential embedding problems (not necessarily of finite type). See
[
BHHW21b
]
. However, for our purpose differential embedding problems of finite type are sufficient. On the ring side, the commutativity of (
39
)
is expressed by the commutativity of

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{k}k[G]}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{k}k[H]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where
R\otimes_{k}k[H]\to S\otimes_{k}k[G],\ r\otimes a\mapsto r\otimes\phi^{*}(a)
with
\phi^{*}\colon k[H]\to k[G]
the dual of
\phi
.

As detailed in the following remark, a differential embedding problem over
k(x)
can always be extended to a differential embedding problem over
k^{\prime}(x)
.

Remark 5.12
.

Let
(\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H,\ R)
be a differential embedding problem of finite type over
k(x)
and let
k^{\prime}
be an algebraically closed field extension of
k
. Then
R^{\prime}=R\otimes_{k(x)}{k^{\prime}(x)}
is a Picard-Vessiot ring over
k^{\prime}(x)
with differential Galois group
H_{k^{\prime}}
(Lemma
3.2
).
Therefore,
(\phi_{k^{\prime}}\colon G_{k^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow H_{k^{\prime}},R^{\prime})
is a differential embedding problem of finite type over
k^{\prime}(x)
.

To apply Theorem
4.26
we first need to “spread out” a solution of the extended differential embedding problem into a family with nice properties. This is achieved in the following lemma. Recall that actions of affine group schemes on differential algebras were defined in Section
3.2
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
(\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H,\ R)
be a differential embedding problem of finite type over
k(x)
. Let
k^{\prime}
be an algebraically closed field extension of
k
and let
S^{\prime}/k^{\prime}(x)
be a solution of the induced differential embedding problem
(\phi_{k^{\prime}}\colon G_{k^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow H_{k^{\prime}},\ R^{\prime})
over
k^{\prime}(x)
, where
R^{\prime}=R\otimes_{k(x)}k^{\prime}(x)
. Then there exist

(i)

a monic polynomial
h\in k[x]
, and a
k[x]_{h}
-
\delta
-subalgebra
R_{0}
of
R
with an action of
H
on
R_{0}/k[x]_{h}

such that
R_{0}\otimes_{k[x]_{h}}k(x)=R
(equivalently,
U^{-1}R_{0}=R
, where
U=k[x]_{h}\smallsetminus\{0\}
) and the induced action of
H
on
U^{-1}R_{0}/U^{-1}k[x]_{h}
is the natural action of
H
on
R/k(x)
,

(ii)

a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
and a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
h\in k[x]\subseteq\mathcal{B}[x]
divides
f
in
\mathcal{B}[x]
,

(iii)

a matrix
\mathcal{A}\in\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}^{n\times n}
and a differential
G_{\mathcal{B}}
-torsor
\mathcal{S}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=\mathcal{A}y
such that
\mathcal{S}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}

and
S^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple, where
K=\overline{k(\mathcal{B})}\subseteq k^{\prime}
is the algebraic closure of the field of fractions of
\mathcal{B}
,

(iv)

a morphism
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebras that is equivariant with respect to the morphism
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}\colon G_{\mathcal{B}}\to H_{\mathcal{B}}
, where
\mathcal{R}=R_{0}\otimes_{k[x]_{h}}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}=(R_{0}\otimes_{k}\mathcal{B})_{f}
and
\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
has an
H_{\mathcal{B}}
-action induced from
(i) above. (The equivariance means that for every
\mathcal{B}
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
and
g\in G_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathcal{T})
the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi(g)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes.)

Proof.

We begin with (i). Choose
A\in k(x)^{m\times m}
such that
R/k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=Ay
. Let
h\in k[x]
be a monic polynomial such that
A\in k[x]_{h}^{m\times m}
and let
Y\in\operatorname{GL}_{m}(R)
satisfy
\delta(Y)=AY
. Then
R_{0}=k[x]_{h}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]
is a
\delta
-subring of
R
because
\delta(Y)=AY\in R_{0}^{m\times m}
.
For a
k
-algebra
T
an element
g\in H(T)
acts on
R\otimes_{k}T
by
Y\otimes 1\mapsto Y\otimes[g]
for some matrix
[g]\in\operatorname{GL}_{m}(T)
. Thus
g\colon R\otimes_{k}T\to R\otimes_{k}T
restricts to a
k[x]_{h}\otimes_{k}T
-
\delta
-
automorphism of
R_{0}\otimes_{k}T
.
This defines an action of
H
on
R_{0}/k[x]_{h}
that clearly induces the given action on
R/k(x)
.

To address the remaining points, fix
A^{\prime}\in k^{\prime}(x)^{n\times n}
and
Y^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}(S^{\prime})
such that
S^{\prime}/k^{\prime}(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring for
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
and
\delta(Y^{\prime})=A^{\prime}Y^{\prime}
. As

R\subseteq R^{\prime}\subseteq S^{\prime}=k^{\prime}(x)[Y^{\prime},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{\prime})}]
, there exists a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}_{0}
of
k^{\prime}
and a monic polynomial
f^{\prime}_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
such that all entries of
Y
and
\frac{1}{\det(Y)}
lie in
\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f^{\prime}_{0}}[Y^{\prime},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{\prime})}]\subseteq S^{\prime}
. Set
f_{0}=hf_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]
. Then
R_{0}=k[x]_{h}[Y,\frac{1}{\det(Y)}]\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{0}[x]_{f_{0}}[Y^{\prime},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{\prime})}]\subseteq S^{\prime}
.

Now Lemma
3.11
yields a finitely generated
k
-subalgebra
\mathcal{B}
of
k^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
, a monic polynomial
f\in\mathcal{B}[x]
such that
f_{0}
divides
f
in
\mathcal{B}[x]
, a differential
G_{\mathcal{B}}
-torsor
\mathcal{S}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
for
\delta(y)=A^{\prime}y
with fundamental solution matrix
\mathcal{Y}
such that
\mathcal{S}
is flat over
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
,
S^{\operatorname{gen}}=\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}K(x)
is
\delta
-simple and
\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k^{\prime}(x)\simeq S^{\prime}
via
\mathcal{Y}\mapsto Y^{\prime}
. So,
\mathcal{S}
can be identified with
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}[Y^{\prime},\frac{1}{\det(Y^{\prime})}]\subseteq S^{\prime}
and
R_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
.

By now we have established (i), (ii) and (iii) of the lemma.
Let us move on to (iv).
Setting
\mathcal{R}=R_{0}\otimes_{k[x]_{h}}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
, the inclusion
R_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
yields a morphism
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebras. Since
S^{\prime}/k^{\prime}(x)
is a solution of the differential embedding problem
(\phi_{k^{\prime}}\colon G_{k^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow H_{k^{\prime}},\ R^{\prime})
the diagram

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G_{k^{\prime}}]}
\textstyle{R^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R^{\prime}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[H_{k^{\prime}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes. Therefore also

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[G_{\mathcal{B}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{k}k[G]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k[G]=S^{\prime}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[G_{k^{\prime}}]}
\textstyle{R_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R_{0}\otimes_{k}k[H]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R^{\prime}\otimes_{k}k[H]=R^{\prime}\otimes_{k^{\prime}}k^{\prime}[H_{k^{\prime}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[G_{\mathcal{B}}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[H_{\mathcal{B}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes. This establishes (iv).
∎

The following theorem was proved in
[
BHHP21
]
under the assumption that
k
has infinite transcendence degree over
\mathbb{Q}
. Thanks to Theorem
4.26
we are able to remove this unnecessary assumption.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Then every differential embedding problem of finite type over
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
is solvable.

Proof.

Let
(\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H,\ R)
be a differential embedding problem of finite typer over
k(x)
. Let
k^{\prime}
be an algebraically closed field extension of
k
such that
k^{\prime}
has infinite transcendence degree over
\mathbb{Q}
. Consider the induced differential embedding problem
(\phi_{k^{\prime}}\colon G_{k^{\prime}}\twoheadrightarrow H_{k^{\prime}},\ R^{\prime})
, where
R^{\prime}=R\otimes_{k(x)}k^{\prime}(x)
. According to
[
BHHP21
, Cor. 4.6]
it has a solution
S^{\prime}/k^{\prime}(x)
. Let
h,R_{0},\mathcal{B},f,\mathcal{A},\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{S}
be as in Lemma
5.13
and set
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{B})
.
Then
\mathcal{S}/\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
satisfies all the assumptions of Notation
4.12
and it follows from Theorem
4.26
that there exists a
c\in\mathcal{X}(k)
such that
S^{c}=\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring. In fact,
S^{c}/k(x)
is a Picard-Vessiot ring with differential Galois group
G^{c}=(G_{\mathcal{B}})_{c}=G
.

The morphism
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}
-
\delta
-algebras yields a morphism

\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)\to\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)
of
k(x)
-
\delta
-algebras. But

\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=(R_{0}\otimes_{k[x]_{h}}\mathcal{B}[x]_{f})\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=R_{0}\otimes_{k[x]_{h}}k(x)=R

and
\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}}k(x)=S^{c}
.
We thus obtain an embedding
R\to S^{c}
of Picard-Vessiot rings over
k(x)
. By (iv) of Lemma
5.13
the diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[G_{\mathcal{B}}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{B}[H_{\mathcal{B}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes. Therefore also its base change

\textstyle{S^{c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{c}\otimes_{k}k[G]}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\otimes_{k}k[H]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

via
\mathcal{B}[x]_{f}\to k(x)
commutes. Thus
S^{c}/k(x)
is a solution of
(\phi\colon G\twoheadrightarrow H,\ R)
.
∎

We are now prepared to prove Matzat’s conjecture in full generality.

Theorem 5.15
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero and let
F
be a one-variable function field over
k
, equipped with a non-trivial
k
-derivation. Then the absolute differential Galois group of
F
is the free proalgebraic group on a set of cardinality
|F|
.

Proof.

The case when
k
has infinite transcendence degree (over
\mathbb{Q}
) is proved in
[
Wib23
, Theorem 2.9]
. So we can assume that
k
has finite transcendence degree. In particular,
k
is countable.
In
[
Wib23
, Prop. 2.8]
it is shown that if Matzat’s conjecture is true for
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
, then it is true for
F
. So we have reduced to proving the theorem for
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
with
k
countable. According to
[
BHHW21b
, Cor. 3.9]
, the absolute differential Galois group of a countable differential field
F
is free on a countably infinite set if and only if every differential embedding problem over
F
of finite type is solvable. It therefore suffices to show that every differential embedding problem of finite type over
(k(x),\frac{d}{dx})
is solvable. This is exactly Theorem
5.14
.
∎
